certain end must be kept in view)--which we know he impressed upon
himself. His essays are evidence that he was successful in working the
maxim into his blood.

Still the mind is only the third part of a man, and mental poise
can exist alongside of emotional debility and incompetence in daily
affairs. Not so with Arnold, however, who impresses one almost as
much by his emotional poise and his practical level-headedness as by
the stability of his mind. He was a poet and is secure of a place
in the history of English poetry, and this is proof positive of his
strength of feeling, for time permits no man weak in feeling to stay
long in the halls of Poesy. More than that, his sentiments, beginning
with the personal and lyric, tended to pass into reflective emotion:
that is, they broadened, became generalized, impersonal, universal,
linked with reflections on the common destiny of mankind. This
represents a maturing of his emotional life. Now just as provincialism
and aimlessness destroy one’s mental balance, so immaturity and the
inability to feel beyond one’s intimate concerns upset one’s emotional
balance. But Arnold ripened, leaving the petty and childish behind
him. He learned emotionally to stand on his own, to practice a sturdy
independence. His sentiments were adult. Mark too his efforts to
round out his emotional life. By disposition addicted to melancholy
meditativeness, he sought--many times with success--to cultivate
gaiety, to support a deeply serious view with as much charm and
lightness as were fitting.

Now regard him in his practical aspect. We have seen that Arnold
did not evade what the middle class man regards as his duties and
responsibilities. He was a man among men, asking for no petting from
society, meeting honestly by his own efforts the obligations of his
lot. He never underestimated as do many modern artists the importance
of conduct. Conduct, he said, was three-quarters of life, and as such
man’s most serious concern. “As for living, our servants can do that
for us,” said Villiers de l’Isle Adam, but Arnold never echoed such
a sentiment. On the contrary, he not only faced daily affairs with
dignity and courage, observing all varieties of men, bearing his own
part, but he stressed the practical side of his writing. He wished
for the best ideas to _prevail_ and he took infinite pains to make
them persuasive and lucid and graceful so that they would irresistibly
invade men’s minds and prevail. In this he is in extreme contrast with
the “ivory tower” attitude which isolates art and thought from events.
The practical man wishes to make a mark in the world’s business, and
this decidedly Arnold aimed to do. The aim saved him from æsthetic
trifling and metaphysical toying. It filled out the third side of his
being.

Now perhaps we may see what is involved in the ideal of
proportionateness: a threefold activity to clear the mind, to ripen and
extend the feelings, to develop a practical attitude. The man of poise
cannot neglect any one of these on peril of remaining lop-sided. Bias
is his enemy, and against it he needs the full operation of a strong
centralizing spirit in himself. Such a spirit, pervading his conduct,
pervading his poetry, pervading his mind, Arnold possessed, and it is
conveyed in his remark that “we are not here to have facilities found
us for doing the work we like, but to make them.” It is the spirit of
a man who faces the natural difficulties of life with a strong will to
overcome them.

 * * * * *

We are trying to arrive at a life-like picture of the man of poise as
American Humanism conceives him. I have chosen Arnold as a working
model of the quality, and an imitable one as well. Naturally American
Humanists vary in their estimates of Arnold, and one or two--notably
G. R. Elliott--are rather harsh in their strictures, but the majority
appear to find in him the prime humanistic virtues, and so it seems
fair to make the use I have of his example. It is obvious, I hope, that
I do not imagine I am passing all of Arnold in review, and furthermore
I confess that were I not looking for a man of poise in the recent
Humanist tradition, I should never have summed Arnold up under that
phrase. Poise to my mind is not the capital thing about him. Beyond
the degree of most men, Arnold was a truth-seeker; and poise was to
him auxiliary to the pursuit of truth. Auxiliary but necessary. For
without moderating one’s powerful prejudices, without attaining a fine
balance and tact, without tempering one virtue by its opposite virtue,
Arnold was positive that one could not see things as they are. Things,
reality, are many-sided, and without the cultivation of many-sidedness
in ourselves, how can we hope to approach them fairly? These words
from the preface to _Essays in Criticism: First Series_ set forth the
right employment of poise in the great endeavor to see life steadily
and whole. “To try and approach truth on one side after another, not
to strive or cry, nor to persist in pressing forward, on any one side,
with violence and self-will,--it is only thus, it seems to me, that
mortals may hope to gain any vision of the mysterious Goddess, whom we
shall never see except in outline, but only thus even in outline. He
who will do nothing but fight impetuously towards her on his own, one,
favorite, particular line, is inevitably destined to run his head into
the folds of the black robe in which she is wrapped.”

A singular fact ought to be patent from all that has been said: the
man of poise is not a type, but an ideal toward which all types of men
may strive. Nature makes types, but she does not make human beings of
poise. She makes will-less Hamlets who, faced by a necessity to act,
simply speculate and speculate, until events force them at last to
act; she makes Byrons who, on the other hand, try to force events:
they act impetuously, but they reflect like children, as Goethe said
of Byron. But Nature does not make the balanced, well-proportioned
individual. Arnold worked on himself: he worked to perfect himself
beyond the maternal care of Nature.

Thus we see that the man of poise, the Humanist ideal, is in reality
a spiritual athlete. His balance and proportionateness are not gifts,
such as a vein of poetic emotion or a capacity for intellectual
analysis may be, but they are deliberately sought and maintained. It
requires constant effort, constant activity, for him to be what he
is. Modernity accepts no such ideal as the New Humanist upholds. It
wants greater Hamlets, greater Byrons, greater Calibans: it wants the
accentuation of types and the growth of specialism: in short, it wishes
to go with the grain of Nature. But the New Humanist proclaims that
the truly man-like part is to work against the grain of Nature. “It is
this backward motion toward the source,” says Robert Frost, the poet,
“against the stream, that most we see ourselves in.”

 IV

 THE NEW BATTLE OF THE BOOKS

  _“Our ‘intellectual atmosphere,’ however, is now rapidly changing,
  is becoming charged with new interests. More and more persons,
  oppressed with the stale skepticism of the post-war period, are
  beginning to grow skeptical of that skepticism, and are looking
  for a new set of controlling ideas capable of restoring value to
  human existence. Certain forces are making for order and for new
  objectives.... One of these forces is known as ‘humanism,’ which
  is rapidly becoming a word to conjure with.”_--Norman Foerster in
  _Humanism and America_.


Before I can treat the grounds of the New Humanist doctrine, I must
turn historian and present, briefly at least, correct information about
the origin and course of the movement which acknowledges Irving Babbitt
and Paul Elmer More as masters. I am forced to this by the amount of
misinformation and crude misrepresentation already widely disseminated
concerning More and Babbitt and their colleagues. How this spreading
of erroneous information, which now stands in our view, has come about
is easily explained. American Humanism has had a quiet development for
thirty-five years, making its way chiefly in academic channels and in
scholarly books. Not until very recently, and then principally because
of the spirited campaign waged in behalf of Babbitt and More by the
_Bookman_, has this body of thought made any strong infiltration into
popular literary journalism.

Now the roving eye of the literary journalist-at-large seldom glances
at anything growing up in the universities, and it missed altogether
the New Humanist movement until it emerged in force in the winter
of 1929-1930. Then, to his surprise, the literary journalist found
himself expected by his public to say something about Humanism. He was
totally unprepared to do so, but experience has given him a cynical
attitude toward his public. He is the lazy tutor who does not study
one lesson in advance of his pupil, but he has learned how to divert
the mind of his charge, should he be asked an inconvenient question.
He then makes a great play on non-essentials, he bluffs it out until
the hour is over, knowing well that his pupil will soon forget both the
question and the mystifying answer. To send the public off on false
scents was the shift, not always adopted dishonestly, of the unprepared
literary journalist when Humanism became no longer an avoidable
topic. For literary journalists, it seems, are never pen-tied because
of ignorance: no difficulty was experienced in promptly delivering
opinions having no bearing on the facts, lacking logic, missing the
real issues,--wrong opinions, in short. Myths about the New Humanism
were quickly generated, giving delight to connoisseurs in the absurd,
but decidedly misleading to gentleman-readers wishing simply for plain
light on the new trend. However, to dispel these myths, which obstruct
thought, nothing more is needed than some bald information.

It is dramatic contrast, in view of the international attention his
teachings have won, to begin with a picture of Irving Babbitt[1] in his
classroom, working obscurely, as befits an underminer of an age’s basic
assumptions, at his task of moulding the mind of a generation. Among
the many professional writers and brilliant teachers who have come out
from his courses--one may mention as a catholic selection T. S. Eliot,
Van Wyck Brooks, Walter Lippmann, Norman Foerster, Gilbert Seldes,
Newton Arvin--was the late Stuart P. Sherman, who has left us a roughly
drawn description of his early master’s wealth of mind and strength for
destruction and construction.

“I knew Professor Babbitt as a graduate teacher in the History of
Literary Criticism and in his Rousseau and His Influence. At that time
he had very small classes--meeting around a table. He came in with a
bag bursting full of books, and took out a handful of notes which he
arranged around him. Began to sway in his chair, then leaped out upon
one of them, and poured a barrage of criticism upon some doctrine or
some line of poetry,--‘to cast o’er erring words and deeds a heavenly
show’--Buddha, Aristotle, Plato, Horace, Dante, Montaigne, Pascal,
Milton, etc.--etc.

“He deluged you with wisdom of the world; his thoughts were unpacked
and poured out so fast you couldn’t keep up with them. You didn’t
know what he was talking about, but you felt that he was extremely in
earnest, that it was tremendously important, that some time it would
count; that he was uttering dogmatically things that cut into your
beliefs, disposed derisively of what you adored, driving you into a
reconstruction of your entire intellectual system. He was at you day
after day like a battering ram, knocking down your illusions. He was
building up a system of ideas.

“You never felt for a moment that he was a pedagogue, teaching pupils.
You felt that he was a Coleridge, a Carlyle, a Buddha, pouring out the
full-stuffed cornucopia of the world upon your head. You were no longer
in the elementary class. You were with a man who was seeking through
literature for illustrations of his philosophy of life. You were
dealing with questions on the answer to which the welfare of nations
and civilization depended. He himself seemed to know the right answer
and was building a thoroughfare of ideas from the Greeks to our own day.

“You went out of the room laden down with general ideas that he had
made seem tremendously important.... He related for you a multitude of
separate and apparently disconnected tendencies to the great central
currents of thought. You carried away also a sense of the need for
immense reading. He had given you theses about literature, about life,
which you would spend a lifetime in verifying.”

A battering ram knocking down illusions ... myself, I like to think
of Irving Babbitt as a dogmatist of the Dr. Johnson type, burly in
his judgments, masculine in his sentiments, downright in manner, an
unyielding hater of sophistry, a stalwart upholder of commonsense.
What is remarkable, in view of the extent of his system, is that he
should have laid out at the age of thirty the ground-plan for his whole
impressive structure of ideas. “The characteristic thought of Irving
Babbitt,” as Norman Foerster observes, “was already adumbrated in 1895
in an address at the University of Wisconsin on ‘The Rational Study of
the Classics,’ published two years later in the _Atlantic Monthly_ and
in 1908 included in his book on _Literature and the American College_.”
Since then he has steadily developed his original thesis: confident
of his foundations, he has put up a heavy, many-fronted, turreted
fortress for the modern mind to retire into and survey across a moat of
commonsense the giddy wheelings of the “modernists,” those, that is,
who make a cult of change and take change to be “progress.”

But what were Babbitt’s sources? His mind is powerfully assimilative
and very muscular in its operations. He has applied it to world
literature, east and west, ancient and modern, and naturally therefore
his sources are varied. He is more Aristotelian than Platonic, however,
and he takes more from Confucius than from Buddha. He feels himself
at home with the rugged conservatism of Dr. Johnson and Edmund Burke,
and he stands with Matthew Arnold as an urger of more Hellenism in
Anglo-Saxon culture.

His more immediate stimulators in American literature seem to be
Emerson and Charles Eliot Norton. Emerson as a volatile literary force
arose from many elements, one of them assuredly humanistic, and from
this fortifying element in Emerson Babbitt has drawn much aid to his
own spirit. It was the element which Emerson expressed in the didactic
poem quoted on the flyleaf of Babbitt’s first book, _Literature and the
American College_, and which no American Humanist since has apparently
been able to refrain from quoting some place or other in his writings.
I also succumb to the poem’s epigrammatic appeal and concision.

  There are two laws discrete
  Not reconciled,--
  Law for man, and law for thing;
  The last builds town and fleet,
  But it runs wild,
  And doth the man unking.

In respect to that man of truly mellow culture, Charles Eliot Norton,
friend of Carlyle, co-editor with Lowell of the _North American
Review_, professor of fine arts at Harvard, and celebrated Dantean
scholar and translator, to him Babbitt acknowledged his indebtedness in
the preface to _Literature and the American College_, written in 1907
shortly before Norton’s death.[2] “Those who,” Babbitt wrote, “during
the past generation have felt the need of a more humane scholarship are
indebted to him, many for direct aid and encouragement, and all for an
example.”

By 1910 Babbitt had found strong fellowship in the direction his mind
was working, notably the fellowship of Paul Elmer More. More had
been born in St. Louis, Missouri, a year earlier than Babbitt: he
graduated from Washington University in 1887 and took his master’s
degree from Harvard in 1893, the same date as Babbitt. In the college
year of 1894-1895 the two men were thrown together again, More as an
instructor in Sanskrit at Harvard and Babbitt as an instructor in the
French department. In the intervening year Babbitt had taught in the
Romance Languages department of Williams College and there he had met
a young instructor, Frank Jewett Mather, Jr., who was destined to
figure importantly in the growth of American Humanism. But not for
long were Babbitt and More to work together as university colleagues,
for from Harvard More went to Bryn Mawr College, where he stayed two
years teaching Sanskrit and classical literature. Then in 1901, after
his retreat to Shelburne, New Hampshire, for meditation, he joined
the staff of the _Independent_ as literary editor. Mather too gave
up academic work at this time and entered journalism as an editorial
writer for the New York _Evening Post_: he was for various terms in
the future to be its art critic as well as assistant editor of the
_Nation_ and American editor for the _Burlington Magazine_. More’s
future included the literary editorship of the New York _Evening Post_
from 1903 to 1909, and the editorship of the _Nation_, then the supreme
organ of American scholarship, from 1909 to 1914. In the meantime he
had begun to write his well known series of literary studies, the
_Shelburne Essays_.

More and Babbitt complement each other extraordinarily. If Babbitt
inclines to Aristotle, More inclines to Plato. If Babbitt writes on
literary trends, using particular authors only in so far as they
illustrate trends, More studies the individual genius, somewhat in
the sinuous fashion of Sainte-Beuve, and then points out the trend.
Babbitt, speaking roughly, is a critic of culture, More a critic of
authors. Their area of agreement is very wide: they unite against
Romanticism, agree substantially on Humanism, and diverge only when it
comes to the kind of interest and the warmth of interest they feel in
Religion. In short, More is more religious by temperament than Babbitt.

 * * * * *

After his retirement from the _Nation_ in 1914, More devoted himself
to an enterprise of scholarship and criticism which well deserves
an adjective to be used with care, an adjective which has several
times been applied to it by men who do not write lightly, I mean
the adjective, monumental; and for the wonderful completion of this
enterprise he has occasionally been mentioned as a candidate for the
Nobel Prize in literature. The undertaking was _The Greek Tradition_
in five volumes, an impressive and inspiring account of the relation
of Platonic and Hellenistic thought to early Christian thought; it is
a work, incidentally, that has not yet even begun to be digested by
American criticism.

There are those who believe More to be the best literary critic in
the history of American literature. I think that is so: he appears to
me an American counterpart to Cardinal Newman in his grasp at once of
the ways of men, the subtle arguments of materialism, and reasoned
belief in the spiritual life. But be that as it may, he is today the
_doyen_ of American critics, and as such a cause for national pride.
Agree or not with their teachings, we must all feel pride that America
presents to Europe as, respectively, her _doyen_ of critics and as
her master-spokesman for classical literary ideas two men of the
resolution, deep scholarship, elevated thought, ripeness of judgment
and wide sweep of purpose which characterize Paul Elmer More and Irving
Babbitt.

 * * * * *

Possibly the future of American Humanism will be dramatic. Already
it has enough adherents of a polemical turn of mind, sharp
controversialists like Robert Shafer and Seward Collins, to make things
hot for their natural opponents, the Liberals, and the Liberals are
certainly not going to relinquish their leadership of present-day
American literature and thought before resorting to every device of
literary dispute. Perhaps in the next decade Humanist and Liberal will
pamphleteer against each other with all the zest and pungency and
fierceness of the verbal warfare waged between Tories and Whigs in
eighteenth century England. But up till now the New Humanists have been
mainly perfecting their viewpoint, gathering strength, and preparing
for action.

I distinguish three generations of New Humanists and divide, very
arbitrarily, the rise of American Humanism thus far into three brief
periods, the first from 1900 to 1917, when Stuart P. Sherman’s _On
Contemporary Literature_ appeared, the second from 1917 to the
publication of Babbitt’s _Democracy and Leadership_ in 1924, and the
third from 1924 to the present.

From 1900 to 1917 the first generation of New Humanists was teaching
and writing out of its maturity. Babbitt was publishing his views on
educational reform, on modern confusion in the arts, and on literary
criticism. More was brilliantly editing the _Nation_ and composing
volume after volume of carefully thought out and gracefully written
studies of literature. Frank Jewett Mather, Jr., was making a name as
an exacting but sympathetic critic of the fine arts, a calling of which
in America he is now the dean. In passing, it may be noted that Mather
was a pioneer in the work of advancing the books of Herman Melville
to a front place in our literature. At the University of Nebraska,
Prosser Hall Frye and Sherlock Bronson Gass were teaching, thinking and
writing. Perhaps most prominent of all was the late W. C. Brownell, of
whom more than mere mention is required.

Brownell, More and Babbitt--the names often formed a triad, and yet
Brownell never held explicitly the general ideas worked out by More
and Babbitt. The bond was sympathetic, but intellectually not clearly
established. I once ventured to call Brownell a Victorian standpatter.
Perhaps this was too harsh. Objection was taken by a critic who
nevertheless declared that Brownell’s “senses never felt a concrete
situation freshly, unmediated by the anticipation of habit. His mind,
acute in distinguishing things, rarely attained any ideas and never
a general philosophy. And his feeling for life seldom drove him to
remove the blinkers of convention.” It will be seen that these defects
lead to standpattism, however, and Brownell resisted change and stood
in general for the Mid-Victorian temper and standards. His mind and
tastes were typically nineteenth century: he did not have the freedom
of the centuries so arduously won by Babbitt and More. But he was
loosely united to them in the low opinion he entertained of many forms
of modernism and in his insistence on critical standards.

Knowledge of the work these men--Brownell, Gass, Frye, Mather, More and
Babbitt--were performing in the early years of this century relieves
a little the oppressive feeling of dearth we have in regarding that
time in America. In _Their Day in Court_, an out-of-print book by a
forgotten impressionistic critic, Percival Pollard, we may get an
excellent picture of this brief period of literary dearth. Pollard
fought against the dry rot of the professors, the genteelness of
editors like Hamilton Wright Mabie, the commercialism of most that
passed for criticism, and the overwhelming mediocrity of the period’s
books. Rather sadly he said in 1908: “It does not seem to me as if
there were, on our side of the Atlantic, any longer such a thing as the
art of letters; it is merely a trade.” In that same year a gifted young
poet, Ezra Pound, fled our country, and later in his poem, _L’Homme
Moyen Sensuel_, expressed his disgust for the quietism of the times
more violently than even Pollard did.

  Still I’d respect you if you could bury
  Mabie, and Lyman Abbott and George Woodberry,
  For minds so wholly founded upon quotations
  Are not the best of pulse for infant nations.

So it was up to about 1912. There were certain giants looming over
the scene--Howells and Henry James and Mark Twain--but they had no
great animating effect upon it. James seemed too remote, Howells too
selective and genteel in his realism, and Mark Twain too given to
compromises. According to Pollard, the one American of literary genius
then alive was almost completely neglected. He meant Ambrose Bierce.

Pollard and Pound were right. The period in spite of James, Howells
and Mark Twain and, if you will, Bierce, was low in creative vitality,
and the bulk of its criticism was feeble,--Huneker flashing the cheap
jewelry of his style, Mabie composing his “White Lists” of books:
both extremists can without a second’s reluctance be dispensed with.
But looking back, now that the transient criticism of 1900-1912 has
been swept away, we see that there were more independent critics
at work than Pollard recognized and that they were doing extremely
hard thinking amidst the plague of mediocre books. They continued to
elaborate their thought as American literature entered upon a new and
more fertile phase between 1912 and 1915.

Some of the multiplying signs of the coming of a new order were the
founding of _Poetry_ magazine in Chicago, the establishment of the _New
Republic_, the beginnings of the _Little Review_ and _Others_, the
blooming of Greenwich Village, and the generative center for fresh art
impulses set up at “291,” the art gallery of Alfred Stieglitz. Soon a
new group of authors began winning laurels: Masters, Dreiser, Mencken,
Brooks, Bourne, were on the rise. The ferment worked faster ... little
theatres ... the _Masses_ and the _Liberator_ ... Coady’s _Soil_
magazine ... the _Seven Arts_ ... the _Nation_ under Villard ... the
_Dial_ as a libertarian fortnightly. American participation in the war
only made the new ventures bubble more furiously: they flourished, when
not strangled by official censorship, on dissent. Finally, in 1919 came
Waldo Frank’s _Our America_, the distillation of this time of ferment
from 1912.

Frank in this book took no cognisance, as his confrère Van Wyck Brooks
did, of the critical activities of the first generation of the New
Humanists. Nor did he pause to examine the work of Stuart P. Sherman,
the forerunner of the second generation of American Humanists. But
Sherman in 1917 had brought about the first spectacular duel between
the New Humanists and the naturalists represented by H. L. Mencken.
Sherman had been trained at Harvard under Babbitt: as a contributor
to the _Nation_, he had been groomed by Paul Elmer More. In 1917 he
published a book, _On Contemporary Literature_, in which he played the
part of the younger spokesman for classicism.

It is the best of Sherman’s books. Nine-tenths of his work is mediocre,
but in _On Contemporary Literature_ he surpassed himself. His insights
into such authors as Synge, George Moore, Anatole France, H. G. Wells,
Theodore Dreiser were sharp, deep and excruciating to those who admired
them. Of George Moore, he wrote: “Purely intellectual initiative he
had none: but he has been swiftly responsive to every new influence
in art and literature. All his life he has lurked in the purlieus of
schools and insinuated himself into movements, solicitating, like
the barren Calpurnia, the fructifying touch of some fleet clear eyed
runner ... Mr. Moore does not think: he muses.” Of Anatole France, he
observed: “He loves above all else the luxury of philosophic despair
... The skepticism of M. France is largely a literary pose. It is his
justification for unlimited intellectual self-indulgence.” And here
is an excellent working distinction between realism and naturalism in
fiction: “Both are representations of the life of man in contemporary
or nearly contemporary society, and both are presumably composed of
materials within the experience and observation of the author. But
the realistic novel is a representation based upon a theory of human
conduct. If the theory of human conduct is adequate, the representation
constitutes an addition to literature and to social history. A
naturalistic novel is a representation based upon a theory of animal
behavior. Since a theory of animal behavior can never be an adequate
basis for a representation of the life of man in contemporary society,
such a representation is an artistic blunder.”

Little wonder such thrusts as these produced an outcry, and there is
now no doubt that Sherman was vilified on the strength of this acute
book. We may look forward to the sober correction of the partisan
denunciations he called forth. In the meantime let us hear a Humanist
version of Sherman’s duel with Mencken, as an offset to the much better
known story given out by the sympathizers with Mencken. I quote from
Seward Collins writing in the _Bookman_:

“Until after the war the main force of his (Sherman’s) work lay in
the brilliant application of the point of view he had learned from
Irving Babbitt and Paul E. More, carrying into the enemy’s camp
their warfare against naturalism and deliquescent romanticism. With
the publication of _On Contemporary Literature_ at the end of 1917 he
became the recognized spokesman for tradition and authority against the
‘liberating’ and ‘innovating’ efforts of the latest generation. The
effectiveness of his campaign may be judged from the shrieks, hoots and
curses which greeted his book in the radical magazines and in some of
the newspapers, notably in Chicago, where the new men occupied most of
the reviewing media. The Chicago outburst, characterized by unanimous,
simultaneous and extreme virulence, makes very amusing reading in
retrospect. Several records in this kind of criticism must have been
hung up during the onslaught, which went on for weeks.

“Participants in the Chicago attack have explained that factors aside
from the book itself entered into the reception of _On Contemporary
Literature_. The cause most frequently given is the review of Mencken’s
_A Book of Prejudices_ which Sherman published in the _Nation_ of
November 29, 1917--just a month before his own book appeared--under the
title _Beautifying American Literature_. The review might well explain
any amount of rage among Mencken’s friends and disciples: it was one of
the most completely devastating slatings of a book and of a man ever
penned....

“This encounter was really an historic occasion in American letters
and thought. On the one side, for a number of years Irving Babbitt and
Paul E. More had brought supreme qualities of intellect, scholarship,
and literary skill to the perennial task of restating the case for
tradition in literature, morals, politics and philosophy. The result
was a series of powerful books providing almost a new basis for the
traditionalist cause, but one related at every point to the earlier
American traditionalists, to the main stream of European culture, and
to the great classical traditions of both the West and the East. In
Stuart Sherman these men had found a young disciple and champion whose
gifts, while not qualifying him to advance their work along all of the
extremely broad front on which they were engaged, yet enabled him to
make immediate and effective application of their views in their own
country and among the writers of the day.

“On the other side, the disruptive, anti-authoritarian,
anti-traditional tendencies, which America had for the most part
resisted even when in the ’nineties they took England by storm, after
their successful tour of the continent, had from the beginning of the
century been gathering strength. As the century got into its teens, the
new forces found a vigorous, infectious spokesman in H. L. Mencken,
who had spent his youth soaking up all the prevalent forms of the
heretical doctrines--Huxleyan agnosticism and science-worship, Pater’s
æstheticism with trimmings by Wilde, the hedonism and routine mockery
of Anatole France, the inverted sentimentality of Nietzsche, Ibsen, and
the other shrunken giants of fifty years ago, and so on. To all this
Mencken had added a Bohemian individualism from various sources (given
aristocratic airs by talk of Federalism, the civilized minorities,
etc.), a smart up-to-dateness, and a talent for the violent buffoonery
of American provincial journalism. To many this rich mixture proved
irresistible.

“These two antagonistic tendencies developed side by side for years
without clashing: the new men were restricted to shady magazines,
fleeting-tendency journals, and infrequent books which remained outside
channels of discussion. But in Mencken had arrived a personality
who could not be overlooked. And in Sherman the traditionalists had
a representative so sensitive to the forceful figures of his day
that he must have his say on them. The two men, who had been born a
few months apart, had entered upon literary criticism within a few
weeks of each other in 1908, and now at the end of 1917 published
almost simultaneously their first significant books. The result was
the episode which we have recounted. The old had met the new in open
conflict, and the fight was on.”

I take this episode which Collins has described so vividly to mark the
end of the first period of American Humanism. This, as stated before,
is quite arbitrary, but it is a temporary convenience. For in this
first brush Sherman and his friends suffered defeat. Not defeat on
intellectual grounds, but defeat when it came to making their ideas
and standards prevail. _On Contemporary Literature_ was given a black
name as a puritan castigation of modern writers which even today
keeps it out of the hands of young people. Sherman himself underwent
a metamorphosis. He coarsened his style and vulgarized his thought
and within a few years was on the side of the very Liberals he had
once scorned. From the Liberal point of view, Sherman had advanced,
escaping from a rigid system of ideas, to a more genial play of his
sympathies and a greater openness of mind. From the Humanist viewpoint,
he disintegrated. Losing him, the New Humanists lost their one champion
who at this time was willing to employ his energies cutting off the
latest heads sprouted by the hydra of naturalism. The Naturalists, the
Liberals, the Romanticists, were to have things their own way for the
next few years.

1920 was their year of jubilation, for in this year their spirit of
revolt, hitherto nourished in the intelligentsia and among the artists,
suddenly became popular. It was spread abroad by the success of such
realistic novels as Sinclair Lewis’s _Main Street_, Floyd Dell’s _Moon
Calf_, and Zona Gale’s _Miss Lulu Bett_. Eugene O’Neill conquered
Broadway at last with _Beyond the Horizon_, and in the same season
_Emperor Jones_ was produced in Greenwich Village. The _Dial_ became
an advanced monthly of the arts, the _Freeman_ launched itself on a
four years’ voyage of philosophic anarchism, Art Young conducted his
short-lived _Good Morning_, contributing his laughter to dissolve the
capitalist régime. Such books as _The American Credo_ by Mencken and
Nathan, _Poor White_ by Sherwood Anderson, _The Brass Check_ by Upton
Sinclair, _The Ordeal of Mark Twain_ by Van Wyck Brooks, and _The Dark
Mother_ by Waldo Frank made the year seem splendid with revolt, with
energy, with novelty. The Red ’Twenties had dawned. In this tumult the
New Humanists were lost from sight, and mouse-valiant book reviewers in
droves echoed the words of J. E. Spingarn without bothering to read
the men they were condemning. “These men,” said Spingarn of Babbitt
and More in the Freeman Pamphlet entitled _On American Books_, “have
the learning and culture of the best academic critics of France; but
what avails their petulant erudition if it serves no other purpose than
to whine over the irrevocable past and to sneer at the aspirations of
modern men?” Spingarn was floating with the new current and it seemed
to carry all the old barriers before it.

Practically every one in the new movement agreed that the objects
of attack were Puritanism and industrialism. In fiction, realism
and naturalism came in as the fashion. Psycho-analysis attained its
greatest vogue. Lyricism, emotionalism, were valued chiefly, and
intellectualism suffered a decline in esteem. The star of the new
America was heralded rising out of the Middle West. An American
renascence has begun, the prophets declared, and now we shall create
an indigenous culture. Expansion was in the air. Who would pay heed in
all this excitement to Babbitt and More who stressed the opposite mood,
concentration?

They, however, continued to swim against the swirling current
of Romanticism. More was at work on his magnum opus, _The Greek
Tradition_, and in 1918, the year after Sherman’s _On Contemporary
Literature_ had been blackened, Babbitt published his remarkable study
of the use and misuse of the imagination, _Rousseau and Romanticism_.
They were putting the last touches on their system of ideas, and
had the satisfaction while so engaged of beholding a small second
generation of New Humanists begin to come forward: Stanley P. Chase
at Bowdoin College, G. R. Elliott at Amherst, Robert Shafer at the
University of Cincinnati, and Norman Foerster at the University of
North Carolina. In 1924 appeared Babbitt’s _Democracy and Leadership_,
a study of modern political thought which conducted the author at its
conclusion back in a great circle to his starting point in his first
book, the need for collegiate reform in our study of the humanities. We
may say, for convenience’ sake, that _Democracy and Leadership_ closed
the second period of American Humanism. The New Humanism’s edifice of
ideas was now virtually complete.

In the next period, from 1924 to the present, the humanistic party
gathered adherents and momentum as a distinct counter-movement to the
still triumphant naturalistic movement. T. S. Eliot, who has made no
secret of his partial indebtedness to Babbitt, was instrumental in
interesting various young critics in the ideas of More and Babbitt.
Two brief notes in _The Sacred Wood_ had the effect, even early in
the Red ’Twenties, of raising a doubt about the correctness of the
current adverse judgments on Babbitt and More delivered by men like
Spingarn, Mencken and Brooks. Later articles by Eliot and the opening
of the pages of his review, _The Criterion_, to discussions of the
New Humanism did much to make Humanism a chief topic of distinguished
conversation. The _Forum_ also undertook in a series of articles pro
and con to advance Humanism to the forefront of the intellectual world.
A third generation of New Humanists--prominent among them being Alan
Reynolds Thompson and Harry Hayden Clark--was formed: the _Hound and
Horn_, an organ of _les jeunes_ edited by a former student of Babbitt,
for a time flirted with the ideas of the counter-movement. Finally, in
1929 under the slashing leadership of Seward Collins the _Bookman_ took
up arms for the cause of More and Babbitt.

Meantime, the main naturalistic movement in American letters was
slackening. In increasing numbers people were growing weary of
Menckenism. Two popular books in 1929 showed the hour was striking
for fresh ideas: one, Krutch’s _The Modern Temper_, was a confession
of impotence and gloom over the loss of the romantic hopes that had
been so dazzling in 1920, the other, Lippmann’s _A Preface to Morals_,
recognized the need for a new discipline in living and made effort
towards a formulation of one. Two prominent magazines of artistic
revolt suspended and each gave a curiously significant reason. The
_Dial_ said that “although a magazine can get along somehow without
readers it cannot exist without contributors.” And Margaret Anderson
said in the farewell number of the _Little Review_: “As this number
will show, even the artist doesn’t know what he is talking about. And I
can no longer go on publishing a magazine in which no one really knows
what he is talking about.” Verily, times were changing! We come to
1930, and hand-to-hand fighting at last between the New Humanists and
the Liberals or Naturalists or Romantics, as you choose to call them.

The occasion was the publication of the symposium, _Humanism and
America_, edited by Norman Foerster with the assistance of P.
H. Houston, G. R. Elliott and Robert Shafer. It provoked a new
“battle of the books” but thus far no Swift has arisen to lash the
gross stupidities committed by the majority of the modernists. The
controversy in general was a repetition on a much wider scale of the
unscrupulous Chicago onslaught on Sherman’s book in 1917. Issues
were obscured, prejudice appealed to, meanings distorted: literary
careerists rushed in, trying to outshout each other, and the low
estate of criticism in America became painfully clear. I need only
cite the testimony of an observer not on the Humanist side who was
finally driven to write: “I confess to impatience with the row about
Humanism, because in the course of it, in the past half year or so, I
have seen the powerful principles laid down by Irving Babbitt, being
confused and cheapened.” The words are by Gilbert Seldes. Finally, an
opposition symposium, _The Critique of Humanism_, was hastily organized
and hurriedly despatched to the book shops three months after _Humanism
and America_ saw the light on the counters. With what seem to me the
cogent objections to the standpoint of Babbitt and More advanced in
_The Critique of Humanism_, I shall deal by and by.

But shall we let the unsavoury episode just narrated pass without
recording the full harshness of judgment it deserves? I conceive it
the strict duty of a historian and lover of letters to say that the
cause of Romanticism is not so poor that it needs to be defended by
poor reasons and mob-appeals and that there is in fact a high standard
of argument already set in this controversy by the writings of the
English critics, Hugh l’Anson Fausset and J. Middleton Murry, _against_
the classicists’ position. One’s first desire is to see, regardless
of one’s party sympathies, the steady advancement, the heightening of
literary excellence, of all schools--let us have everything at its
highest point--and it is just this desire for _quality_ of controversy
that was ungratified by the outpourings of the American modernists.
Really, they should have done better! I can only say that if we imagine
that criticism has a deity, as love was once fabled to have, then we
shall have to invent two forms of it, even as the Greeks conceived of
Aphrodite Uranus and Aphrodite Pandemos. We shall have to say that
there is presiding over the labors of critics Polite Criticism and
Vulgar Criticism, and that it was Vulgar Criticism which ruled most of
the pens attempting to deface the New Humanism in the spring of 1930.
No fierce Tory with the genius of Swift arose, it is true, to wither
the present-day worshippers of Vulgar Criticism, but we can turn back
to Swift himself for the necessary scathing words.

“Meanwhile, Momus, fearing the worst, and calling to mind an ancient
prophecy, which bore no very good face to his children the Moderns
bent his flight to the region of a malignant deity, called criticism.
She dwelt on the top of a snowy mountain in Nova Zembla; there Momus
found her extended in her den, upon the spoils of numberless volumes,
half devoured. At her right hand sat Ignorance, her father and husband,
blind with age; at her left, Pride, her mother, dressing her up in
the scraps of paper herself had torn. There was Opinion, her sister,
light of foot, hoodwinked, and headstrong, yet giddy, and perpetually
turning. About her played her children, Noise and Impudence, Dulness
and Vanity, Positiveness, Pedantry, and Ill-manners. The goddess
herself had claws like a cat; her head, and ears, and voice, resembled
those of an ass; her teeth fallen out before, her eyes turned inward,
as if she looked only upon herself; her diet was the overflowing of her
own gall; her spleen was so large, as to stand prominent, like a dug
of the first rate; nor wanted excrescencies in form of teats, at which
a crew of ugly monsters were greedily sucking; and, what is wonderful
to conceive, the bulk of spleen increased faster than the sucking
could diminish it. ‘Goddess,’ said Momus, ‘can you sit idly here while
our devout worshippers, the Moderns, are this minute entering into a
cruel battle, and perhaps now lying under the swords of their enemies?
Who, then, hereafter will ever sacrifice, or build altars, to our
divinities?’”

 * * * * *

Unlike the quarrels that were the occasion for Swift’s writing _The
Battle of the Books_, the new “battle of the books” caused by the
advent of American Humanism involves issues that far transcend the
ordinary interests of scholars and professional literary men. So far as
the present controversy is literary, it is pretty much the case of the
warm dispute in Æsop’s Fable between the Bee and the Spider as to which
was the better artist. This fable Swift rewrote in _The Battle of the
Books_ and then made Æsop draw out its application in words that cover
almost equally well the respective arguments of the Modernists and the
New Humanist today, for it will be seen that the modernistic spider
makes a cult of originality and tends toward science-worship while the
humanistic bee objects to the kind and impermanency of the modernist’s
materials and extols wide ranging and “sweetness and light.”

“The disputants,” Swift has Æsop say, “have admirably managed the
dispute between them, have taken in the full strength of all that is to
be said on both sides, and exhausted the substance of every argument
_pro_ and _con_. It is but to adjust the reasonings of both to the
present quarrel, then to compare and apply the labors and fruits of
each, as the bee has learnedly deduced them, and we shall find the
conclusion fall plain and close upon the Moderns and us. For, pray,
gentlemen, was ever anything so modern as the spider in his air, his
turns, and his paradoxes? He argues in behalf of you his brethren and
himself, with many boastings of his native stock and great genius;
that he spins and spits wholly from himself, and scorns to own any
obligation or assistance from without. Then he displays to you his
great skill in architecture, and improvement in the mathematics. To all
this the bee, as an advocate, retained by us the Ancients, thinks fit
to answer--that, if one may judge of the great genius or inventions
of the Moderns by what they have produced, you will hardly have
countenance to bear you out, in boasting of either. Erect your schemes
with as much method and skill as you please; yet if the materials be
nothing but dirt, spun out of your own entrails (the guts of modern
brains) the edifice will conclude at last in a cobweb, the duration
of which, like that of other spiders’ webs, may be imputed to their
being forgotten, or neglected, or hid in a corner. For anything else of
genuine that the Moderns may pretend to, I cannot recollect; unless it
be a large vein of wrangling and satire, much of a nature and substance
with the spider’s poison; which, however they pretend to spit wholly
out of themselves, is improved by the same arts, by feeding upon the
insects and vermin of the age. As for us the Ancients, we are content,
with the bee, to pretend to nothing of our own beyond our wings and
our voice, that is to say, our flights and our language. For the
rest, whatever we have got, has been by infinite labor and search,
and ranging through every corner of nature; the difference is, that,
instead of dirt and poison, we have rather chosen to fill our hives
with honey and wax, thus furnishing mankind with the two noblest of
things, which are sweetness and light.”

More quickly than anything else, and that is why I have invoked it, the
potent breath of Swift ought to lift the battle-smoke that now obscures
the literary quarrel between the New Humanist and the ultra-modernist
and permit us to see the essentials of the doctrine of More and
Babbitt. What are they? What importance and meaning and utility have
they for the lay reader not afraid to look _everywhere_ for guidance in
facing the alternatives that life is so ingenious in forcing constantly
upon man? I propose now to consider the very center of humanist
teaching, the duality of human consciousness so uncompromisingly
affirmed by the two leaders.

 V

 THE CLASSICAL REVIVAL ABROAD

  _“People have fallen into a foolish habit of speaking of orthodoxy
  as something heavy, humdrum, and safe. There never was anything so
  perilous or so exciting as orthodoxy. It was sanity: and to be sane
  is more dramatic than to be mad. It was the equilibrium of a man
  behind madly rushing horses, seeming to stoop this way and to sway
  that, yet in every attitude having the grace of statuary and the
  accuracy of arithmetic.... It is easy to be a madman: it is easy
  to be a heretic. It is always easy to let the age have its head;
  the difficult thing is to keep one’s own. It is always easy to be
  a modernist; as it is easy to be a snob.”_--G. K. Chesterton in
  _Orthodoxy_.


However, before fulfilling the promise made at the close of the
preceding chapter, it seems well to take a cursory view of the
classical revival in England and France. Recent experience has shown
that American critics with only two or three exceptions cannot be
trusted to situate a comprehensive movement like the New Humanism in
its proper climate of sentiment, opinion and idea. On the contrary,
many of them regarded or affected to regard the New Humanist group as
a quaint local phenomenon sprung up miraculously without atmosphere
and totally unrelated to similar groups anywhere else. This delusion
embraced by the majority of the opponents of American Humanism
badly distorted their sense of perspective: to the broad challenge
of the followers of More and Babbitt they replied with singularly
inappropriate defiances. The plain truth is that a stiff parochial
frame of mind simply cannot make much sense of the application of
Babbitt’s criticism to current American literature and life. The
obvious remedy is wider travel in the countries of the mind.

More and Babbitt are spokesmen for classicism on the ground that
it contains a wisdom superior to modernism. They uphold the longer
traditions of the human race against the newly formed traditions of
the last fifty, seventy-five or one hundred years. They believe in a
profound vital conservatism, not in the contemporaneous versions of
radicalism. They are, when one reviews European history in a large
way, orthodox, not heterodox. In England and France we shall find
distinguished writers who like Babbitt and More are classicists,
traditionalists, conservative, orthodox, men who oppose with all
their might and intellect the drift of modern civilization and seek
to renew our sense of time-tested standards. In this chapter I intend
merely to point out some of these men, to quote from each of those
designated only a few characteristic words on the modern crisis: that
will be enough to show on the one hand the many sides and the diversity
that living classicists everywhere manifest, and on the other hand
their unanimity on the errors of modernity and the need for standards
and discipline will be enough to destroy the silly notion that the
classical party in America is engaged in a unique reaction. I cannot
here attempt rounded estimates, however brief, of the individuals
mentioned, but I shall try to transfer to the intellectual situation
in America, which is our center of interest, several useful hints they
offer. We must expect to find conservative force in England to be _sui
generis_, and likewise in France, but for that and many other reasons
an acquaintance with them, even though but a bowing one, makes for
that suppleness of mind necessary to comprehend the home movement. We
may even rid ourselves of senseless fears of certain words: the reader
whose object is the freeing of his mind must soon overcome any horror
he feels for the word, “revolutionary,” or for the word, “reactionary.”
The free mind goes everywhere and tries to judge without prejudice the
thing itself and not the name.

One thing we shall be immediately struck by is that it is purely an
accident that in America the conservative attitude should temporarily
be expressing itself almost entirely in the form of ideas and
criticism, for this is, of course, by no means an inevitable outlet
for the conservative temper. Tories, as witness Swift, can be great
artists: art was never the monopoly of the Whigs, but has always been
common to men of all hues of conviction. American Humanism therefore
does not inculcate an uncreative attitude. It cannot, it is true,
make creative ability in the arts where such ability does not exist,
but neither, for that matter, can such a theory as self-expression
produce creative ability. The Humanist attitude is a check on bad
art: self-expression, unfortunately, is a release of bad art. On this
score, which is to be preferred? No; it is a mere happenstance that
at present Humanism’s representatives are of a critical turn, men of
ideas rather than men of pronounced sensibility. Hence, their relative
unpopularity. They advocate ideas, and ideas--it is a truism--always
must fight against more inertia than emotions. An initial prejudice
exists against ideas as such, whereas some one’s strong feelings
directly affect us either to sympathy or to resistance: emotions convey
themselves almost as swiftly as actions do, but ideas, being quite
the weakest element in psychology, have a much harder time travelling
from person to person. Nor does one need to enlarge on the enmity felt
toward criticism itself, the disparagement constantly cast on this
necessary function of literary life, a habit of dispraise which, it
should be noted, grew up in the romantic period. The New Humanists are
mostly critics, and because of that many people conclude that their
doctrine, their conservatism, is sterile in the practice of the arts.
It does not follow, for it is the person who is or is not an artist,
not the doctrine which is responsible. Look abroad and you will see how
fertile in the arts of fiction and verse a classical reaction may be,
and no doubt yet will be in our own country.

Here is a gallery of brief portraits of anti-romantic writers in
England.

Philip S. Richards:--

One of the youngest English critics and probably the closest in spirit
and theory to American Humanism. In the pages of the famous review,
_The Nineteenth Century and After_, he has expounded very faithfully
to the English reader the ideas of Babbitt and More. No one has better
described the precise effect of Babbitt’s main principle on those who
come to accept it. “Only by following it up in all its ramifications,
as Babbitt works it out in his books with a wealth of illustrative
evidence, is it possible to realize its amazing fruitfulness, and its
universal applicability to the intellectual and moral problems which
we have inherited from the nineteenth century. _When once its import
is fairly grasped, its influence is like that of a new principle of
classification in one of the descriptive sciences. New and natural
lines of cleavage or affiliation at once appear, introducing order
and interdependence into a mass of chaotic or misrelated facts._ A
simple criterion is put into the reader’s hands, which he cannot help
using, even though it mean the abandonment of secret loyalties and the
dethronement of long-cherished ideals.... We may regard him (Babbitt)
as a pathologist who traces the romantic malady from its origin in
Rousseau, through its mild and chronic stage in Wordsworth, to its
acute and delirious phases in Tolstoy or the expressionist drama.” (The
italics are mine.)

T. S. Eliot:--

The prestige of this erstwhile American citizen among the younger
generation has caused the same generation to be particularly attentive
to his views on American Humanism. Eliot’s poems, so exquisite in
workmanship, subtle in perception, almost Dantean in the clarity and
power of the individual image, contain the poignant suffering of a
sensibility tortured by the ugliness, crassness and lowness of modern
civilization. His earlier criticism dealt accurately with the effects
of literary masterpieces upon his sensibility. In recent years he has
broadened his basis as a critic and introduced a religious note into
his poetry. His general point of view he now describes “as classicist
in literature, royalist in politics, and anglo-catholic in religion,”
and wisely adds, “I am quite aware that the first term is completely
vague, and easily lends itself to clap-trap; I am aware that the second
term is at present without definition, and easily lends itself to what
is almost worse than clap-trap, I mean temperate conservatism; the
third term does not rest with me to define.”

Eliot holds that we should come “to the conclusion that the humanistic
point of view is auxiliary to and dependent upon the religious point
of view. For us, religion is of course Christianity; and Christianity
implies, I think, the conception of the Church. It would be not only
interesting but invaluable if Professor Babbitt, with his learning, his
great ability, his influence, and his interest in the most important
questions of the time, could reach this point.” But on the other
hand Eliot has written an essay “to call attention to the danger of
the other extreme: the danger, a very real one, of _religion without
humanism_.”

Herbert Read:--

A close co-worker with T. S. Eliot in the field of literary criticism,
this author is severe in his condemnation of the flatness of modern
life. “For Froissart,” Read declares, “glory was the measure of all
things, the crown of all virtues. For us it has become something remote
and elusive; even something romantic and literary. We have lost the
sense of glory because we have lost the habit of faith. We neither
love deeply enough, nor feel deeply enough, nor think deeply enough,
to enjoy life’s most impressive sanction.” Read follows the path of
T. E. Hulme, whose _Speculations_ he edited, back to pre-Renascence
philosophy: he has disciplined himself on the pages of the _Summa
Theologica_ and has caught fire from the complex splendor of the Middle
Ages.

A champion of orthodoxy, he is critical of any particular form of
orthodoxy, such as American Humanism, as his essay in the _Criterion_
on _Humanism and the Absolute_ reveals. This paper discussing the
views of Norman Foerster and Julien Benda was sub-titled _The Texts
of a Debate_, and I hope it is not lost ink to refer again to the
higher standard of controversy observed in England by writers who deal
with the New Humanists and to regret that our critics of Foerster’s
_American Criticism_ were, as a rule, unable to reach up to the mark
set by Read. In the essay mentioned, Read maintains that unlike Julien
Benda, “Mr. Foerster, on behalf of the New Humanists, nowhere makes
it clear that he accepts this absolute severance of things divine and
things human. He admits that ‘the ethical will’ is ‘a power above
the ordinary self, an impersonal reality in which all men may share
despite the diversity of personal temperament, and toward which their
attitude must be one of subjection.’ But he also says that ‘the value
of supernatural intuition must be tested by the intellect,’ and
rejects any formal theology on these grounds. But the greatest of all
formal theologies, that of St. Thomas Aquinas, was precisely such a
‘testing’ of supernatural intuition by the intellect or reason. If
religious intuitions must be submitted to rational inspection, then
surely the result is a theology. Mr. Foerster cannot have it both ways:
to _hesitate_ ‘to pass beyond’ experimental knowledge to dogmatic
affirmation is merely to sit on the fence: once admit the mediation of
reason and you are involved in the necessity of dogma.”

This point, the reader will recognize, is connected with Hulme’s
argument for radical discontinuities in the universe, which was
injected into our first chapter and to which I pledged myself to return.

Wyndham Lewis:--

He has dramatized himself as an Ishmaelite in Occidental culture, the
sworn and ferocious enemy of “Time-cults” and “Time-philosophies,”
of which Bergsonism is a typical specimen. Before the Great War, he
was a radical painter and associated with Ezra Pound to propagate an
artistic cult baptized Vorticism. Since the War he has turned social
philosopher, and possibly may be a first rate Tory mind in the making.
I say in the making, for up to the present Lewis is still a chaotic
personality, given to extravagances, and not yet sufficiently trained
in metaphysics to cope with A. N. Whitehead, S. Alexander, and some
of his other _bêtes noires_. But the New Humanist can second him in
many of his theses. He and Lewis are at one in their scorn for the
contemporary cult of the child and the primitive and in their hostility
to the modern feminization of man.

In his book, _The Art of Being Ruled_, Lewis maintains--I cannot better
Montgomery Belgion’s succinct restatement in the _Bookman_--that
“what happens in a ‘liberal democracy’ is that rule by the few is not
banished, but concealed. And it is this concealment which is, Mr.
Lewis holds, responsible for so many of our contemporary ills, the
greatest of these being, perhaps, mass suggestion. In a democracy the
actual rulers are not the best rulers, but those who are most cunning
in obtaining power, and, furthermore, since their rule has to be
secret, it becomes necessary for these rulers to hypnotize the ruled
into acquiescence, to discredit the old standards of existence through
the agency of Science, and to organize the populace, to mechanize it,
to pump it full of a destructive fatalism while it is kept imagining
itself its own master.” Therefore let us have again an authority that
is an open authority. On Lewis’s analysis of pseudo-liberty the New
Humanist will agree even more heartily. “The present decay of Europe,”
I quote from Belgion again, “Mr. Lewis ascribes directly to the notion
of freedom. Since people cannot be free and in any case do not want to
be free, but only to be irresponsible, the notion of freedom has been
thought to be the notion of irresponsibility, and, thus misunderstood,
has been steadily weakening Europe. To this wasteful and disorganizing
notion of irresponsibility he opposes the notion of ‘working together’
under a central control, which, he points out, gives the comfort and
strength of solidarity.”

Hilaire Belloc:--

As a Catholic, this versatile man--politician, poet, storyteller,
essayist, historian, biographer--can naturally not regard American
Humanism as more than a half-way house. This would be in general the
Catholic position: however, both the New Humanist and the Catholic
intellectual are anti-naturalistic and pro-classical and this sets
vibrating a certain bond of sympathy for each other’s efforts to infuse
into modern literature something of what Belloc calls “the poise, the
fulness, the solemnity, the disdain of sharp effect, the glory in
control, the magic of proportion” which marked the productions of the
great classical periods.

“The classical spirit,” observes Belloc, “has not fallen into disrepute
among us: it has been forgotten. It has become alien and strange, so
that men coming across it today, in our society, are like rustics
coming across some marble of the ancients. It does not repel them, but
it hardly attracts them at all--they can make nothing of it.... It is
said that men who drink spirits lose their taste for wine and certainly
men who use violent sauces lose their taste in cooking. Under the
effect of the romantic extravagance, the general sense of the classic
died. But the last extravagances of that extravagance were left for our
time to suffer.”

I pass now from Belloc, anti-communist, anti-capitalist, and
anti-puritan, to the brilliant debater he has so greatly influenced
and to whom I shall give more space than has been devoted to the other
members of this little gallery of English champions of high tradition.

Gilbert K. Chesterton:--

First, I will show at once his views on the New Humanism because
they pour light on the subject matter of this book, the dilemmas of
liberated people today. Granted, says Chesterton: “we need a rally
of the really _human_ things; will which is morals, memory which is
tradition, culture which is the mental thrift of our fathers.” But for
him “the question really is whether Humanism can perform the functions
of religion.” His reply is unequivocal. “I do not believe that Humanism
can be a complete substitute for Superhumanism.” Why? Well, “the fact
is this: that the modern world, with its modern movements, is living on
its Catholic capital. It is using, and using up, the truths that remain
to it out of the old treasury of Christendom.” The New Humanism in
Chesterton’s judgment draws on Catholic capital, but it “has one very
wise and worthy character. It is trying to pick up the pieces; that is,
to pick up all the pieces.” Only “can it stick them together? Where
is the _cement_ which made religion corporate and popular, which can
prevent it falling to pieces in a débris of individualistic tastes and
degrees?” Chesterton makes clear his doubt that the New Humanists “have
the secret of continuity; certainly not of corporate continuity.”

Next, I should like the reader to join me in regretting that we in
America have had no G. K. Chesterton to work as an antidote to such
a popular “philosopher” as H. L. Mencken. Admit all the faults of
Chesterton, the occasional specious arguments, now and then the
patch of tawdry rhetoric, the lapses in taste and the falls into
mechanicality of style: call him, if you desire, no more than a
super-journalist. These flaws, however, are no great handicap in coping
with a popular propounder of belated nineteenth century rationalism
of the type of Mencken, when one is as playful, as pyrotechnical, as
hearty as Chesterton. By his buffooning Mencken hopes to attract the
populace to the banners of heresy: by his humor and the dazzlement
of paradoxes Chesterton aims to draw the public to orthodoxy. Thus,
the two men by their methods neutralize each other and leave the
reader free to examine coolly what it is that in all seriousness they
maintain. And in that respect Chesterton is immeasurably the larger man.

Thirdly, the history of Chesterton presents a couple of features
of special interest for the person who is reacting against the
contemporary welter of half-truths. How came Chesterton to his present
strength of conviction on so many topics toward which his foes preserve
an attitude of squeamish indecision? The startling answer is, because
his early education, like that of Cardinal Newman and of Coleridge,
consisted of fairy tales. Startling to us, that is, for many of us have
been led to conclude that there should be no place for fairy tales in
the training of the young, that the less of “fantasy” and the more of
“fact” we incorporate into education, the better developed will be the
sense of reality of our children. But this is one of the crimes against
children, the most abominable class of crimes. No one has more forcibly
stated the case for fairy tales than Coleridge. “Should children be
permitted to read romances and relations of giants and magicians and
genii? I know all that has been said against it; but I have formed my
faith in the affirmative. I know no other way of giving the mind a
love of the Great and the Whole. Those who have been led to the same
truths step by step, through the constant testimony of their senses,
seem to me to want a sense which I possess. They contemplate nothing
but _parts_, and all _parts_ are necessarily little. And the universe
to them is but a mass of _little things_.” Chesterton argues somewhat
differently, but it is apparent that he was stimulated by fairy tales
to love the inclusive, to desire to reach the center, to devote himself
to the transcendent conception. It is in the centrality and breadth of
his mind that he exceeds Mencken and the smaller fry of rationalists.

Another thing to take into account is Chesterton’s reaction to the
Yellow Nineties in England. In his youth he was immersed in Huxleyan
agnosticism and Wildean æstheticism, and he revolted vigorously from
these excesses. He found young men proud of their boredom; ennui was
taken to be a symptom of personal distinction. And he was informed
that art was divorced from morals. He replied curtly that “there is no
such thing on earth as an uninteresting subject; the only thing that
can exist is an uninterested person.... The bored has certainly proved
himself prosaic”--which places the blame where it should go. And to the
_l’art pour l’art_ people, he said: “A small artist is content with
art; a great artist with nothing except everything.”

Fourthly, Chesterton delighted himself as every one knows with the
pleasures of a modern heresy-hunt. The hunting ground was certainly
happy, for, as he pointed out, we are living in the decadence of the
great revolutionary period, and nowadays “we are more and more to
discuss details in art, politics, literature,” but “general theories
are everywhere contemned ... we will have no generalizations.” “When
the old Liberals removed the gags from all the heresies,” Chesterton
goes on to say, “their idea was that religious and philosophical
discoveries might thus be made. Their view was that cosmic truth was
so important that every one ought to bear independent testimony. The
modern idea is that cosmic truth is so unimportant that it cannot
matter what any one says.”

Vague relativism was the enemy and Chesterton went slashing after it
in the essays on Shaw, George Moore, Wells, G. Lowes Dickinson, Joseph
McCabe, Whistler and other moderns included in _Heretics_. Of them
all it seems to me that Wells best typifies what Chesterton stands
against, Wells in whom is fused sentimental naturalism and scientific
naturalism, Wells the prophet of humanitarianism. Chesterton put his
finger unhesitatingly on the soft spot in Wells’s whole program when
he noticed that “in his new Utopia he says, for instance, that a chief
point of the Utopia will be a disbelief in original sin. If he had
begun with the human soul--that is, if he had begun on himself--he
would have found original sin almost the first thing to be believed
in. He would have found, to put the matter shortly, that a permanent
possibility of selfishness arises from the mere fact of having a self,
and not from any accidents of education or ill-treatment. And the
weakness of all Utopias is this, that they take the greatest difficulty
of man and assume it to be overcome, and then give an elaborate account
of the overcoming of the smaller ones.”

Finally, I wish the reader to note that the “paradoxes of Christianity”
which Chesterton defends with so much gusto in _Orthodoxy_ were not
imposed on him, were not slavishly accepted by him because of their
attributed authority, but were personally rediscovered. It is often
thought, sometimes with cause, many times unjustifiably, that the
exponent of tradition is simply a timid and unoriginal man: he flees,
so it is said, to tradition for safety; he does not return to it
because his experience has taught him that a given tradition is right.
But do all men adopt traditional teachings out of fear? Chesterton sets
us right. Classicism is properly a life, and not a mere subscribing to
inherited doctrines, and Chesterton has described the spirit and result
of that life when he said: “I am the man who with the utmost daring
discovered what had been discovered before.... When I fancied that I
stood alone I was really in the ridiculous position of being backed up
by all Christendom. It may be, Heaven forgive me, that I did try to be
original, but I only succeeded in inventing all by myself an inferior
copy of the existing traditions of civilized religion.... I did try to
found a heresy of my own; and when I had put the last touches to it, I
discovered that it was orthodoxy.”

The vivacity of commonsense! That, in a phrase, describes the appeal
of Chesterton, and nothing, it seems to me, is more often lacking from
American literary journalism than commonsense in the first place and in
the second place commonsense sparkling at its vivacious best.

 * * * * *

To many American critics any stick is good enough to beat Humanism
with. But it has happened several times in our literary history that
the French have been more quickly appreciative than we of a genuine
new power appearing amongst our writers: it seems almost to be a law
that an unpopular spiritual force in America should first receive its
full due in France. It is a Frenchman, Louis J. A. Mercier, who has
written the first book treating the New Humanism as a movement. _Le
Mouvement humaniste aux Etats-Unis_, which makes a study of Babbitt,
More and the late W. C. Brownell, was crowned when it appeared in 1928
by the French Academy, and it drew forth sympathetic comment from the
spokesmen for a variety of groups, from the Catholic papers to the
Action Française, from the Abbé Bremond to Baron Seillière. We can
easily understand the warmth of this reception by French critics. “Here
were American authors,” says Mercier, “who spoke their own language,
who were fully alive to all the traditions, who faced squarely the
problem of man, in the light of all these traditions, and who brought a
formula for a twentieth-century approach to a common reëxamination of
all the data involved.”

Why have we not more examples in translation of the work of the
French classical reactionaries? We have a few--Benda’s _Belphégor_
and _The Treason of the Intellectuals_, Maritain’s _Three Reformers_
and _Art and Scholasticism_, Leon Daudet’s _The Stupid Nineteenth
Century_, Henri Massis’s _The Defence of the West_--but these are
barely suggestive of the resources remaining to be exploited by our
publishers. We have--to name names--no translations of such important
works as Pierre Lasserre’s _Le Romantisme français_ and _Cinquante
Ans de Pensée française_ by which we may trace the evolution of this
former pupil of Charles Maurras, nor have we a translation of Ernest
Seillière’s _Le Mal romantique_ or of his studies of Rousseau or of his
_Philosophie de l’Impérialisme_, all efforts to “de-Rousseauize” France
by large European perspectives. Above all, we have no books in English
from the energetic pen of Charles Maurras. This is odd, since no less
discerning a critic than T. S. Eliot classes Maurras as a philosopher
of rank equal to Irving Babbitt and declares that he “has written as
fine prose as any French author living.” Moreover, he says, referring
to England, “if anything, in another generation or so, is to preserve
us from a sentimental Anglo-Fascism, it will be some system of ideas
which will have gained much from the study of Maurras.”

But perhaps before speaking of Maurras further I should give a very
general classification of the French affirmers of the classic order in
life and literature. We can divide them into three groupings: (1) those
associated with the Action Française and committed to its program of
“integral nationalism” which involves a return to royalism; (2) the
Neo-Thomists, such as Henri Massis with his bogey of Orientalism and
Jacques Maritain, “the most conspicuous figure and probably the most
powerful force in contemporary French philosophy,” according to T. S.
Eliot; (3) certain critics independent of organized groups like Julien
Benda whose recent book, _The Treason of the Intellectuals_, attacks
with great force the fomenting of political passions indulged in by
some of the French classicists, notably those of the Action Française.

Charles Maurras is the outstanding man in the Action Française. He
began as an enthusiast for Auguste Comte and Positivism. In 1890 he
joined Jean Moreas in the founding of the Ecole Romane, a literary
return from Symbolism to antiquity. He wrote fiction and verse
expressive of his admiration for Hellenism. But his life’s work has
been in political and social thought and in literary criticism. For
years “Politics first” has been his cry: he establishes his teaching
on the doctrine of Joseph de Maistre who preached a turning back to
monarchism after the French Revolution. The sifting of Maurras’s
career, however, is a rather complicated business. To many he is
an atheist, certainly an agnostic; yet for some years he and his
organization, the Action Française, were Catholic, only to be condemned
in the end by the Pope. T. S. Eliot’s explanation is that Maurras is
“an unbeliever who cannot believe, and who is too honest to pretend
to himself or to others that he does believe; if others can believe,
so much the better not only for them but for the world at large. The
peculiarity of Maurras’s agnosticism (or atheism if you like) is that
he recognizes that he has much more in common, in the temporal sphere,
with Catholics than with Protestants or atheists.”

Today Maurras directs--with the partnership of the truculent Leon
Daudet--the newspaper, _Action Française_, which flies on its mast the
following inscription: “_Chef de la Maison de France, j’en revendique
tous les droits, j’en assume toutes les responsabilités, j’en accepte
tous les devoirs_” over the name of “_Le duc de Guise, héritier des
quarante Rois qui en mille ans firent la France_.” With Maurras’s
bellicosities, his fanning of national and racial hatreds, I am not
now concerned, nor should I like in any case to defend them. It is
more germane to this book to quote from him on tradition. “Not all
traditions,” he says, “are equal.... Amongst all others one tradition
offers itself, of which the conspicuous character is its almost
complete conformity with those principles of taste which express
humanity raised to its highest point. We see it come to birth in Ionia
as from the ashes of Homer; it continues under the olive trees of
Cephisus. Roman sages who adopt it make a new home for it. The middle
ages do not banish it completely either from France or from the towns
of Italy. It reforms itself and occupies the whole of Europe; but it
is Paris now, as Athens once, that preserves it. From the middle of
the sixteenth century to the end of the seventeenth, humanism and the
classical spirit, refined to an Attic point of taste, never lacked
representatives among us, or an élite worthy of understanding and
loving it.” This Græco-Latin classical tradition, which belongs to
France, Maurras goes on to say, “surpasses all others. It reveals, in
fact, a force of life equal in flame, in coloring, in simple brutality
or refined delicacy to whatever the rest of the world can show, from
the steppes of Russia to the shores of Guinea; and as no other popular
tradition can vie with it in sensibility, it defies forever all its
rivals, in the order and luminosity of its writings, in the great
generality of their style. The works of this tradition are rightly
called Humane Letters, in that they interest the whole world.” Finally,
he exclaims: “But the pursuit of the classical tradition includes
other things than gods and goddesses. It authorizes a scrupulous and
passionate criticism. Far from freezing the senses, it excites, begets
creation. Even among ourselves, above all among ourselves, there is
much to be done. We can dream of tragedies nearer to nature, more
direct and simple than those of Racine. The heroic vein of Corneille
leaves room for a poet who should be wiser than he. The reader will
be surprised if I add that what we lack especially is lyric poetry!
Consider how little can be preserved from the romantic lumber-room.
We must bring things back to Malherbe. There was once a literary
criticism, there can be again, yet finer, and lead our literature to
its renascence.”

Turning to the Neo-Thomists, it seems to me that Jacques Maritain is
the dominant figure. He began as a student of Bergson but shortly
afterwards joined the Catholic Church and today holds the position of
professor of modern history and philosophy at the Institut Catholique
of Paris. He grounds himself entirely on the doctrine of St. Thomas
