Aquinas and his whole effort is to translate it into terms of modern
life, to make it serviceable and illuminating to the man of the
twentieth century. For a few years Maritain associated himself with
the Action Française but withdrew when in 1927 the Pope proscribed the
journal of Maurras, Daudet and Pujo.

Maritain writes with an austere clarity. A fiery faith burns in the
depths of his thought and makes his most abstract pages strangely
intense. He is an utterly ruthless opponent of what he conceives to
be modern errors, and, like the American Humanists, he considers
Romanticism as the source of many of these illusions. “The word
romanticism,” he notes and I recommend his definition to the reader,
“may give rise to many confusions. I am aware that there have been
all kinds of things, even very good things, in the complex movement
classed under this name. But nothing is so idle as to argue about
words and recast their definition to suit your subject; and in spite
of everything usage has adequately settled the meaning of this one. In
so far as it signifies a religious eviction of reason and its works,
the sacred unbridling of sensation, the holy parade of self and the
adoration of primitive natural instinct, pantheism as theology, and
emotional stimulus as the rule of life, it must be confessed that
Rousseau, by his mystical naturalism, is at the direct source of such a
spiritual evil.”

Again, like the New Humanists, Maritain attacks the ubiquitous sway of
modern science on the ground that science, unconfined to its proper
rôle, dehumanizes man. In his essay on Descartes, who to Maritain
founded the principle of the deep inhumanity of modern science, we may
read: “In the higher order of Cognition the Cartesian reformation gave
the irretrievable stability of the things of the spirit to the moral
attitude of turning towards perishable goods. See what the great name
of Science has become. It is today hardly applied to anything but the
knowledge of matter, and science, _par excellence_, is regarded by
most modern thinkers as belonging to a museum. In the modern world,
reason turns its back on eternal things and is ordered to the creature.
It rates the mathematics of phenomena above wisdom. From the mountain
of its excellence it has descried all the kingdoms of the material
universe and the glory of them, and it goes down to possess them.”

And now we come to the freelances of the classical revival, to proud
polemicists like Julien Benda who never tire in the exposure of
romantic follies. Benda, novelist and essayist, tenacious opponent of
Bergson, has invented a name for the disease of pan-lyricism which
rages in America as well as in France. He calls it “belphégorism,”
for it is in his argument the worship of a false god, Belphégor or
Baal-Peor as it is written in the Bible. Belphégorism briefly is the
requirement of modern society that works of art should be _exclusively_
productive of emotions and sensations, that history should be written
as a series of heart-throbs, that philosophy and science should give
thrills. Belphégorism is the love of the indistinct, of the novelty
purely for its own sake, of subjectivity carried as far as possible.
Conversely, belphégorism hates the mind, hates discipline, hates
form. Benda makes it clear that he is no objector to the lyrical in
itself, but rather to the invasion by lyricism of all provinces of the
intellectual life. To the softness in taste of modern French society he
opposes the plastic sensibility of the great seventeenth century, the
period of Descartes and Pascal, of Corneille, Molière and Racine, of
Bossuet, of La Fontaine, of Madame de La Fayette.

By a plastic sensibility Benda means a sensibility, whose principal
modes are sight and touch, which organizes itself about the idea
of form. It loves precision and firmness. This sensibility is
localized, perhaps because it involves the more highly specialized and
developed nerves. It is, Benda declares, a centralized and correlated
sensibility, the source of poise. In contrast he places the musical
sensibility whose principal modes are hearing, taste and smell. This
is a disquieting sensibility, for it consists in sensation without
contour, it overflows the whole being with scattered and diffused
sensation. The consciousness is decentralized. The result is the
opposite of poise, being intoxication and vertigo. Modern society tends
toward a musical sensibility, a tendency Benda has become seasoned in
combating.

This distinction between two types of sensibility is very usable, and I
shall come back to it when we take up Nietzsche’s great interpretation
of the Apollo-Dionysus conflict in the final chapter. But now this
exhibit of the friends of order abroad must be closed by a longish
quotation from Benda’s _The Treason of the Intellectuals_ illustrating
the difference between two types of humanitarianism (or more properly
between Humanism and humanitarianism), a difference our New Humanists
constantly insist upon. In addition Benda brings out very well a point
usually overlooked by people who, perceiving the aristocratic leanings
of the New Humanists, accuse them of being too disdainful of their
fellow men.

“In our age the descendants of Erasmus, Montaigne, Voltaire, have
denounced humanitarianism[3] as a moral degeneration, nay, as an
intellectual degeneration, in that it implies ‘a total absence of
practical common sense’; for practical common sense has become the
measure of intellectual values with these strange ‘clerks.’

“I should like to draw a distinction between humanitarianism as I mean
it here--a sensitiveness to the abstract quality of what is human, to
Montaigne’s ‘whole form of human condition’--and the feeling which is
usually called humanitarianism, by which is meant the love for human
beings existing in the concrete. The former impulse (which would more
accurately be called humanism) is the attachment to a concept. It is a
pure passion of the intelligence, implying no terrestrial love. It is
quite easy to conceive of a person plunging into the concept of what
is human without having the least desire even to see a man. This is
the form assumed by love of humanity in the great patricians of the
mind like Erasmus, Malebranche, Spinoza, Goethe, who all were men, it
appears, not very anxious to throw themselves into the arms of their
neighbors. The second humanitarianism is a state of the heart and
therefore the portion of plebeian souls. It occurs among moralists
when lofty intellectual discipline disappears among them and gives way
to sentimental exaltation, I mean in the eighteenth century (chiefly
with Diderot) and above all in the nineteenth century, with Michelet,
Quinet, Proudhon, Romain Rolland, Georges Duhamel. This sentimental
form of humanitarianism and forgetfulness of its conceptual form
explain the unpopularity of this doctrine with so many distinguished
minds, who disavow two equally repulsive commonplaces in the arsenal of
political ideology. One of them is the ‘patriotic bore’ and the other
‘the universal embrace.’

“The humanitarianism[4] which holds in honor the abstract quality of
what is human, is the only one which allows us to love _all_ men.
Obviously, as soon as we look at men in the concrete, we inevitably
find that this quality is distributed in different quantities, and
we have to say with Renan: ‘In reality one is _more or less_ a man,
_more or less_ the son of God ... I see no reason why a Papuan should
be immortal.’ Modern equalitarians, by failing to understand that
there can be no equality except in the abstract and that inequality is
the essence of the concrete, have merely displayed the extraordinary
vulgarity of their minds as well as their amazing political clumsiness.

“Humanism, as I have defined it, has nothing to do with
internationalism. Internationalism is a protest against national
egotism, not on behalf of a spiritual passion, but on behalf of another
egotism, another earthly passion. It is the impulse of a certain
category of men--laborers, bankers, industrialists--who unite across
frontiers in the name of private and practical interests, and who only
oppose the national spirit because it thwarts them in satisfying those
interests.

“In comparison with such impulses, national passion appears an
idealistic and disinterested impulse. In short, humanism is also
something entirely different from cosmopolitanism, which is the simple
desire to enjoy the advantages of all nations and all their cultures,
and is generally exempt from moral dogmatism.”

 * * * * *

Such, briefly indicated, is the general field of contemporary thought
in which American Humanism fits. I deem it, however, premature and
over-enthusiastic to perceive in this field the beginnings of a new
renascence, and likewise I do not think the parallel between the living
asserters of classical ideals and the Humanists of the Renascence
should be stressed. True enough, the modern revivalists have entered
into a gigantic battle to overthrow the preëminence of the dogma of
Naturalism, just as the Renascence men assaulted the preëminence of
petrified theological dogma, and true enough the moderns catch their
vision, as did their brothers in the fifteenth century, from the
ancient literatures. But it is yet to be seen whether they can do more
than _defend_ the ancestral treasures, whether they can be more than a
corrective force to the excesses and barbarisms of our era. Have they
the potency to initiate a real transformation of modern society? Can
they even arrest its downward plunge, let alone reverse its direction?
Have they the hidden strength to work a miracle? The questions
discourage all desires to prophesy. The only thing that can be said
is that the traditionalist thinkers are making a supremely important
contribution to the thought of today, that by their contribution they
tend to improve the general intellectual situation, and the first step
is the production of a more favorable situation, in itself a huge task,
for diamond ideas about the “good life.” Meanwhile, the most that can
be said about any notion of a coming renascence is that it may have the
energizing value of a myth: it is perhaps akin in its tonic effect on
the spirit to the conception of the general strike which Georges Sorel
proclaimed should be the heroic myth of the proletariat in its long
struggle.

Now if we compare the theory of American Humanists with the theories
or partly worked out theories of their allies in Europe, we shall see
that we have at home not the least developed and least positive form
of conservatism. The Europeans either come to rest on Catholicism
(Chesterton, Belloc, Massis, Maritain), which for many Americans
seems impossible, involving an act of too much faith, and furthermore
they feel too _psychologically_ distant from Rome; or the Europeans
dedicate themselves to some political movement such as French
royalism (Maurras and his disciples) which to some extent perverts
their classical attitude; or finally they fall back on some personal
philosophy, scarcely more than a strong predisposition in favor of
the disinterested activity of the mind (Benda) or else very much in
the forming and full of idiosyncrasies (Wyndham Lewis). American
Humanism has not complicated itself by theological alliances, nor
subordinated itself to the pursuit of definite political aims. What
is striking is the extent to which it has freed itself from the
personal and idiosyncratic. It exists apart from the personalities
of More and Babbitt. Here is a body of general ideas, substantial
yet pliable, to which all who seize the few underlying principles may
resort and from which all may take a working experimental outlook on
the problems of their own lives. Here is a system for the guidance
of one’s thought, not for its repression and confinement. Here is an
ideology too definite to be treated whimsically, but not so rigid as to
be unadaptable to the taste of the individual. It has a completeness
wanting in the personal philosophies of Lewis and Benda, and it does
not appear to many so arbitrary as the doctrines of the Church. _Its
great recommendation is that it is founded on the positive critical
experimental spirit of modern times._ This in the remainder of the book
I shall try to demonstrate.

 VI

 A CREATIVE CONCEPTION OF MAN

  _“Humanism is the study and practice of the principles of human
  happiness_ uncomplicated _by naturistic dogmas on the one side and
  religious dogmas on the other.”_--G. R. Elliott in _Humanism and
  America_.


Humanism is a term that has been defined, and well defined, many times.
Yet there persists in the public mind the feeling that Humanism is a
portmanteau word and the New Humanists have failed to make it denote
anything precise. The public is not to blame for this feeling, nor
are the New Humanists responsible. Several causes may be alleged,
the first being the romanticizing of the word by writers like Walter
Pater. “For the essence of humanism,” Pater once wrote, “is that belief
of which he (Pico della Mirandola) seems never to have doubted, that
nothing which has ever interested living men and women can wholly
lose its vitality--no language they have spoken, nor oracle beside
which they have hushed their voices, no dream which has once been
entertained by actual human minds, nothing about which they have ever
been passionate, or expended time and zeal.” This is to make Humanism
denote simply an all-embracing curiosity, an insatiable appetite for
everything, no matter how trivial. _Humani nihil a me alienum puto_
(I consider nothing human alien to me)--this widely quoted line is
the motto of those who take Pater’s definition too seriously. But the
line is spoken in Terence’s play as an excuse for meddling, and Babbitt
comments drily that it “might serve appropriately enough as a motto for
the humanitarian busybody with whom we are all so familiar nowadays,
who goes around with schemes for reforming almost everything--except
himself.” No, Humanism means more than a universal curiosity about
one’s fellow creatures, and it does not mean humanitarianism. The
humanitarians, however, have claimed the gracious word, and this is the
second cause of confusion as to definition.

It is essential to see that Humanism and humanitarianism are quite
distinct. “A person who has sympathy for mankind in the lump,” to quote
from Babbitt, “faith in its future progress, and desire to serve the
great cause of this progress, should be called not a humanist, but
a humanitarian.” Such a person--he is legion!--exalts sympathy, and
expects that somehow in the coming years man’s altruistic impulses will
triumph over his egoism. He is an optimist, an ardent believer in the
dogma of progress, and usually a superstitious worshipper of modern
science. _Exempli gratia_: Auguste Comte, Rousseau and Herder have
at one time or another been hailed as “humanists,” whereas, Babbitt
asserted in his first book, they are properly called humanitarian
enthusiasts. So today we sometimes read in our newspapers that certain
former Unitarian preachers are Humanists, despite the fact that their
sermons upon examination prove to be almost pure humanitarianism,
or we hear writers like the American historian, Harry Elmer Barnes,
referred to as a Humanist. But Barnes, we may say in passing, would
have seemed to Matthew Arnold the propagandist for a “spurious and
degenerated sub-form” of Hellenism. Arnold would doubtless have dubbed
this very vocal rationalist simply a Millite, a word he coined from the
name of England’s famous utilitarian philosopher. Humanism, properly
understood, is in fact the stern critic of philanthropic rationalists
like Barnes or sentimental enthusiasts like the Reverend John Haynes
Holmes. _For Humanism is a doctrine and a discipline, aristocratic
in temper, selective in practice, which makes its first concern, the
self-perfecting of the individual._

There is still a third cause which must be touched on to explain
the confusion that blankets the public mind. In the literary
campaign staged against the New Humanists in the spring of 1930, the
naturalistic writers employed an old debating trick:--to pretend
bewilderment at the definitions offered and to press for closer and
closer definitions. The aim apparently was to force the New Humanists
at last into some utterly rigid statement of their creed, if not into
some ultimate banality; therefore anything short of a completely rigid
definition was called an inadequate one. But the New Humanist is
too awake to the dangers of Neo-Classicism to yield to this type of
pressure. Humanism lays the stress on the inner life, Neo-Classicism
on the external conventions. The New Humanist maintains the seat
of authority in himself: he studies with zeal the wisdom stored in
tradition, but he insists on his internal corroboration of that
wisdom as the all-important thing. He does not, that is, set up an
authority, as the Neo-Classicist does, which is completely exterior to
himself. Above all, he perceives the folly of an Age of Rules, which
happens whenever Neo-Classicism gains ascendency. In short, if the New
Humanism could be set forth in a perfectly dry detailed code, you may
be certain that it would not be Humanism, but only another recurrence
of Neo-Classicism. A true definition of Humanism must breathe with the
vitality inherent in its subject.

The New Humanist, then, disassociates himself from the impoverishment
of Neo-Classicism, from excessive purism, as consciously as he
separates himself from indiscriminate humanitarianism and the mere
inquisitiveness of the æsthetic type. As was said above, Humanism is
a doctrine and a discipline, aristocratic in temper, selective in
practice, which makes its first concern, the self-perfecting of the
individual. This sense for the term is not new or perverse; it has, in
fact, long been emphasized as the following passage from a late Latin
writer, Aulus Gellius, will show. Gellius was of a somewhat crabbed
and pedantic temper: Babbitt admits this and adds that he was too much
inclined to leave sympathy out altogether; but what is interesting
is that in the decadence of Rome Gellius made complaint about the
corruption of the term _humanitas_ to give it a humanitarian flavor.
Here is his protest--it might have been written yesterday--against the
confusion of humanism with humanitarianism.

“Those who created the Latin language,” the passage occurs in the
_Noctes Atticae_, “and those who spoke it well do not give the word
_humanitas_ the vulgar meaning which is synonymous with the Greek word
_philanthropia_, which means an active kindness, a tender benevolence
for all men. But they give the word the meaning which the Greeks attach
to _paideia_, which we call education, knowledge of the fine arts.
Those who show the most taste and disposition for these studies are
the most worthy to be called _humanissimi_. For man alone among all
living beings is able to devote himself to cultivating a study which
for that reason has been called _humanitas_. Such is the meaning given
to this by the ancients, particularly by Varro and Cicero. Almost all
their works show examples, so I shall content myself with quoting one
only. I have chosen the opening of the first book of Varro ‘Concerning
Human Things’: _Praxiteles, qui propter artificium egregium nemini est
paulum modo humaniori ignotus_ (Praxiteles, whose excellent talent as
an artist has made him known to every man at all skilled in the arts).
Here _humanior_ does not bear the vulgar meaning of easy, tractable,
benevolent, even though lacking in knowledge of letters. That meaning
would not express the author’s thought. It means an educated, a learned
man, one who is acquainted with Praxiteles through books and history.”

 * * * * *

What is the doctrine of the New Humanism? The most compact statement
I have found was written by Robert Shafer in the course of a powerful
polemical article in the _Bookman_. It deserves the most careful study
by the reader, for from it can be extracted almost the whole of
Humanism. It is a remarkably comprehensive formulation: despite its
brevity it manages to imply all that a New Humanist has to say about
his conception of man.

“Against romanticism, humanitarian sympathy, mechanistic or vitalistic
determinism, the doctrine of progress and the like, has been opposed a
skeptical criticism of life and letters which rests ultimately on the
proposition that man differs not alone in complexity of organization,
but in kind, from the animal, and that his happiness depends upon his
recognition and cultivation of that element in his being which is
distinctive of him. This is held to be possible; man is held to be,
within limits, capable of responsible choice. This capacity is regarded
as the core of the distinctively human element in man’s constitution,
which is thus dual--‘animal,’ one may roughly say, united inexplicably
with ‘something more,’ or the unlimited chaos of impressions and
spontaneous desires united with the potential capacity of control and
reduction to ordered activity. No conclusion is necessarily implied for
or against the doctrine of grace, but insistence is steady that without
a wisely directed educational discipline and a strict inner discipline
man’s fulfilment of his proper end is certainly impossible. To choose
is to discriminate, and, for this, habituation to self-restraint is
essential; it is, indeed, the foundation on which the whole structure
of distinctively human life rests.

“It is recognized that this general position is not susceptible of
rational proof--reason itself being the organ of discrimination, and
acting, when it acts legitimately, in a purely instrumental capacity.
But none the less his position is, the humanist adds, irrefutable and
soundly based. It concerns a realm of being into which ‘modern science’
simply does not enter. Exact science, so-called, is concerned with
quantity--how much?--but about quality--how good?--it has nothing to
say. The humanist is concerned, not with physical existence taken by
itself, but with distinctively human excellence, or self-fulfilment,
or, in a word, happiness. Here the one possible appeal is to actual
human experience--the recorded experience of past generations and
the inner experience of the individual disciplined and formed in the
light of selective tradition. The strength of humanism lies in its
positive experiential basis, which affords it, not an absolute, but a
sufficient, authority.”

Could the sum and substance of Humanism be put more plainly than the
above? The topic can be expanded, explained, illustrated, but in the
end when it is boiled down again, we shall find that Robert Shafer has
given the candid and accurate summary of its meaning. Greater rigidity
would be inexact--remember that words like “idealism,” “skepticism,”
“deism,” even “pragmatism” offer a similar necessary fluidity to the
definer--for the thing defined has flowing contours and is alive with
movement. What then is the difficulty many inquirers about Humanism
confess to experiencing when it comes to seizing its import? Well,
our difficulties with understanding definitions are not entirely with
words; they are of two kinds: verbal and psychological. The first
is more easily overcome: it requires simply a clear statement and
attentive reading. In view of the fulness with which More and Babbitt,
Foerster and Shafer and many others have written about the meaning of
Humanism, there is no excuse whatever for any failure to grasp verbally
what they are driving at. A college student could write it out on an
examination paper. But the other kind of difficulty, the psychological
obstacle to understanding, is the one that takes real effort to remove.
By understanding a definition psychologically, I mean experiencing
the definition. I mean relating it to one’s own experience or lack
of experience. I mean filling the verbal signs with living personal
content.

I will give one simple illustration of the gap between verbal
understanding and psychological comprehension. A good deal of the
vocabulary of warfare is used by both soldiers and civilians. The
counters are the same, but what the soldier has experienced as the
meaning of these words--“No-man’s Land,” “trenches,” “battle,” etc.--is
radically different from the mere verbal associations the civilian
makes with them. The soldier and the civilian try to converse: it is
well known that the returned soldier quickly gives up and refuses
to talk about his war experiences except to those who have had an
actual taste of battle. The civilian wonders why the soldier is
uncommunicative about the war and never guesses that it is partly
because the soldier has become aware that what to him are signs of
experience are to the civilian simply words, as we say.

Now in the case of Humanism this disparity in experience between
the expounder and the listener does not exist. There is a ground of
human experience common to all that can be appealed to. But how to
reach it? Here the reader must exercise his good will, must be honest
with himself, must make an effort to carry the interpretation over
from words to experience, to take it out of the verbal forest of the
mind and apply it to all his psychology. This means in many cases an
effort against the habits of education we have received, for the latest
reformers in education are correct in telling us that our civilization
is too exclusively verbal, that we trust too much in words cut off
from things. Such a psychological translation of the perfectly clear
verbal answers already given to the question, what is twentieth century
Humanism, I now propose to attempt.

 * * * * *

_The final object of the New Humanist is happiness._ If he is asked,
why discipline oneself, why try to live in a certain way, his candid
reply is, to attain happiness. All his system-making is directed toward
that end, and that end, happiness, happens to be one of the very few
values unquestioned by human beings, for who in a state of happiness
could conceivably question his own happiness?

Here there is practically no barrier to what I have called the
psychological understanding or, perhaps better, realization of
humanistic doctrine. For every one knows at first hand the state of
happiness: it has come to him accidentally, it has been fleeting, but
brief and chance as its visitations have been, the impression of them
is indelible. We long all our lives to re-experience happiness, and
we are incredibly ingenious in trying to find it. The cynical are no
exception: their cynicism about happiness arises from repeated failures
to rediscover it, and their real wish is still for that on which they
pour (why so bitter?) ridicule.

Mankind universally hopes for happiness. It flees from the unhappy
state and looks everywhere for the acknowledged good thing, the state
of being happy. The sad platitude is that mankind fails first to find
as much happiness as it wants, second, to maintain the state when it
is experienced, and third, to gain an insight into the secrets of
producing happiness. Not on happiness as the ultimate human value,
but on the way to happiness, the means of procuring it, is there
disagreement.

The majority--a fact that has always struck wise men with
astonishment--have always depended on distractions and pleasures. They
have situated the source of happiness in the world around them. They
have felt that a change in the environment was what was needed. Make
the world an Eden, say the reformers, and man will be happy. But the
Garden of Eden, like the Kingdom of Heaven, is within, has been the
retort of the sage.

The New Humanism commits itself first of all to a serious study of the
principles of happiness. It does not dream of a geographical Paradise,
but says, look to human nature! What is the constitution of human
nature? How ought man to live according to this constitution? For in no
other way but by obeying the laws of his being can he win an enduring
happiness.

But here is where an apparent paradox deludes so many. Man is not
born free, as Rousseau claimed, but born slave to things. Things are
in the saddle and ride mankind, and mankind expects to be happy under
those conditions. To change the conditions, to come out from servitude
to things to a command over them--the aim of the religious and the
humanistic alike--is so arduous and perilous a revolt that it looks
like anything but the way to happiness. Man’s _inclination_ is to
remain enslaved to things the while he hopes they will not ride him
harshly. His _wish_ is to oppose his inclination to servitude, and
to fight his way to psychological freedom. He cannot however imagine
that he can be happy warring against his attachment to things. And yet
Thoreau speaks for all the sagacious reflecters on life when he says:
“But men labor under a mistake. The better part of the man is soon
ploughed into the soil for compost. By a seeming fate, commonly called
necessity, they are employed, as it says in an old book, laying up
treasures which moth and rust will corrupt and thieves break through
and steal. It is a fool’s life, as they will find when they get to the
end of it, if not before.”

 * * * * *

_The New Humanist maintains that human consciousness is dual._ This is
the primary fact to be recognized, he says, in any quest of happiness.
He expresses this fact by speaking of our two selves, which he calls
the natural self and the human self, or the animal and human selves or
the lower and higher self. In respect to this terminology, admittedly
crude, it must be kept in mind that the New Humanist writes as a
practical psychologist, not as a theoretical one who also has to drop
technical language and speak in the world’s tongue when it comes to
practical psychology, and it must be still more kept in mind that no
moral connotations are meant by the terms. If I say that plants are
a lower form of life than animals, I do not imply that plants are
reprehensible for their lesser development--nor does the New Humanist
regard the life of the body as morally inferior to, let us say, the
life of the mind. The grading is made in the scientific spirit: it
is based on measurement of life, not on moral requirements. The
discrimination between lower and higher is psychological, and nothing
else.

Surely, no elaborate documentation is needed to establish the fact that
there is an overwhelming crowd of witnesses from antiquity onwards to
the existence of a practical dualism in human psychology. Two eloquent
writers will stand for the multitude: Cicero in antiquity and Matthew
Arnold in the nineteenth century. They can be combined in a single
quotation from the latter’s _Literature and Dogma_. “Man alone of
living creatures, Cicero says, goes feeling after ‘the discovery of an
_order_, a law of _good taste_, a _measure_ for his words and actions.’
Other creatures submissively follow the law of their nature; man alone
has an impulse leading him to set up some other law to control the bent
of his nature.” That is, a goldfish or a wren or a crocodile or a cat
is always true to its species. Nature looks to it that they obey, as
it were, the conscience of their species. They behave strictly as they
should, being what they are. They are loyal to themselves. But man is
not so completely cared for. He belongs to a very elastic species: his
behavior can vary enormously and fall far away from the norm of the
race. We have words to indicate this drop from what we instinctively
feel man should be, such words as “bestial,” “sub-human,” “inhuman,”
“unmanly,” and the like. Nature apparently is interested in man only
up to a certain point, giving him a human form, the capacity to feel,
the ability to think, but for all Nature cares, he may be as disordered
as he pleases in his emotional and mental life. An idiot, from the
standpoint of Nature, is as good as a genius. From this arises man’s
sense of division in himself. He is a natural creature; true; but add
that in comparison with the beasts and their constant observance of
their laws he appears a crazed natural creature. But, a strange thing,
he has the intuition that he also has the possibility of becoming a
sane creature. Only--he himself must learn what sanity is, what its
laws are and how to follow them.

He has, in Arnold’s words, “the notion of a whole self as opposed
to a partial self, a best self to an inferior, to a momentary self
a permanent self requiring the restraint of impulses a man would
naturally have indulged;--because by _attending_ to his life, man found
it had a scope beyond the wants of the present moment.” I wish to
bring home, if I can, the nature of what is here called our partial,
inferior, momentary self, and to do so I shall call upon the studies of
the Behavioristic psychologists. What the New Humanist terms our lower
natural self is in my opinion best named our mechanical self.

The Behaviorists consider man to be a behaving mechanism. Man has
nothing to say about his heredity, his birth, his early environment,
the language and customs he is taught, and so forth. He comes into the
world with the native disposition to make certain responses (unlearned
responses) and he soon is trained on the basis of these to make
thousands of others (conditioned responses), often of a very arbitrary
character. A response-mechanism set ticking and continued in motion by
the ceaseless impact of stimuli from the environment.

Physiologically, we are, every one admits, best viewed as machines.
Our tissues repair themselves, our blood flows through an elaborate
system whose tracings we know only by hearsay (i.e., by diagram),
our nails and hair grow without our supervision, we are unaware of
the functioning of organs like the liver, unless they get out of
order,--but it is unnecessary to specify details of what every one
grants: namely, that the ceaseless movements of our bodies do not
depend very much on our accurate and intimate knowledge of them, nor
upon our directive powers. We stand toward them in the relation of
mechanician to machine, only we are rather poor mechanicians and in our
ignorance frequently harm what we are supposed merely to tend.

But the Behaviorist enormously extends the reign of mechanics: he
puts our psychology--or what we call our psychology--under mechanical
rule, going so far as to reduce our emotions to visceral tensions and
our thoughts to sub-vocal movements in the larynx. In this he is far
more right than most people think. But we do not need to argue that
out now. I refer to very obvious matters, such as the occurrence of
emotions to us. _They occur to us_: we do not as a rule induce them.
“A peculiar feeling came over me,” we say. It did! “I was seized by
anger.” I was! “I was taken by surprise.” Right! Every day long we are
swept by a succession of emotions, the majority of which clearly we had
nothing to do with. We did not choose to experience them. They came to
us.

Again there is a constant procession--better say jumble--of images,
ideas, associations, words and nonsense in our minds. Our brains
wander most of the time: they are as Arnold Bennett calls them
“gentlemen-at-large” and deucedly unreliable. They go on operating
after a fashion whether we will or not. And what is this but sheer
mechanicality?

But this is our natural self, this is as biology and sociology
have made us. We take stock of ourselves in adulthood and we find
probably that we are a pronounced type--an intellectual, a highly
emotional artist, a sportsman. Did we create our type, our particular
psychological build, any more than we colored our eyes or invented the
shoes we Westerners wear? Our temperament--did we make it or do we find
ourselves with one?

A very great deal of what we call ourselves we have to consign to the
Behaviorist and agree with him that it is determined. But the New
Humanist will not yield all to Dr. John B. Watson and his relentless
observers in the laboratory. He will not deny consciousness, nor the
will, nor higher potentialities than are manifest in the animal self.
He balances against the mechanical the human self which, he maintains,
man can develop. What, more precisely than thus far stated, is meant
by the human self? We can get at the conception if we think of our
potentialities.

Actually, we are turned out by Nature and Society as uneven-sided
human beings, not well-rounded ones. Actually, we are prejudiced and
partial, not unprejudiced and impartial. Actually, our minds are
enslaved, not free. Actually, we are nonsensical, not commonsensible.
But we have the potentiality for all these things we have not,--for
harmonious development of ourselves, for disinterestedness and
impartiality, for the free intelligence and the most esoteric of all
virtues, commonsense. Only none of these things ever comes to man as a
gift. Each must be deliberately and laboriously acquired. Man, then,
humanistically viewed, is born with a lower self but has a margin of
potentiality which he, and only he, can cultivate, and by so doing he
creates a higher self to which it is proper to give the name human.
Acquirements, not gifts--that is the sure mark of the truly human.
(Incidentally, the greatest of these acquirements is will, but of that
later.)

At the risk of seeming unable to let go of the idea of a cleft between
the natural man and self-perfected man, I will cite one illustration
of the difference between our actual psychology and our potential
psychology. Man has the possibility of simultaneous thought on
several different subjects, but this possibility is never actualized
by accident in the ordinary course of events. Ordinarily we think
in succession, following a train of thought, as we say. But we
can, by special training, learn to follow several lines of thought
at once. One begins with very simple exercises (immensely difficult
to the beginner, however), such as focussing on the movement of the
second-hand of a watch, reciting a stanza of poetry, and running
through a multiplication table at the same time, and then works up to
what appear staggering exercises in mental gymnastics. For this sort of
work, at which geniuses fail the first time as completely as the most
ungifted, the natural self is of no avail. Simultaneous thought becomes
an actuality only to those who themselves bring it to birth.

It seems strange that any one should deny on any practical basis the
selves of the New Humanist’s conception: one, largely or altogether
automatic, actual, natural, subject to the “law for thing,” the other
acquired, born out of potentiality by one’s own effort, not natural but
human, subject to the “law for man.” Yet this dualism has been fought
throughout recorded history. Many gifted men have made frenzied efforts
to deny the gap, to coalesce the two selves, to prove that man not
only theoretically but practically must be monistic. One of the latest
epical mass-movements to deny psychological dualism was Romanticism.

 * * * * *

_Submission to the natural self is the Romantic attitude._ Here, I
believe, we can strike rock bottom. As sketched above, a human being
consists of three related modes of experience (sensations, emotions,
and ideas) and in these three modes everything, according to the
Behaviorist, a very great deal according to everybody, is done not by
our consent and knowledge, but, as it were, by itself. But toward such
a threefold apparatus for experiencing, an attitude may be assumed,
and this attitude is fundamental in our psychology. Given one kind of
attitude, and you get the foundation of Romanticism. Another attitude
toward the selfsame creature, and you have laid the cornerstone of
the humanistic life. Who or what takes the attitude? Whatever we mean
by the word “I.” The Romanticist glories in identifying as closely
as possible whatever the word “I” means with his mechanical self. He
resigns his Ego to his organism. He submits to the riot of his senses,
feelings and thoughts and calls the submission good.

It might be said, I suppose, that earlier in this book I scored an easy
victory by putting in contrast a composite figure, quite lacking in
“personality,” with Matthew Arnold. Of course, it might be retorted to
me, Arnold is superior to any constructed representative of modern men.
Arnold should rather be compared with some great Romantic, not with an
instructive psychological chart. Very well: in the place of that poor
G. R. we unfeelingly exposed, let us put Jean-Jacques Rousseau, the
man of genius who preached so eloquently the surrender to our natural
selves.

Irving Babbitt has often been accused of “Rousseauphobia.” I cannot
find the justice in the accusation, which ignores Babbitt’s reasons
for emphasizing Rousseau’s influence. Babbitt cannot be said to have
slighted the precursors of Rousseau, such as Hutcheson and the Earl of
Shaftesbury, or not to have put a great deal of weight on Francis Bacon
as a founder of the modern world. And it is true, as Babbitt claims,
that though Rousseauistic ideas were in the world long before Rousseau,
it was the Genevan who gave them their great driving power in our era:
it was Rousseau who cast the wonderful glamor over them that made them
irresistible to modern man. Rousseau is the representative Romantic. He
is the symbolic figure, and hence he is bound to appear very large in
any treatment of Romanticism. Furthermore, the extent and depth of his
influence can hardly be minimized, as the enormous literature written
about him shows. Let it be Rousseau then to serve here, as in so many
previous expositions, as the arch-exponent of the Romantic attitude.

He accepted the contradictions in himself, saying that his heart
and his mind did not seem to belong to the same person. He scorned
the intellect, the weakest and least developed of our three modes
of experience, saying that the man who thinks is a depraved animal.
He gave the primacy to feeling, and exalted sensation. He declared
that man was corrupted by Society and that man was naturally good. He
developed on his own account, as Maritain says, “a singular doctrine
of absolute non-resistance to the impulses of sensation, a doctrine of
complete passivity as the condition of full expansion of the primitive
goodness, which is simply a natural quietism.” Yes; the “Father of the
modern world” was an irresponsible. For he loved, nay he worshipped,
that irresponsible creature, the natural self.

The natural self is innocent: that is, it is neither good nor bad.
Good or bad can only appear in the attitude taken toward the natural
self. The Romantic with all his enthusiasm follows the inclinations of
the natural self. He shirks the disciplining of it. He wants unity but
he thinks he can find unity by subsiding into the life of feeling and
sensation, stilling the mind. He goes with Nature, like a child led by
its nurse, and he calls it liberty.

At the end of Romanticism there is the pseudo-necromancer of Vienna
with his complicated method for teaching the proud spirit of Man to
stoop, to chain itself to the blind forces of his temperament, to
forget in slavery to the senses and feelings that man is in essence a
passion for perfection.

 * * * * *

_Responsibility for the natural self is the Humanist attitude._ Like
the Romanticist, the Humanist discovers that whatever is signified by
the word “I”--and the determination of the signification of the word
“I” is a poser for the infant science of psychology[5]--is attached
to a surprising creature, unreasonable, expansive, changeable, an
absorbing mystery to study. He finds furthermore that the creature has
the complete upper hand so far as the “I” in it is concerned. But the
Humanist cannot acquiesce in this arrangement. It seems to him that the
creature is not wicked but certainly a fool. There is nothing wrong
with its appetites, only the organism is singularly unintelligent in
gratifying them. It is full of whims, some of them dangerous to the
organism’s life. It loves to waste time in day-dreaming, to waste
emotion in foolish anxieties, to waste energy in muscular fidgets.
Clearly, the creature itself would be happier if it were under
intelligent guidance, if it were taught to obey instead of always being
given the reins and dashing away at random. Thus, the New Humanist
feels that his real, deepest self is somewhat apart from his natural
mechanical self. He wants to be the driver of his machine, not a mere
intoxicated passenger in a runaway vehicle. He wants to be responsible
for the manifestations of his personality. In responsibility for his
natural self lies for him the dignity of man. This for him is the true
liberty.

But how to attain and exercise this liberty? For, to begin with,
the workings of the natural self are not known to the one who
wishes to master it. Hence, the humanistic stress on self-study and
self-knowledge. But further, even granting a fairly complete working
knowledge of oneself, of what one’s biases and characteristic reactions
are, what one’s defects and points of strength are, one may still
be at a total loss, powerless to alter or control the spontaneous
phenomena of the organism. Hence, the humanistic stress on the need for
developing the will.

 * * * * *

_The New Humanist upholds a creative conception of man._ The word
“creative” has been so cheapened by Romantic usage in which it is
made synonymous with mere temperamental overflow that one is almost
inclined to drop the word until it loses its present indiscriminate
associations. However, no other word will do to describe the humanistic
conception of man, for in the Humanist’s view man literally creates
his manliness. Man has to find his own center, develop his own
potentialities, create his own order in his psychology.

We can put the matter thus. Every one without exception has felt that
his psychology was organized about a principle of dispersion. His
inclinations have pointed outwards; he wants to let himself go, he
yearns to express himself. “You must be yourself,” said Jean-Jacques
Rousseau over and over, and in his mouth it meant what Maritain says
it did. “You must _be_ your feeling, as God _is_ His being. Does God,
Who is all act, need forming? You must regard as a sin every attempt
to form yourself, or allow yourself to be formed, to right yourself,
to bring your discords to unity again. Every form imposed on the inner
world of the human soul, whether it come from nature or grace, is a
sacrilegious wrong to nature.” This is psychology based on the Many.

But just as there is a metaphysic of the One as well as the Many,
so there is a psychology of the One. Every one without exception,
I daresay, has felt in his psychology a principle of unity. He has
experienced, that is, at various moments a strong wish to integrate
himself, to pull himself together, to become a concentrated unified
individual. It is with this wish the New Humanist identifies the human,
and he pits it against the inclination to accept the discords. The
wish for unity is the creative wish.

What could be more uncreative than the despising of the mind by Luther,
Rousseau and scores of lesser Romantics? The mind, a part of our
resources to be exploited along with the other parts--why should it
be treated puritanically any more than the body should be despised?
Again what can be more uncreative than to transfer the blame for one’s
faults to Society, or to make a cult of inaction as Rousseau did? The
New Humanist replies that the fault after all is not in our stars or
in Society but in ourselves and that we must work upon ourselves--a
genuinely creative attitude.

I direct attention again to human potentialities. Proportionateness--it
must be created. The free intelligence--it must be struggled for.
Disinterestedness--an ideal toward which no step is ever taken without
effort. Commonsense, the fusion of mental, emotional and practical
judgments--it must be striven for. Self-control--impossible without the
use of will. The will--dormant, it must be awaked. Unity--it must be
achieved.

These, according to the New Humanist, are some of our higher
possibilities. He takes the attitude that a man’s work is to bring them
towards fruition, that in so doing lies happiness, that by so doing one
is truly creative, making and strengthening a human self to oppose and
dominate the vagaries and weaknesses of the natural self.

 VII

 THE DISCIPLINE OF HUMANISM

  _“My whole method is experimental, or it might be less ambiguous
  to say if the word were a fortunate one, experiential.”_--Irving
  Babbitt in _Rousseau and Romanticism_.


It is obvious that the New Humanist does not think there is much
insight in Walt Whitman’s line, “And if the body were not the soul,
what is the soul?” We can epitomize the preceding chapter and at the
same time add a new element by extracting two sentences from Babbitt’s
_Rousseau and Romanticism_. “Like all the great Greeks Aristotle
recognizes that man is the creature of two laws: he has an ordinary
or natural self of impulse and desire and a human self that is known
practically as a power of control over impulse and desire. If man
is to become human he must not let impulse and desire run wild, but
must oppose to everything excessive in his ordinary self, whether in
thought or deed or emotion, the law of measure.” The new element, the
law of measure, relates to discipline. I have dealt with the major
conception in the Humanist doctrine: I have shown that the New Humanism
is primarily individualistic and for the sake of individual growth:
I described it as aristocratic in temper. The last is surely evident
from the humanistic stress on the rule of our momentary ordinary selves
by our best selves, and from the Humanist’s search in the inherited
literatures for “the best that has been thought and uttered,” and
finally from his making the question of leadership the paramount
problem for democratic nations to solve. But in the general description
of the New Humanism I noted in addition that it was a discipline and
selective in practise, and these are the matters of this chapter.

Here again we have to combat psychological obtuseness. To repeat: a
logical understanding of a text is one thing, and quite another is a
living warm apprehension, a psychological understanding, of the same
text. The first is an affair of words: the second is more real, related
to feelings and conduct as well as to the formulating intellect. Now
it so happens that it is a peculiarity of our age to be blank-minded
whenever there is question of a psychological method to realize
in practice the ideas and ideals we adopt. We think that the mere
entertaining of the ideas and ideals we are attracted by is sufficient,
we think that we shall naturally grow to embody them completely without
the need of any special discipline or “way of life.” In short, never do
we inquire into “ways of life” at all, never do we exercise the kind of
thought Arnold employed when he sought in the Biblical texts for the
_method_ and _secret_ of Jesus. In dealing with the New Humanism in
its aspect of a technique for living intelligently, we must therefore
make a special and unusual effort to see the theory in terms of actual
practice.

There is no initial difficulty in feeling the spirit of the New
Humanist discipline, for though it derives from Aristotle, it is as
thoroughly modern as the laboratory spirit. No one could in fact be
a stauncher supporter of the modern spirit defined by Irving Babbitt
as “the positive and critical spirit, the spirit that refuses to take
things on authority” than Babbitt himself. Only, he adds, you must be
a complete positivist. He then goes on to exhort his readers to be
individualists: “the man who plants himself, not on outer authority but
on experience, is an individualist.” Aristotle, Babbitt takes to be the
arch-type of the positive and critical Humanist, for Aristotle teaches
us “how to have standards and at the same time not to be immured in
dogma.” All this, as I say, is quite in the modern spirit and goes to
explain the appeal Babbitt exercises on many young Americans who cannot
be accused of yearning for some long-gone age and whose defection from
the banners of Menckenism has puzzled some people.

At this point should be brought up the Babbittean distinguishing
of three levels on which life may be experienced: the religious,
the humanistic, and the naturalistic. The naturalistic we have seen
exemplified by a host of Rousseauists and Baconians, of sentimentalists
and utilitarians, of all those who in one way or another submerge Man
into Nature. The religious level is occupied by the great systems of
Buddhism and Christianity. Here the supernatural enters, here the
consuming search is for the Absolute, here there is dying to this world
and here there is the life more abundant that comes through rebirth
into a world of greater consciousness. The _via media_ is alleged to
be the humanistic life, represented in the past by Confucianism in
the Orient and by Aristotelianism in the Occident. The middle way has,
as outlined by Babbitt, its special methodology, its special virtues,
and its special attitudes. For example, we have already noticed its
hostile attitude toward Naturalism, its clash with Naturalism on first
principles. On the other hand, its attitude toward those who work on
the religious plane is sympathetic. Quotations bearing on this could be
accumulated but a recent utterance by Irving Babbitt will do for all.
“For my own part,” he said in _Humanism and America_, “I range myself
unhesitatingly on the side of the supernaturalists. Though I see no
evidence that humanism is necessarily ineffective apart from dogmatic
and revealed religion, there is, it seems to me, evidence that it
gains immensely in effectiveness when it has a background of religious
insight.”

But to the New Humanist the reasons for risking one’s life on the
religious level do not, one judges, seem overwhelming. Here is one of
Babbitt’s statements on that point, a statement on which I reserve
comment for the present. “I have been struck,” he says cannily, “in
my study of the past by the endless self-deception to which man is
subject when he tries to pass too abruptly from the naturalistic to
the religious level. The world, it is hard to avoid concluding, would
have been a better place if more persons had made sure they were human
before setting out to be superhuman; and this consideration would seem
to apply with special force to a generation like the present that is
wallowing in the trough of naturalism. After all to be a good humanist
is merely to be moderate and sensible and decent. It is much easier
for a man to deceive himself and others regarding his supernatural
lights than it is regarding the degree to which he is moderate and
sensible and decent.”

Though Babbitt seems to emphasize here the fear of self-deception as
the main ground for preferring the humanistic plane to the religious,
the New Humanist can actually make out a very strong case based on hard
commonsense. A. R. Orage, the finest living English literary critic,
has suggested what the sensible attitude, in the present state of our
knowledge, should be toward religion. So interesting a point of view
justifies a rather lengthy quotation.

“Suppose that a remote posterity, unversed in mathematics and the
scientific research equipment of our age, should inherit one of our
current science manuals. There they would read or, rather decipher,
such statements as that light travels at the rate of 186,000 miles a
second; that the sun is 92 million miles distant from the earth, and
that the light of the nearest star takes four and a half light years to
reach us. What would they make of it all? Some of them, it is probable,
would hold that their forerunners must have possessed a faculty lost
to themselves and in consequence would attach a mystical significance
to the unverifiable dogmas; they might even repeat these dogmas as
possibly magical formulas. But undoubtedly the best common sense of
the day, in the absence of the means of verification, would dismiss
the statements as being childish guesses or, at best, as barbarous
abracadabra. Only a very few would suspect that perhaps we were not
such fools as we appeared, and give us suspended credit for a method
behind our madness. But our method itself and the instruments we employ
would be still to seek.

“The foregoing picture may serve to illustrate what may possibly--let
us say no more than possibly--be our own situation in regard to
the ancient ‘science’ of religion. We have inherited a few of the
textbooks once circulated among the illuminati of more or less
extinct civilizations, and we find them to contain statements of
equal exactitude and incredibility concerning things of which we have
no verifiable knowledge, as that there is a God Who is a trinity of
Persons, Who created the Universe according to Reason, and Man in
his own image, and Who placed us in the world with potentialities of
consciously becoming like unto Himself. Some of us today are disposed,
like our imagined descendants, to take these traditional statements
mystically; to repeat them as magical formulas; and to assume that
a lost faculty, the so-called religious sense, was possessed by our
ancestors of ancient Egypt, India, Persia and Syria. So relatively
powerful, in fact, are these that their attitude towards the inherited
dogmas of ancient religion is still the standard of respectability.
The weight of common sense, however, is slowly but surely making
itself felt; and the day is not far off when the intelligence of our
civilization will explicitly decline even to be interested in grandiose
statements apparently insusceptible of proof. Only a few, a very few,
will continue to suspect that perhaps the Egyptians, the Buddhists,
the Pythagoreans and the Gnostics were people very like ourselves in
respect of faculty and unlike us only in the same respect in which
we shall differ from a remote posterity without our science, namely,
in the possession, not of a lost faculty, but of a lost method or
technique. And for these few, too, the method or technique is still to
seek--or perhaps only to recognize.”

The foregoing is more than a caution against the easy specification
of what religious experience is; it also reinforces the attitude--a
strictly modern one which most of the New Humanists adopt--of wariness
toward the religious life. But can we find in our ordinary diurnal
living anything that corresponds to the humanistic way and to the
naturalistic way? Nothing is simpler than to exemplify the latter,
for we all live naturalistically most of the time. Most men, Thoreau
observed, lead lives of quiet desperation. Most men must work for
their livelihood, and the work they are driven to do holds little or
no attraction for them. (I do not speak of the increasing number of
intolerable jobs an industrial society creates, but rather of the
monotonous occupations of many members of the middle class.) The
time spent in offices each day is for many men so much time spent in
bondage, cut off from their vivid interests: they repine, but succumb
to a dull routine. Their permanent mood is one of passive endurance.
Like horses they uncomplainingly draw their loads and rejoice when at
five o’clock they are let out to pasture. This is a usual and mild
instance of merely naturalistic living. The humanistic attitude, on
the other hand, is, toward the identical necessity and circumstances,
an active one. A curb is put on futile repining: vain longings
are disciplined: the situation is faced with the minimum useless
feeling. The question for the Humanist is, granting the inescapable
circumstances, how can I make the best use of them to my advantage as
a human being? How may I bend them to suit my design in life, how may
I gather them into focus? How may I extract from my burdens values
that are not accessible to the mere beast of burden? At this point
Arnold’s triumphant converting of his drab job of school inspecting
to the main interests of his life springs to mind as an instance
of working on the humanistic level. In other words, where it is a
case of the disagreeable inevitability, the Naturalist remains under
compulsion from the outside: the Humanist, in contrast, makes an effort
to alter his attitude from mere negative endurance to a search for
positive results in the way of personal discipline and the discovery
of unsuspected values inherent in the day’s work. Is his real business
in life not the cultivation of the distinctively human, and is not
everything grist to that magical mill if one only tries to keep the
mill in operation; that is, if one maintains an active attitude toward
experience?

But, perhaps some readers are saying, you are merely balancing an
act of good sense against the case of unintelligent adjustment:
by what right do you label the former humanistic when many avowed
Naturalists--Charles Darwin in his years of invalidism, for
instance--perform feats of intelligent and heroic overcoming of
difficulties? The answer is that the Naturalist often rises above his
doctrine just as the Humanist often fails in his discipline, but it
still remains true that it is the Humanist who assigns himself the
task of organizing a life based on good sense, while the Naturalist
puts his confidence in following, so far as he is permitted by
events, the grain of his mechanical self. Have we not inherited the
nineteenth century Romantic disparagement of good sense in favor of the
imagination?

 * * * * *

The central maxim for practising the humanistic life is a
crystallization in three words of a large part of Greek
wisdom--“Nothing too much.” The New Humanists plan to do not less than
to restore to its pristine attractiveness the law of measure extolled
by Aristotle and called by Horace--so worthy of praise did it seem to
that exquisite poet and epicurean--the Golden Mean.

About this I think there is no difficulty in understanding what the New
Humanist leaders are driving at. The law of measure is not legalistic;
it cannot be codified. Negatively put, it means, avoid excesses.
Positively, it means the rule of temperance, but temperance manifested
in the mental and emotional realms as well as in the physical. The
real difficulty lies in applying measure to our deeds, our thoughts,
our feelings, and it is an immense difficulty, demanding what tact and
delicacy, what perceptiveness and reflection, what resolution and zeal.
In essays by American Humanists we often run upon certain sayings of
Pascal which not only illuminate the import of the law of measure but
should serve here to enhance the reader’s sense of what I have just
said about its difficulty in operation.

“Grandeur is not shown by being at one extremity, but in touching both
at once, and filling the whole space between,” wrote Pascal, and there
could not possibly be a better recognition of the _great scope_ for
the play of human psychology allowed for by the law of measure. “When
we would pursue the virtues to their extremes on either side, vices
present themselves, which insinuate themselves imperceptibly there,
in their insensible course towards the infinitely great, so that we
lose ourselves in vices, and no longer see virtues.” The history of
Romanticism (how often!) testifies to the weight of truth in this
saying. The following _pensée_ might well be called the essence of the
New Humanism. “Excessive or deficient mental powers are alike accused
of madness. Nothing is good save mediocrity. To leave the mean is to
leave humanity. The greatness of the human soul consists in knowing how
to keep the mean. So little is it the case that greatness consists in
leaving it, that it lies in not leaving it.” That is the reply the New
Humanist can make when it is objected that his strictures on eccentric
genius are too harsh. “The greatness of the human soul consists in
knowing how to keep the mean.”

For a genius is often simply an exaggeration of one, and but one, of
the three major aspects of a man. For we may say that man is a compound
of Knowing, Being and Doing. Exaggerate his wish to know into a lust
for knowledge (_libido sciendi_), depress at the same time his wish for
Being and Doing, and you get the Gargantuan hungerer for knowledge, the
monstrous head and the starved heart and impotent faculty for doing,
you get an encyclopædist, a Diderot (whose heart was active, however)
or a Herbert Spencer. Again exaggerate a man’s wish for Being, let him
despise the acquisition of knowledge and the doing of deeds, and you
have the lust for feeling (_libido sentiendi_): you have a genius like
Rousseau or Schiller. There is also an inordinate lust for power, for
doing (_libido dominandi_), which like the lusts for knowledge and
for feeling is all-consuming and warps the development of character,
and this time you get a monstrous man of action like Napoleon or like
some great industrialists of the present era that any one can name.
These incarnations of the three great lusts of human nature excite our
wonder and admiration: they fascinate us by their “divine madness.”
But we remind ourselves that there is also a “divine sanity,” and it
seems in the character of Plato or Buddha a finer touch of divinity.
Babbitt transcribes for us in his argument directed against the _libido
sciendi_, the _libido sentiendi_, and the _libido dominandi_ the
Buddhist proverb: “If one man conquer in battle ten thousand men and
another man conquer his own self, he is the greatest of conquerors,”
and to this he subjoins his own belief that “that man is most human
who can check his faculty, even if it be his master-faculty, and his
passion, even his ruling passion, in its mid-career, and temper it by
its opposite.”

In one phrase, then, the Humanist method is mediation between extremes.
It is the finding of the middle way, and I hasten to add that the
middle way is never found mechanically, never by merely striking an
average between two extremes. It is a strait and narrow way, obvious
when it is recognized, but hard to recognize. Finding it is a call
upon all of one’s experience: effort, thought, observation, a scale of
values, spiritual agility, all are needed to make the discovery of the
Golden Path.

 * * * * *

But useless is all writing about the Golden Mean if man has no power
of choice, or potentiality of acquiring a power of choice. As well
exhort a beggar to buy a palace! Nothing could be vainer than the
formulation of a discipline based on selection, selection and again
selection, if there is in man no capability for selecting. According
to the Mechanists there is not. They have much truth on their side.
In the sense that man is self-ignorant and therefore uncontrolled
(for who can control what he does not know?), man _is_ a machine. But
here is something to ponder on: there is a wide uninvestigated region
of man which we may term his potential psychology. Psychology as a
science--described truly by its leaders as an infant science--has not
yet given us a norm for human psychology, which includes both our
actualities and our latencies. An honest worker like Dr. J. B. Watson
speaks frankly for all his profession when he says that we have seen
no norm, and adds that in his opinion it would be infinitely desirable
to find one. The New Humanist, weighing the evidence and testimony of
the past, concludes that man has at least the potentialities of gaining
self-knowledge and self-control. He therefore seeks to actualize
them. He challenges the Mechanists and appeals to our sense of latent
psychology. In his effort to build up a working system of psychology
based upon potentialities (our human self) as well as upon actualities
(our mechanical self), the New Humanist has been more grotesquely
misunderstood than at any other point.

We approach now the meaning of the phrases he uses so often,--“a
principle of restraint,” “a power of vital control,” “_frein vital_”
as against “_élan vital_,” “the inner check.” I have been through the
mass of writings directed against Babbitt’s theory, and these terms
are harped on with so much irritation that I can only conclude the
vocabulary of Babbitt is highly offensive to our age. “Now why the
deuce,” peevishly writes one of the younger critics, speaking for
all of them, “is virtue, with the Humanists, always made to reside
exclusively in what Babbitt calls the ‘will to refrain’?” In the
petulance of that question there is perhaps a clue to the psychology
of our age, a psychology so extraordinarily blind that it fails to see
that a principle of restraint is only another way of saying a principle
of selection, as indeed in Babbitt’s writings the two expressions are
interchangeable from his first book wherein he spoke of “a humane
principle of selection, or what amounts to the same thing, a true
principle of restraint.” But a very vocal part of our age is infuriated
by the very notion of restraint, and we have to interpret this strange
fact. Can it be that, as is sometimes charged, this is really an age
of weaklings in the arts and philosophies? It is noticeable that men
of strength are usually the very men who most feel the need of curbing
themselves, that men of rich and powerful personalities are the ones
who try hardest to purge themselves of the merely gratuitous and
“temperamental,” that men of real passion are the first to acknowledge
need of canalizing it. These men are not afraid that a little
discipline will take away their precious personalities: they have so
much personality that it is precisely discipline they look for. But
what is this outcry on the part of so many of our formative writers
against the negative admonition? What feebleness of energy and passion
is it that is afraid of being strangled by a “power of vital control”?
I am driven to think of the reports of sexologists who say that it is
the poorly sexed man whose erotic life is one of excessive indulgence,
whereas the powerfully sexed are sensible of the need for some
moderation of their desires, and I wonder if many a writer in our day,
determined to let nothing stop the expression of his soul (the size of
an emmet, alas, in so many cases) is not comparable to the unrestrained
man of poor sexual powers.

However that may be, the American intellectuals and artists as a body
have totally failed to perceive what may be fairly termed the gallantry
of the Humanist conception of “a will to refrain.” For if we could, as
it were, put our ordinary selves under a glass and gaze coolly at them,
we should see very numerous and complicated phenomena, a flux subject
to endless change and relativity, which except for an infinitesimal
fraction we are not aware of at all. This world of impressions, and
bodily changes, and variations in feeling goes its own way, and we
are, it seems, at its mercy. Have most of us any more control of our
mental processes than we have of our glandular secretions? But against
this imperious self the Humanist dares to assert his very deepest
self. He dares to say no to its impulsions. He dares to say, though
these phenomena _arise_ in me, I yet shall deal with these arisings,
denying some and permitting others to run their natural course. And
it is this assertion that I call gallant. The odds are all against
the assertion being made good: the attraction of the ordinary self is
almost irresistible; and yet man will struggle to assert his freedom of
the ebb and flow of his ordinary life. In positive language, the “will
to refrain” is an attempt to strike a blow for freedom.

The principle of restraint is dramatized by Babbitt when he calls it
the inner check. It is less metaphorical than many terms appropriated
by, let us say, psycho-analysts who speak of a “censor,” for example,
but still it is only a metaphor. What does it stand for? I find it very
difficult to say, for it seems to be on the outskirts of a tremendous
conception, a conception that is excessively hard to put into words.
Many opponents of American Humanism have thought the inner check to
be merely an inhibition, or some streak of puritanism, or in one case
an equivalent to what the psycho-analyst means by the censor. As if
More and Babbitt were naïve men of limited experience! As if, though
trained in skepticism for a lifetime, they would make an error that any
fledgeling reviewer could see! No; the meaning of the inner check must
be sought in a deeper way if it is to be elicited, and in plumbing for
it we must first of all divest ourselves of our usual notions about
inhibitions.

Both Babbitt and More have, as a matter of fact, clearly indicated
that they held no ordinary conception when they spoke of the inner
check.[6] The phrase is probably Babbitt’s, who derived it from Emerson
in whose writings it appeared as the inward check; the inward check was
referred by Emerson to Hindu scriptures where the meaning was an inward
guide or control. This in itself is sufficient to put us on guard
against a quick assumption that we understand what it is all about.
But there is another reason, equally strong: Babbitt has several times
linked up his sense of an inner check with the dæmon of Socrates, who
is made to say in the _Phædrus_: “My divine monitor’s wonted sign--now
it never occurs save to deter me from something or other I am intending
to do.” This is why I said above that the More-Babbitt conception of
the inner check appears to me to be on the fringe of a tremendous
conception, for I have not the effrontery to regard Socrates as naïve
in matters of his own psychology. At the same time, what was meant by
the ancient idea of a deterring monitor in one’s breast and what is now
meant by the Babbitt-More derivation? The difficulties of an answer are
at once visible.

Something like this may be modestly ventured, however. Neither
ancient nor modern had or has in mind, when speaking of a dæmon or an
inner check, what we may call a sociologically formed conscience. By
education we have all had instilled in us a kind of pseudo-conscience:
in our childhood and youth older people told us definitely what was
right and wrong, they traded on our susceptibility, and now that we
have grown critical, we learn that these deeply stamped notions of
wrong and right are purely relative, often silly, and in some instances
downright harmful. Take anything you please--our sense of sexual
shame, for one--and any intelligent person today can riddle much of it
by simply showing how arbitrarily we have been conditioned. So much
depends on social custom! But the inner check and the Socratic dæmon
are not, I feel confident, the products of respectable training.

For, to continue with the case of sexual shame, is there not a true
shame as well as a pseudo-shame? Is there not a shame so deep, so
primitive, that it seems biological, owing nothing to sociology, a
shame that when it is violated seems to overthrow the whole being?
Pseudo-shame can be almost painlessly removed, but this organic
shame, if corrupted, is corrupted only by a violent wrench to one’s
whole character. It is as though something died in one, whereas when
sociological shame is taken away, one feels liberated and even gay.

By my line of reasoning, the inner check conception is to be taken as
the faint shadow cast down the ages by the Socratic dæmon, and this
dæmon in turn I am half-guessing to be a figure of Conscience. But not
conscience in our sense, the pseudo-function that Society sets going
in us, but--how shall I put it?--some abiding developed sense of how it
is fitting that the being Man should conduct himself in a life that may
be a school of experience, but certainly reveals no easily accessible
master of the school.

For fluid as is the nature of man--and few recent writers have better
displayed man’s fluidity than Joseph Wood Krutch in an essay entitled
_The Paradox of Humanism_--there does yet seem to be buried in the
creature something, almost non-functioning, which we may agree to call
a conscience, since it is independent of social conditioning, and this
something, this conscience, sometimes says no in emphatic command. Who
is there who, on sincere self-examination, does not find there are
a few things he knows with absolute certitude he could never do? No
power on earth, no conceivable circumstances, he declares to himself,
would make me do thus-and-so. And yet why not do them? There may be no
consequences that inspire fear, there may even be strong incentives to
take the detested course of action. But something internal says Veto so
powerfully that there is no possibility of committing the action. _The
action would seem like a betrayal of the species one belongs to!_

The interpretation is here offered then that the inner check works in
the interests of some essential humanity in us. It seeks to counteract
that fluidity of our nature whereby men become beasts or go mad. It
warns us when we are about to desert our adult human standards and
retrogress to the puerile or infantile or sub-human.

And now I think of the life of Socrates as we have it: the simple
dignity, the steadiness of his passion for self-perfection, the
courage of his behavior, and all the multitude of other points of
homage the world has paid him. And I see his life as a drama of the
true conscience: in his illustrious example, a conscience developed,
sensitized, responsive, a marvellous monitor surely directing him as to
how it was fit that a member of the human species should live.

 * * * * *

The technique of the New Humanism, to sum it up, is mediation under the
guidance of the vestiges of true conscience still in our psychology.
It is as simple as that, and, I am tempted to add, as tremendous as
that. To repeat Pascal’s profound words: “To leave the mean is to leave
humanity.” The inner check or as I take it the vestige of conscience
in modern man warns us when we veer toward barbarous extremes. The
activity of the inner check is the very heart of the method, but
dependent from the principal technique are several lesser techniques
which may now be taken up rapidly and schematically. The New Humanist
calls them (a) the right use of illusion, (b) the imitation of nature,
and (c) decorum.

(a). The Right Use of Illusion. The quotations are from Babbitt’s
_Rousseau and Romanticism_. “Life does not here give an element of
oneness and there an element of change. It gives a _oneness that is
always changing_. The oneness and the change are inseparable. Now if
what is stable and permanent is felt as real, the side of life that
is always slipping over into something else or vanishing away entirely
is, as every student of psychology knows, associated rather with the
feeling of illusion ... The problem of the One and the Many, the
ultimate problem of thought, can therefore be solved only by a right
use of illusion ... failure (with the problem of the One and the Many)
can be retrieved only by a deeper insight into the imagination and its
all-important rôle in both literature and life.”

In the above I think we may detect the hand of Joubert who reflected
deeply on _illusion et sagesse_. “Joubert,” Paul Elmer More says,
“conceives it to be the rôle of the imagination, mediating as it does
between sense and reason, to lend its magic and glamour to the latter,
to throw as it were a veil of divine illusion over some essential
truth.... The imagination must be really free and spontaneous, and the
truth itself must not be too precisely formulated, if we are to arrive
at that vital fusing of illusion and insight with the accompanying
sense of infinitude that is found in the true symbol.”

In the ideal Humanist then the Imagination coöperates with Reason. Its
rôle is not debased in order to exalt good sense as happened in the
Neo-Classicism of the eighteenth century, nor is it made antagonistic
to good sense as was done in the romantic recoil from Pope and other
eighteenth century men. Something like the play of Imagination in
