behalf of Reason as we behold it in Plato is what Babbitt and More
contend is the Imagination’s highest function.

(b). The Imitation of Nature. “The first point to observe,” notes
Babbitt, “is that according to Aristotle one is to get his general
nature not on authority or second hand, but is to disengage it directly
for himself from the jungle of particulars that he has before his eyes.
He is not, says Aristotle, to imitate things as they are, but as they
ought to be. Thus conceived, imitation is a creative act. Through all
the welter of the actual one penetrates to the real and so succeeds
without ceasing to be individual in suggesting the universal.” The
consideration above is not purely literary. The Humanist with his
practical attitude toward the leading of “the good life” tries to form
a model before his eyes of what normal representative human nature is
and to imitate that. Out of human nature as it actually is he tries
to make a general picture of what it ought to be, and to hold that
up as a guide for his efforts to become more representative in his
own person. Since many people apparently find it hard to receive much
meaning from such abstract statements as the preceding, since they
are not at once and vividly conscious of the vast difference between
a standard of human nature and the average of human nature, I may be
pardoned for urgently recommending a close study of Charles Maurras’s
explicit account of how the normal (or perfect) man appears to his mind
and imagination. It is hard to over-estimate the importance of such an
attempt as Maurras’s. Classicism, if it is to be a life, must be more
than a set of ideas and a discipline for actions: it must rally the
emotions and make use of their great driving force. It happens that
our emotions can be appealed to by images, and hence the desirability
of forming as concrete an image as possible of what the classical
man, man _par excellence_, is like. In his _Prologue to an Essay on
Criticism_, Maurras has said: “Good taste is the taste of the perfect
man.” He then goes on:

“There is such a thing as a perfect man. I mean that it is not
difficult to conceive a man who should fulfil exactly the idea of our
common condition and who could serve as a model for all of us. I hope
I shall be excused for not bestowing upon this fictitious creature
(fictitious but true) the nickname of _Beau Idéal_: that name crops up
whenever people want to talk German or abandon a subject in favor of a
vague terminology. People who argue about the ideal do not mean by that
the total of the characteristics that build up and complete a being,
but, as they say in their jargon, something over and above. The perfect
man fulfils the definition of man, the ideal man deforms it here and
there; I hasten to add that in the ideal man excesses are balanced by
defects, and extravagances by omissions.

“By the perfect man I do not mean a mediocre figure in which virtues
and faults are balanced. I am thinking of the limit of human power.
Monsters and prodigies do not exceed that limit, as is commonly said.
They are very well within it, like the mediocrities. Man conceives
nothing beyond that limit, beyond which live the gods.

“In a few words, man is a reasoning animal. This old definition seems
to me the only satisfactory one. Neither morality, nor sociability,
nor, to be sure, feeling is peculiar to man. He is distinguished only
by reason; that is what distinguishes him, without separating him,
from the rest of creation. Created nature is completely represented
in him. In his body which has weight, number and measure as have the
metals, organization like the plants, sensibility and movement like
the animals, and which thus appears as the achievement of our earth.
His reason is incessantly nourished, sharpened, enlivened, illuminated
by the tribute which the world pays him through these three channels.
Reason, thus conditioned by the whole of nature, must develop the
fulness of its energies in the exact measure in which that can work
no ill to the perfect expansion of a flourishing body; reason pushed
to the point of parching the animal would exhaust its own sources of
being; as for the exclusive culture of the body, that would coarsen the
soul and deprive it of reason. So the most subtle of the Athenians used
to drain bowl after bowl of wine without confusing his brain, and would
then reason like a god. His companions admired him all the more.

“To express the natural harmony of these two powers, we commonly use
a very vicious figure of speech: we speak of ‘equilibrium’ as if the
objects on the scale were equivalent! _Order_ is what we should call
this conformity of a being to all the elements of its fate.

“What should be the critical sense of a human being composed in
such a way that his instincts, his impressions, his feelings, his
passions, when brought to the highest point, are guided and ruled by
an intellect more powerful, itself fortified, sustained and nourished
by the abundance of nature? His animal ardors will never usurp the
seat of control which belongs exclusively to judgment and reason; but
will reason abandon a nature which is too robust, in order to follow
vain imaginations? No dessication, no flaccidity either; forms full
and well-nerved; philosopher and on occasion even metaphysician, but
rigorously; a sense of real and true poetry; infused with all human
warmth, but firm and secure against it if need be: such is the general
idea that is possible of the good taste of an accomplished man.”

I do not say I regard the above as the best depiction of the perfect
man to be encountered, but Maurras indubitably proceeds along the
right lines, using his imagination to catch a glimpse of the universal
and at the same time not sacrificing his own individuality. He has
succeeded in visualizing a model with fair claims to normality,
representativeness, soundness, for his own personal imitation or
emulation. Note that the logic by which Maurras has made his conception
is the logic of probability, commended so often in the New Humanist
books, and not simply a flight into the improbable.

(c). Decorum. What a fate has befallen this noble word! It is, in my
opinion, impossible for the New Humanist to raise it from its low
estate. Babbitt has exposed the way in which in the eighteenth century
it came to mean etiquette: as he well says, the Neo-Classical writers
made the bad mistake of confusing the language of the nobility with
the nobility of language. This blunder Wordsworth recoiled from in
his advocacy and use of plain diction: the Romantics hated decorum
because it had become a stiff and formal and external affair. But worse
degradation awaited the word, and at the present time in America it
seems to mean, so far as I can judge from the writers who object to it,
the “genteel” manners, the prudish code of behavior which it is the
fashion to attribute to the past generation. As I think, the word is
beyond rescue: it is drowned in its present debased associations. But
is that an excuse for those whose profession it is to read critically
to charge classical scholars like More and Babbitt with attaching
merely a “genteel” significance to the term?

How could Babbitt, for instance, have made it plainer than he did in
the next passage I shall quote that decorum for him does not mean
empty formalism, or a mere veneer of manners, but is inward and to be
understood in a large way? I resort to italics, for truly they seem
necessary to combat our habitual associations with the word. “Whoever
has succeeded in bridging the gap between the general percept and some
particular emergency has to that extent achieved the fitting and the
decorous. _Decorum is simply the law of measure in its more concrete
aspects._ For every type of humanist decorum is, in Milton’s phrase,
the ‘grand masterpiece to observe.’ _Actually this observation may rest
on deep insight_, as it did in the case of Milton himself, or it may
degenerate into empty formalism. The adjustment of which I have spoken
between the variable and the permanent elements in human experience
requires _spiritual_ effort and most men are spiritually indolent. For
genuine adjustment they tend to substitute outer conformity so that
decorum itself finally comes to seem a mere veneer, something that has
no deep root in things. Moreover the notions of decent behavior to
which men have conformed at any particular period have always been
more or less local and relative. It is easy to take the next step and
assume that they have been _only_ local and relative, an assumption
subversive not merely of decorum but of humanism itself.”

But in spite of Babbitt’s care in expressing his meaning, he has been
constantly defeated by his readers’ different sense of the term, and
not even his using the phrase “a sense of proportion” as equivalent to
decorum has enlightened most of them. Yet all that decorum means is the
activity of our sense of proportion: it means the carrying out of what
it is seemly for a man to do. It is not seemly for a man to whine, we
say: not seemly for him to shirk the offices of the mind: not seemly
to be an utter fool in practical affairs. Notions of fitting behavior
vary from place to place and time to time, as Babbitt observes, but
above all the varying notions there remains a permanent sense of how it
is appropriate for a man to live. He should face the facts: he should
put away childish things: he should deal with the difficulties of
existence intelligently, courageously, forcefully: he should realize
his innermost being. The indecorous is simply the inappropriate, the
disproportioned, whether it occurs in art or in conduct. Bathos is
indecorous: sentimentality, the gush of emotion far in excess of the
fact, is indecorous: the intrusion of the purely private into public
discussion is indecorous. The things named are either inappropriate or
unmeasured.

To close the subject: Decorum, or a sense of proportion, is simply the
law of measure in its more concrete aspects, it is the law of measure
in actual operation in the shifting tangle of daily existence.

 * * * * *

In describing the discipline worked out by the New Humanists I have
striven for extreme simplicity of statement. My aim has been to
concretize as much as possible the technical formulations in Humanist
literature. I was moved to do this, because I have sometimes read
that the More-Babbitt doctrine and discipline are academic in the bad
sense of the word. Babbitt and More advocate, it is said, notions that
are narrow and have a freezing effect on the “spirit.” They are, most
damning accusation, puritanical. But what, when correctly understood,
is evilly academic about their conception of man and the means they
advocate for true self-fulfilment? What is narrow about their ideas
when they are distinguished at the source and not “interpreted” by some
overworked journalist? How can the law of measure possibly freeze up
the springs of personality, if one has any personality to begin with?
And are they puritanical? I cite Babbitt’s critical position toward
Puritanism for a sufficient reply.

“The crucial point in any case is one’s attitude toward the principle
of control. Those who stand for this principle in any form or degree
are dismissed by the emancipated as reactionaries or, still graver
reproach, as Puritans. Mr. Mencken would have us believe that the
historical Puritan was not even sincere in his moral rigorism, but
was given to ‘lamentable transactions with loose women and fiery
jugs.’ This may serve as a sample of the assertions, picturesquely
indiscriminate, by which a writer wins immediate notoriety at the
expense of his permanent reputation. The facts about the Puritan happen
to be complex and need to be dealt with very Socratically. It has been
affirmed that the point of view of the Puritan was Stoical rather than
truly Christian, and the affirmation is not wholly false. The present
discussion of the relationship between Puritanism and the rise of
capitalism with its glorification of the acquisitive life also has its
justification. It is likewise a fact that the Puritan was from the
outset unduly concerned with reforming others as well as himself, and
this trait relates him to the humanitarian meddler or ‘wowser’ of the
present day, who is Mr. Mencken’s pet aversion.

“Yet it remains true that awe and reverence and humility are Christian
virtues and that there was some survival of these virtues in the
Puritan. For a representative Puritan like Jonathan Edwards they were
inseparable from the illumination of grace, from what he terms ‘a
divine and supernatural light.’ In the passage from the love and fear
of God of an Edwards to the love and service of man professed by the
humanitarian, something has plainly dropped out, something that is very
near the center. What has tended to disappear is the inner life with
the special type of control it imposes. With the decline of this inner
control there has been an increasing resort to outer control. Instead
of the genuine Puritan we then have the humanitarian legalist who
passes innumerable laws for the control of people who refuse to control
themselves. The activity of our uplifters is scarcely suggestive of
any ‘divine and supernatural light.’ Here is a discrimination of the
first importance that has been obscured by the muddy thinking of our
half-baked intelligentsia. One is thus kept from perceiving the real
problem, which is to retain the inner life, even though one refuse to
accept the theological nightmare with which the Puritan associated it.
More is involved in the failure to solve this problem than the Puritan
tradition. It is the failure of our contemporary life in general. Yet,
unless some solution is reached by a full and free exercise of the
critical spirit, one remains a mere modernist and not a thoroughgoing
and complete modern; for the modern spirit and the critical spirit are
in their essence one.”

To me it is a jest at the expense of the last decade that while so
many of us have been marching behind the drums of Menckenism, round
and round the same old circle of naturalistic ideas, there has issued
forth from the despised quarter of university thought the most truly
modern system of all, a system far more in accord with the spirit of
Goethe, who spoke, says Babbitt, not as a Puritan but as a clear-eyed
man of the world, than all the humanitarian and romantic panaceas that
are so solemnly discussed in our liberal weeklies. Goethe said that
everything that liberates the spirit without a corresponding growth in
self-mastery is pernicious.

 * * * * *

As a matter of fact, the New Humanist like the historical Humanist
revolts against Calvinism. But he has hit upon an interesting
substitution for the Christian dogma of Original Sin or Depravity.
Most attempts men have made at an objective critique of the human race
have wound up in the conclusion that there is something radically wrong
with it, that there is a Moby Dick loose in the ocean of humanity which
must be hunted down and captured before the race can achieve peace. It
is as though we all existed under some curse, as though we had been
cheated of some birthright. Take the opening pages of _Walden_. Thoreau
is trying for an objective view of mankind. He finds fault after fault
sweeping through the race. He finds it indeed a very odd race, doing
penance in a thousand singular ways for existence. Why is it thus? He
is led to make one master indictment of human beings:--they are torpid!
The humanistic view almost coincides, it amounts to saying the main
trouble with mankind is torpor. Original Indolence in place of Original
Sin! The criticism is reinforced by Buddhistic teachings. For the
doctrine of original depravity to Babbitt is not thoroughly acceptable:
it rests on rather metaphysical grounds and seems hard to verify
practically. The Buddhist doctrine of the original laziness of human
nature, however, appears to come closer to the ascertainable facts.
“The greatest of vices according to Buddha is the lazy yielding to the
impulses of temperament (_pamāda_); the greatest virtue (_appamāda_)
is the opposite of this, the awakening from the sloth and lethargy of
the senses, the constant exercise of the active will.” By this stroke
Babbitt once again frees his doctrine of theology, and as always makes
his appeal to verifiable experience. The New Humanism, to offer still
another definition, is a warfare on man’s original indolence. We may
be said to be born with a “won’t”: the Humanist tries to oppose a
“will” to this “won’t.”

What the New Humanism has to say about the Will is another borrowing
from Eastern philosophy. The Will is regarded as anterior and superior
to the Intellect: to it and not to the Intellect as in St. Thomas
Aquinas is given the primacy. But you will find all this treated
exhaustively in the works of Babbitt, More and their followers.

What I wish to say is that the problem of the Will is the very
crux-problem of all our civilization. I am convinced that Babbitt
has put the supreme question to our times, and that all debate over
American Humanism is beside the main point until that question is
fully faced. “Here at all events is the issue on which all other
issues finally hinge; for until the question of moral freedom--the
question whether man is a responsible agent or only the plaything of
his impulses and impressions--is decided, nothing is decided; and to
decide the question under existing circumstances calls for the keenest
critical discrimination.”

But how uncritical and impractical we are about this problem! Vague
and futile! If anything is plain, it is that the Will presents first
a psychological problem and only after that a metaphysical question.
But we argue and theorize endlessly about the Will. Hardly ever do
we think of a practical self-demonstration, and if we do it is some
such ineffably silly demonstration as saying, “I _will_ to open this
window.” But did you will to move all the muscles that shifted when you
did so, including the numerous unnecessary movements that accompanied
the action? The truth is that Will can only begin where Behaviorism
leaves off, but very few have digested Behaviorism. Why do we not howl
with laughter when we read in William James: “My first act of free will
shall be to believe in free will”? Or take this piece of naïveté upon
which my eye has fallen while writing this chapter. A young critic
is telling his audience that “the philosophic implications of the
quantum theory, the writings of Whitehead, Eddington, C. J. Herrick
and Millikan, and the spectacle of American artists recovering from
paralysis of the will, had all combined to revive a sense of free will
in certain Americans.” It may be a sense of free will, but if it was
arrived at by these means, by reading, we may be positive that it is an
illusory sense. As if the same critic had listed a number of authors
who had proved to him that he had what he was not previously sure of,
a mind. We say we have minds because they demonstrate themselves to
us beyond any possible doubt. If we indeed have Wills, they also will
be self-demonstrating: no one need prove to us we have what we cannot
doubt we possess. Where there is doubt, there is no Will. Where there
is no doubt, let it be added at once there still may be no Will.

One thing we know: Will is not to be associated, as is often done, with
desire. Our desires come and go and fight among themselves: we commonly
act, not on _a_ desire, but on the resultant of our various contending
desires. But Will, in the very nature of the case, is distinct from and
independent of desire. Not in the region of desires but of efforts
should we look for the roots of Will. On this the New Humanist is
absolutely clear-headed.

And perhaps there we had better leave the discussion for the present.
Making effort is always unmistakable to us. It has a psychological
taste, if I may say so, so strong that we cannot delude ourselves
into thinking we are not engaged in effort. Our desires on the other
hand have quite another taste, and many times as is well known we are
only dimly conscious of them. The use of Will then could never be
unconscious, could never be a mere matter of believing theories. Will
would make itself felt in the psychological realm as indubitably as our
bodies feel kinæsthetic strain in hauling a heavy boat up on shore.

Everything in American Humanism depends ultimately upon the Will as an
undeniable fact, not as an assumption. We may start with the assumption
but unless we can convert it into a fact for ourselves as patent as the
fingers on our hands we are playing with an illusion.

There is a practical problem of the Will: a practical approach to
it: and perhaps a practical solution. The New Humanists project this
key problem to the place where it belongs: in the front of all our
dilemmas and puzzles as to how to lead “the good life.” Should we all
try seriously to work at the key problem, there would occur in American
life a revolution that would be unimaginable.

 * * * * *

The New Humanist not only associates Will with effort, but--what is a
great advance over his opponents’ muddled notions about Will--with
a particular type of effort: work on oneself, a work that takes the
form of knowing oneself, harmonizing oneself, disciplining oneself.
And in this lies the secret of the devotees of Humanism. It is,
however, an open secret, older than Aristotle and still advertised
today. It is that happiness comes not from psychological inertia but
from psychological work. Once again I shall quote from Babbitt: “this
sluggishness and slackness of spirit, this mere drifting and abdication
of will, may, as Chaucer’s parson suggests, be the crime against the
Holy Ghost itself.” The agreement is with Aristotle who conceived “of
happiness in terms of work. All types of temperamentalists are from
the human point of view passive. The happiness that they crave is a
passive happiness. A man may pursue power with the energy of a Napoleon
and yet remain ethically passive. He may expand his sympathies until,
like Schiller, he is ready to ‘bestow a kiss upon the whole world,’
and yet remain ethically passive. A man ceases to be ethically passive
only when he begins to work in the Aristotelian sense, that is when he
begins to put the brake on temperament and impulse, and in the same
degree he tends to become ethically efficient.”

And now let one of the ancients, “the subtlest of the Athenians,” the
hero of the Platonic Dialogues, have the final word. “The best man is
he who most tries to perfect himself, and the happiest man is he who
most feels that he _is_ perfecting himself.”

The reader may think it strange that such a doctrine and discipline as
we have been sketching should arouse the violent and oftentimes abusive
antagonism that flared up in the winter and spring of 1930. Whatever
its ultimate deficiencies may be, the New Humanism wears commonsense on
its face, and its thought is both broad and elevated. Nevertheless it
provoked a storm.

It is explicable. In the first place, much random opposition to
American Humanism springs up because of the specific attitudes
Humanists take in respect to art, literature, science, education,
politics, and so forth. Stemming from the parent attitude of
responsibility toward the natural self are a variety of consequential
attitudes when the Humanist looks out upon the world around him. And it
is these consequential (or possibly only contingent) attitudes which
have caused some individuals to hurl their thunderbolts. An admirer
of the cumbersome novels of Theodore Dreiser, let us say, reads some
detached article by a New Humanist in which his hero is held up as an
exemplar of “romanticism going on all fours”: he inquires no further
into the basis of American Humanism but immediately rushes to the
defence of Dreiser and current American literature. And so with the
advocate of Professor John Dewey’s educational ideas. A criticism by
a Humanist of these ideas is sufficient to incur the hostility of the
disciple of Dewey: the system of the attacker on Dewey is of no moment,
for his ideas on education are objectionable and so must be his whole
outlook. There have been many such quarrels over specific opinions,
which have naturally led to a condemnation without examination of
Humanism itself. The New Humanists work out their critique of modern
life on many fronts. They advance a new-old program on the same number
of fronts. It is easy to see then how, being comprehensive, they manage
to stir up so many enemies. In the next three chapters I shall examine
the Humanist attitude toward modern science, educational impressionism,
and politics, and no doubt the reader will then easily comprehend why
the New Humanism has provoked so much heated debate.

But in the second place there is a far more important reason why the
reception of Humanism has been acrimonious beyond any precedent in
American literary history. _It is because American Humanism dares to
question the unconscious assumptions of our age._ It is well known
that if you touch a man in his pocketbook, you frequently get a sudden
dramatic agitated revelation of his psychology. Or touch a person in
his sex life, and again there is the parting of the customary veils
over his behavior: you see vividly the flashing of the person’s
psychological forces. There is a third way: instead of asking for a
loan of money, or tossing a rabelaisian joke at an acquaintance, toss
an idea at him that goes beneath his customary thoughts to strike at
his latent assumptions, the thoughts he was unaware of, upon which,
nevertheless, all his opinions come to rest. _Then_ he will become
active: he jumps as if a hypodermic needle had been applied to his
mind: he bristles with defences against this idea that has irritated
his mind at its very base.

You are attacking the context of things he did not know he believed,
but instinctively he knows now that if they are threatened, all
his opinions are threatened. For it is _through_ these unconscious
assumptions that he has seen everything else. No wonder he rallies to
their defence! No wonder he strikes back! It is the very quick of his
mind you have touched.

American Humanism is a concerted assault not so much on what our age
has assumed knowingly but on what it has unconsciously assumed. That
is why it has been and will be violently opposed, fought with every
weapon, shouted at and shouted down, if possible. The outcome may be
within a decade the complete submergence of the Humanist movement.
In that event so much the worse for an age that cannot be brought to
criticize its unknown postulates once the light has been let in on them.

 VIII

 SCIENCE AND THE MODERN MAN

  _“Science has its legitimate pretensions to power; but false claims
  are now being advanced on all sides under the shelter of its name,
  and it is these false claims which the humanist is concerned to
  expose.”_--Louis Trenchard More in _Humanism and America_.


Do you wish a vivid proof that American Humanism probes through the
crystallized portions of the modern mentality and wounds its quick
nervous base? Then consider the intense annoyance created among so many
modernists by the temperate criticism the classical party makes of the
current prestige of science. The New Humanist attempts only to moderate
the very great esteem of our time for science. Yet pained outcries
on every hand greet this effort at moderation, and this extreme
sensitivity of many holding no stake in the matter to a criticism of
the large claims made in the name of modern science surely calls for
investigation. This conclusion cannot be escaped:--the men of our age
are nowhere near so emancipated from Authority as they like to fancy.

It is true we no longer acknowledge Authority in the theological
quarter. To accept on faith the cosmology of the Bible is to be
mediæval. But are we the self-conscious rebels we picture ourselves?
In revolting from the theological fold, have we not rebelled on
command of another Authority? However we came to revolt, now the
situation is plain. We have merely transferred the seat of Authority,
planting it today in the scientific quarter. Ye have conquered, O
Galileans! To be modernistic is to be as subservient to the oracular
opinions of scientific savants as the ordinary mediæval man was to the
interpretations of his theologians. The boastful modernists are, in
fact, still looking up to something far above: they are still on their
knees before Authority as the bulk of mankind has always knelt.

What we take on authority is by definition what we cannot scrutinize or
have not troubled to scrutinize, and of course we must be uncritical
about the uninspected. Here, in the region of the authoritative, is
where _aberglaube_, extra-belief, has a chance to enter; here is
where we become superstitious. The New Humanist has never attacked
exact science in itself, and in some respects his critique of what
seem to him the mistaken pretensions of science is very mild. But he
does pierce the _aberglaube_ that in the nineteenth century attached
itself to modern science. He is the foe of those who are gullible
about science, who take it altogether on faith, who are, in short,
superstitious. The language is not too strong.

There is a very quick way to test the tenacious hold that scientific
authority exerts on us. (To repeat myself, I mean to test the existence
of what to us are pure assumptions which have been implanted in us
almost without our knowledge by contemporary science.) The quick way
is to read two extraordinary and weird books of recent years, _The
Book of the Damned_ and _New Lands_, both by an investigator of
phenomena which have been excluded by orthodox science, Charles Fort.
The author of these strange documents is acutely aware of the very
human tendency of scientific system-makers to achieve a harmony of
knowledge by excluding the inharmonious data. He has therefore turned
his attention to the “disregards” of the scientists (geographers,
geologists, astronomers, physicists, etc.), and comes forward as the
champion of the damned data--and some of the data is horrendous. A
daring writer, for he is obliged to challenge the correctness of many
elementary things we learned in grammar and secondary schools. He
adduces evidence, that is to say, totally at variance with what we
were taught in school, when we were most submissive, and which we have
_never_ questioned. The result is that in reading Fort we experience
a most peculiar sense of strain. We find that we _hate_ to have any
one argue with a show of good reasons that interstellar distances are
rather small when we have always been told and believed, most of us
without any detailed proof, that they were inconceivably immense. There
are certain things--many items in fact--about the world which we want
to regard as having been settled and learned once for all. The idea of
new learning is attractive, but the idea of re-learning in fields where
we have assumed certain things to have been thoroughly proven is most
repugnant.

Among his provisional theories or “acceptances” as he calls them,
Fort holds “that we do not really think at all; that we correlate
around super-magnets that I call Dominants--a Spiritual Dominant in
one age, and responsively to it up spring monasteries, and the stake
and the cross are its symbols: a Materialist Dominant, and up spring
laboratories, and microscopes and telescopes and crucibles are its
ikons--that we’re nothing but iron filings relatively to a succession
of magnets that displace preceding magnets.”

To use Fort’s terminology, the New Humanist recognizes that science is
the Dominant of our civilization, and that most people, impressed by
its marvels in the field of mechanical inventions, simply correlate
around it. He invites us therefore to _think_ about this super-magnet,
to mark out its true sphere of influence, and to be conscious of
our relation to it. He urges us, in brief, to be as critical of its
magnetism as we are now able to be of the theological Dominant of
former ages.

The first step is to discriminate between real science and what the
French call _petite science_ and we call popular science. The latter is
often out-of-date, frequently over-simplified, and usually boastful.
Yet it is what the average man, and many literary men with him, browses
upon. In contrast, the scientific leaders are as a rule models of
caution and modesty. The next step is to perceive that there is a
hierarchy of sciences. It is to see with A. S. Eddington “that physical
science comes nearest to that complete system of exact knowledge which
all sciences have before them as an ideal. Some fall far short of
it. The physicist who inveighs against the lack of coherence and the
indefiniteness of theological theories, will probably speak not much
less harshly of the theories of biology and psychology. They also
fail to come up to his standard of methodology.” But it is notorious
that the prestige rightly won by the most perfected sciences diffuses
itself, in the eyes of the layman, over the newer and half-baked
sciences, such as sociology, anthropology, and various schools of
psychology, precisely those branches of science or pseudo-science
which the literary are most attracted by the while they shirk the hard
labor involved in getting a real acquaintance with physics. Thus the
New Humanist turns out to be actually in accord with the strictest
scientific attitude in his refusal to attend with the same degree of
seriousness to, let us say, a “science of history” as that with which
he attends to the science of chemistry.

There is a third step, and once it is really accomplished, one
is released from either sentimentality or superstition about the
scientist. Ridiculously simple, yet it is hard to make. It consists in
realizing the subjectivity of men of science. Just as in a complex way,
the style is the man, so in a complicated way science is also the man.
The scientist has elaborated a method and perfected his instruments
to eliminate the “personal equation,” but it is nevertheless still
present to haunt everything he does, to color his interpretations, to
set the patterns for his theories. Science is not the voice of God but
a distinctly human product with human limitations. Science may today be
on a higher imaginative level, as A. N. Whitehead believes, but mark
that Whitehead says this is “not because we have finer imagination, but
because we have better instruments.”

After a sincere effort to understand sympathetically the anti-Humanist
viewpoint, it is still hard to see why such violent objection by
intelligent liberals should be taken to the general position adopted
by Humanism toward modern science. To sum up that position:--the New
Humanists differentiate between true science and _petite science_, they
draw attention to the differing degrees of development and stability in
the various sciences, they dwell on the imperfection of the man who is
using perfect instruments and a perfect method. It can only be that the
modernists are in no mood whatever for skepticism when it is exercised
toward their Authority.

 * * * * *

We would expect, however, American Humanists to touch modern science
far more closely than the foregoing sketch of a general attitude
necessitates, and in fact they do. They take up particular attitudes:
they debate details of the scientific dogmas: they subscribe or
tend to subscribe to a given theory of the world. In their detailed
criticism of science, they suggest a number of questions that so far
as I know have not heretofore been raised about the New Humanism.
The New Humanism, we have seen, is a philosophy of conduct. The New
Humanists--note that I am discriminating between the philosophy of
conduct and the persons who enunciate it--adopt a theory of the world.
In the first place, is the theory of the world usually held by the
New Humanist the direct and necessary outcome of his philosophy of
conduct? Granting the truth of his theory of psychology, does that
theory require a cosmology to go with it, and is the cosmology of
the majority of the New Humanists in strict correspondence? We have
examined the picture of himself which the New Humanist declares is
fundamental to the “good life.” But is the picture of the world he
holds before him also fundamental? What I wish to determine is whether
the series of attitudes toward himself, the world, and the affairs of
the world which we attribute to the New Humanist is seamless or whether
we can with a sharp critical knife shear the attitudes apart where they
join each other and say of any one of the specific attitudes, this is
not essential or an alternative attitude would be just as logical in
place of this one. Can we have Newtonian Humanists and Einsteinian
Humanists?

Basically, the New Humanist endeavors to be an artist of life. He
strives to become acquainted with his medium, the shifting texture
and stuffs of daily experience. He wishes like every other artist to
dominate his material, which means, in the chaotic field he has had
the noble audacity to choose, self-conquest. It is necessary for him
to dramatize his work as the struggle of two selves, the ordinary self
being the recalcitrant material and the human self being the artist.
Finally, like other artists he requires a technique for moulding his
subject matter, and he advocates as the technique the employment
of mediation. All this is practical psychology, and stands secure
against any theoretical psychology now before the general public. For
the New Humanist can say that whatever the ultimate mystery of human
nature is, however the two selves may be attached to each other and
reconciled, whatever the secret unification of man may be, you cannot
get around the fact that he is dualistic in practice. He can develop
himself on one assumption, and one only, and that is the assumption of
dualism. This is a hard stubborn fact of experience against which the
Humanist makes his mind collide, just as Dr. Johnson kicked another
hard stubborn fact of experience, a stone, to terminate a discussion
of Berkeley’s idealism. _Why_ this should be so, _why_ man should
indubitably be in essence a struggle, the Humanist cannot say, but no
more can he doubt that it is so whenever he makes the attempt to win
moral freedom. There it is, in the path of his wish for freedom, and he
must accept it as being _practically_ true.

Undeniably--though it is seldom commented upon--the dualistic picture
of man has great emotive power. Our emotions are swayed by images, a
truism as old as ancient India, as new as Christian Science and Coué.
It is not the whole story, but in a large measure emotions do respond
to pictures. The time-worn illustration is the coil of rope in the
road which one takes to be a snake. One’s emotions form themselves
accordingly, but on discovery that the supposed snake is only a coiled
rope they change at once. So a person with a Rousseauistic image of man
feels according to that image: his emotions drag against any effort to
rouse himself from Arcadian dreams. The Humanist with his picture of a
best self conferring style and order on an impressionistic protean self
rallies his emotions to give their impulsion and sanction to the end in
view: the achievement of normality. A creative conception of man calls
forth creative emotions.

Now is there incorporated in American Humanism a clear-cut creative
conception of the world? Is the world condensed into an image that
is as powerful in its effects as the image of the two selves, as
irrefutable on the practical plane, as inspiring to human effort? I do
not find one, and this I take to be a serious shortcoming in American
Humanism as a philosophy of conduct. But in my judgment the defect
can with relative ease be remedied, and remedied in a manner most
congenial to a philosopher of conduct so Greek as the American Humanist
in his self-attitude. The reader ought to remember what was said in
the chapter, _A Portrait of Our Age_, about the various life-pictures,
the “æsthetic,” the “puritan,” the “serious,” et cetera, current among
us, and to recall the unlucky G. R. who had his imagination of life
altered from the “Victorian” to the “scientific” imagination. None of
those attitudes mentioned will do for the American Humanist, but why
should he not find, being gymnastic in his relations with his ordinary
self, aiming as he does to develop strength of character by exercises
in mediation and selection, that the external world is best figured as
a gymnasium? A vast enlargement of the ancient Lyceum which existed
for the exercise of the mind as well as the body? Why should not the
present-day Humanist revive this Greek symbol as he revives the Greek
law of measure? Let us utilize again the practical psychologist whom we
quoted in the earlier discussion of world-attitudes.

“There can be little doubt that for most of us, in the present epoch,
the image of life as a gymnasium is a greatly needed tonic. Compare
the difference in your attitude (emotional response) on entering
a gymnasium and on entering, let us say, a cabaret or a Y.M.C.A.
lecture-hall or a house of mourning. Try to realize what and how
you actually feel. You are braced up, you have the intention of
strengthening yourself, you delight in the difficulties,--provided you
choose them yourself. In short, you feel at your strongest and getting
stronger.

“The classic Greek conception of life was just that; and everybody
knows that the gymnasium was the most popular institution of
Pythagorean Greece. What is not so well known is that the gymnasium was
for the Greeks a symbol or image of life itself. Their God ran this
planet as a gymnasium for the exercise of men, and all experiences were
to be taken as movements, turns, stretches, exercises, in wrestling,
running, lifting, and so on. Moderns will find what the ancient Greeks
found in the image of life,--the evocation of a creative emotion.

“It is difficult to see in what other direction we moderns can look for
a new image and therefore attitude towards life. We have no longer the
possibility of religion in the traditional sense. Ordinary goodness--in
the sense of doing what others call good--has no intelligent appeal.
And, after the still recent Great War, the belief in world progress
is superstition. But the clean, strong idea of life as a field of
exercise for the development of all our muscles--physical, emotional
and intellectual--has still the unspoiled beauty of manly and
womanly idealism. And life lived in that attitude would certainly be
interesting as well as profitable.”

The above seems eminently fitted to be the humanistic attitude toward
the world. It involves no theology, no metaphysics, no science, being
a matter of personal psychology alone. It presupposes, of course, that
man can act as well as be acted upon, the first contention of the
Humanist, and it reinforces his wish to act. It definitely humanizes
the external world, making it a field for human beings to follow their
end of self-perfecting, but it does not sentimentalize or minimize the
world. It is, in fine, a tenable world-picture coöperating with the
main aim of Humanism. But it is not a theory of the world. That is
still to seek.

It is not far off. The theory of the world that has been joined on
to the humanistic theory of the self is Cartesian: at least so far
as one can determine from the commitments of the majority of the
New Humanists, it is Cartesian. They are not, as a rule, any more
precise than other literary men in what they say about their beliefs
concerning the nature of the physical world, but any one can hear that
they sing in chorus against the “New Physics.” They resist the thought
of A. N. Whitehead, A. S. Eddington, Albert Einstein, and re-affirm
their confidence in something like the following:--“Unless it can be
indisputably proved that man, with his infinite variety of thoughts
and emotions, is but an aggregation of mechanical atoms held together
and moved by physical forces--an hypothesis for which there is not the
slightest proof--there seems to be no necessity to deny the existence
of a spiritual world not subject to the laws of mechanical energy
or circumscribed by the space limitations of material or electrical
substances.” This means that on the one side they as a practically
united body reject deterministic psychology (Behaviorism, for example)
and on the other side they hand over the inorganic world to science,
concentrating on the faith that there are in the living organism
elements not analyzable or accountable by science. The quotation was
from _The Dogma of Evolution_ by Louis Trenchard More, the brother of
Paul Elmer More, and Dean of the Graduate School at the University
of Cincinnati. Here is another from the same source to which the New
Humanists would subscribe.

“It is no casual separation of our ideas into the physical, biological,
and psychological realms. The physical world is a combination of
substances acting according to a certain set of laws of force and
energy, the biological world is composed of the same substances, but
the laws of action are not the same. They have, however, this great
principle in common; they, both, are limited by time and space. Then,
somehow to the biological world an added principle is given that we can
call consciousness which is not material but is associated with matter
and which is not limited by time and space. To the present time, at
least, these three realms are to us incommensurable and we have made no
progress in measuring or explaining one by the other.”

Louis T. More is the scientist among the New Humanists, a thorny fact
to those who are trying to create the legend that the New Humanists
are anti-scientific. The legend is punctured by this physicist, who
has read and thought widely outside his subject; especially he has
browsed in the subject of philosophy, which is generally eschewed by
the rank-and-file scientists. The result of this wandering is that L.
T. More has equipped himself to disassociate scientific evidence from
the faith that twines so tightly around it: he is an able, diligent
critic, for example, of evolution as science and as faith. Considering
him therefore as the most conscious author among the New Humanists
when it comes to formulating a world-theory, let us look at his
essay, _The Pretensions of Science_ in the symposium, _Humanism and
America_. You will find him very restrictive. To begin with, “science
has a legitimate field of its own when investigating the phenomena
of the objective world and attempting to find law and order in the
flux of events.” But there are two classes of false claims made for
science, one by “those men of science who are not content to work in
their limited field, but are really metaphysicians who have created a
fictitious world of the imagination made out of ethers, electrons, and
mathematical symbols, and have confused it in their own and others’
minds with the sensible world of brute fact,” and the other by “those
who are claiming that the phenomena of the subjective world also lie in
the field of science and have imposed on the age the pseudosciences of
psychology and sociology.” This is nakedly Cartesian, for as Whitehead
shows so clearly, the principles of Descartes “lead straight to the
materialistic, mechanistic nature surveyed by cogitating minds. After
the close of the seventeenth century, science took charge of the
materialistic nature, and philosophy took charge of the cogitating
minds.”

Let us pursue along these two lines and learn what further L. T. More
has to say of the objective world and the “cogitating mind.” On the
first track, we read that “the cosmogony of Descartes has long since
crumbled to dust, but the gap he made between the subjective and
objective worlds has never been closed in spite of incessant later
attempts ... we have more and more regarded the universe as a machine,
a combination of inert matter and moving forces, acting not as we may
desire, but according to invariable laws which we have personified as
Nature. Man was left, by the physicists, as a unique outsider who could
interpret this machine in terms of his sensations and mind, but could
neither alter nor avoid its fateful operation ... the scientific method
is limited to experimental observation and the formulation of laws ...
the inevitable tendency of science is to investigate all phenomena
quantitatively, and to view the whole universe as a vast and measurable
machine ... exact science is first of all based on the naïve belief in
an objective and real world whose events are connected in an orderly
and prescribed manner, and occur independently of our thought or will
... the mechanistic world can, at best, only picture an objective world
as it seems to us, and not as it is.”

Now following the second track, we read of “the two equally valid
methods of learning by objective experimentation and by personal
experience.... In a general way, we may say that the scientist should
follow in his investigations the phenomena of the objective world
only until their special forms of energy are absorbed by our nervous
system. It is the province of the humanist to study the phenomena of
the subjective world after these stimuli have been translated into
emotion and thought ... the false pretensions of science must be wholly
abandoned, and the problems of our destiny be examined by a wise
judgment drawn from human experience, before we can hope for a sane and
humanistic philosophy.”

Here we can stop with some assurance that the main position toward
which the New Humanists gravitate has been disclosed. Generally
speaking, it is unfair to set forward any Humanist as an “official
spokesman” and perhaps I should not have assigned L. T. More to that
rôle. But his brother, P. E. More, and Robert Shafer have, I believe,
been fertilized by his reflections on science, and I cannot detect in
the writings of the other New Humanists any radical divergence from
the Cartesian scheme. The question is, is the Cartesian world-picture
integral to American Humanism or is it simply one of the possible views
that might be adopted by American Humanists and held by them in common
with members of various other schools of thought? Also, it is evident
there are a number of difficulties which L. T. More’s statements
immediately raise, and which no doubt the reader feels should be dealt
with. In the next section I shall put forward these difficulties myself
and at the same time try to formulate what I think the modern man’s
attitude should be toward scientific world-building.

 * * * * *

It seems to me that the truly modern man will first of all strive to
free himself from the great spell cast by science in our epoch. He
will try to overcome his own suggestibility upon which propagandists
for science from Huxley and Spencer to the present have played, so
that he may answer himself frankly when he wonders if our hopes in
science have not deceived us. Furthermore, as was earlier suggested,
he will try to arrive at some conception of the external world which
is not irrational, not violable by the teachings of science, and is
at the same time dynamic and humanizing in its influence. This demand
the world symbolized as a gymnasium, an immense Academy or Lyceum or
Institute of Crotona, appears to meet perfectly. It makes no difference
whether one adheres to a materialist or an organic or an idealistic
philosophy: the world as a gymnasium for the strengthening of human
fiber is a valid imagination under any of the three types of philosophy
named. The question then becomes: picturing life as a gymnasium for
the development of men, what values can be extracted from science by
the man who does not propose to train himself to be a professional
scientist?

Such a man, aiming at a general coördinated culture, must come to
regard science in the same way he regards art and philosophy--with
respect, but not sentimentally or superstitiously. No divinity
surrounds any of the three: each subject is man-made and therefore
fallible. But each is primarily to be enjoyed for its own sake. We
accept this principle in art, not so fully in philosophy, and still
less in science. Yet science provides a manipulation and stretching of
mental muscles that is highly enjoyable for the sake of exercise alone.
It provides adventures and perils of thought of a kind differing from
philosophy and art. A case should in fact be made out for science as a
humane discipline, though I think of no one in America who is a shining
example of such a discipline. I mean that we do not seem to produce
men of the type of J. W. N. Sullivan in England, who are exquisitely
cultivated both in the arts and sciences, who are supple-minded,
adventurous in thought, thoroughly skeptical, at home with Einstein as
with Beethoven or Dostoyevsky or Plato. We need cryingly more gifted
amateurs among our laymen who study science and fewer intolerant
“gospellers” of the type of Harry Elmer Barnes. I am reminded of some
sagacious things about the _use_ of philosophy for human beings which
T. S. Eliot has recently written, because they apply equally well to
the _use_ of science.

“When we study philosophy as a humane discipline we do not do so merely
in order to pick out one which we shall adopt as ‘true,’ or either
to confect a philosophy of our own out of all philosophies. We do so
largely for the exercise in assumption or entertaining ideas; for the
enlargement and exercise of mind we get by trying to penetrate a man’s
thought and think it after him, and then passing out of that experience
to another. Only by the exercise of understanding without believing,
so far as that is possible, can we come in full consciousness to some
point where we believe _and_ understand.” Eliot adds, “Similarly
with the experience of poetry,” and I would add, similarly with the
experience of science.

The blunt truth is that the grounds of the attack on the New Humanists’
statements about science have been mistaken. They were accused of
hostility to the workings of science. But they should have been
accused of lukewarmness toward science as a humane discipline.
Excepting L. T. More, P. E. More and Robert Shafer, the New Humanists
may be said to lack zeal for that kind of development of their minds
which science is most able to confer. Mildness of interest in a subject
and active hostility to it are so different that not even blind
partizanship would seem to be a sufficient excuse for identifying them.

But let not this argument for science as a humane discipline by
which one flexes and enlarges the mind be construed as advocacy of
dilettantism in the current debased sense of that word. The end in view
is to become an alert critic of world-theories. And here I will say
that I believe there is no essential connection between Humanism as a
philosophy of conduct and the Cartesian materialistic theory of the
universe. They are joined in the minds of the New Humanists, but that
I think is a fortuitous juncture. Nor can I see that Humanism depends
in any way soever upon a defence of Newtonian physics against later
modifications and developments. The Christian cosmogony and cosmology
constitute to most of us a mythology. The mythology may be profoundly
true, more true than modern science, but we are in the position of not
being able to verify its statements by an exact procedure. The means
of individual verification may once have existed, but where are they
now? We may assent to the Christian mythology, but it is assent based
on faith, a very different thing from the _faith based on reason_
of the great doctors of the Middle Ages. Add to this the fact that
the materialistic, mechanistic world-view has been shaken by the
tremendous blows of mathematical physicists like A. N. Whitehead.
It has got into the epicyclic state which presages a breakdown.
Alternative world-schemes, imposing, daring, almost sublimely
intuitive, are now being put forward. In other words, the problem of a
world-theory has been reopened on a scale not dreamed of for centuries.

“The eighteenth century,” observes Whitehead, “opened with the quiet
confidence that at last nonsense had been got rid of. Today we are at
the opposite pole of thought. Heaven knows what seeming nonsense may
not tomorrow be demonstrated truth.”

Now it appears to me that the New Humanists, by and large, retain
something of the eighteenth century attitude in the face of the new
physical problem. But a problem of this kind calls for the suspension
of belief, for all the skepticism one can summon, for the complete
willingness to run down any turning of thought, for the surprise
attack, for an attitude best described as adventurous. The law for
thing and the law for man, as they work out in human experience, do
not depend on Cartesianism. At the present time, in fact, the law for
man may be said to dictate the extrication of one’s self from grooves
of thought about the world: it lays an obligation upon one to be
provisional in one’s acceptances of world-theories: it requires that
the problem be recognized and faced in the spirit appropriate to a
mystery. Not to be a farranging agile critic of theories of the world
is, in the twentieth century, not to be in key with one of its major
trends in thought.

To illustrate the point, compare your feelings while traversing the
familiar way home and while threading your route out of a labyrinth.
The difference is like the difference between our take-it-for-granted
psychology in handling a solved problem and our active searching
psychology in moving about in a new problem. We are the same person
but the intellectual environment has altered. It seems to me that
most of the New Humanists have yet to adapt their psychology to the
labyrinth of modern physics. Note, however, that I do not urge upon
them any recommended way out: I do not ask them to adopt Eddington’s
world-theory but to tackle it in a different spirit.

For it may well be that this intense critical search for a tenable
conscious world-view will not meet with complete success in our
century. But not to make the search is not, in my judgment, to be
humanistic, since the problem is the problem of any one who aspires
toward completeness. The final personal reward of a maximum use of
our critical powers on classical physics and the new physics is that
we at least shall know with the utmost clarity what we believe and
understand, and what we do not believe and find insoluble. That is,
we shall be as conscious as we can possibly be of the nature of the
external world, even if that consciousness consists but of alternative
possibilities, doubts, queries and speculations, and perhaps a few
certitudes.

There is another large fact to be grasped, toward which our psychology
must make adjustment. It is that modern science is stranded on a
“plateau” and its giants are making their greatest efforts to raise
it, to initiate a new curve of development. The analogy is with those
curious graphs of learning processes, which show that one learns
rapidly at first, then comes to a period of little or no progress (“a
plateau”), then mounts again, arriving later at another “plateau,” and
so on. To get off a “plateau” a special effort, produced either by
some external incentive such as the offer of a reward or by a renewed
determination, is required. Now it has for some time been recognized
by advanced minds that modern science was in just such a condition
of arrest. It was able to refine upon what it had already done, but
not able to initiate any great rising movement. There is material for
novel reflections in the description Whitehead gives of the state of
science at the end of the nineteenth century. “Almost suddenly, a pause
occurred; and in its last twenty years the century closed with one of
the dullest stages of thought since the time of the First Crusade.
It was an echo of the eighteenth century, lacking Voltaire and the
reckless grace of the French aristocrats. The period was efficient,
dull and half-hearted.” The cause? We may venture to say that it was
because, as Whitehead elsewhere notes, science for two centuries had
been running on the accumulated capital of ideas of the seventeenth
century, “the century of genius.” A pause, a blocking, was bound to
come. And today science, as Whitehead and Eddington recognize, is at
a crucial turning point. It seeks to discover the elements that will
make for another period of development, and discovery of the necessary
elements Whitehead tells us requires more genius than to exploit them
when found.

Who can fail to be stirred by this tremendous drama of the human mind?
And yet it appears that many are unmoved, anti-Humanists as well as
Humanists. The scientist, going about his business, discovers that his
personal limitations, the usual habits of scientists, are retarding
the progress of science itself. Science properly is concerned with
the what-ness of things. From the why-ness of things (philosophy)
and the æsthetic and other emotional imports of things (values) it
has resolutely cut itself off. It has, every one knows, discounted
the intuitive, discounted other forms of knowledge. But now see: the
scientist is discovering that his exclusive interest in _what_ and his
impatience with _why_ and with value is ultimately stultifying. He
feels the basic need to fertilize himself by considering other reports
on reality than his own, he struggles to escape the bondage of his
traditional habits. It is a situation that should call forth the full
interest and sympathy of the humanistically inclined.

For what it emphasizes is the desperate need for a coördination of all
our knowledges. A knowledge of “porous atoms” does not destroy our
practical knowledge of a table, nor take away the table’s æsthetic
properties. The world exists as a source of ideas, of emotions, of
actions; it is simultaneously a field of practical affairs, an object
of feeling, an intellectual feast. Reality is threefold, but our
reports upon reality seldom harmonize. The rationalist underrated two
of these reports and trusted fully to one only. The scientist now shows
a disposition to admit the validity of all three and to bring about
a _rapprochement_ of the practical, emotional and mental worlds. A
very faint glimmer still comes across the centuries, elusive witness
to the grandeur of the Pythagorean synthesis. Who knows but what the
twentieth century, treasuring the glimmer, may not succeed eventually
in reflecting it back with an unexpected glory?

“Know thyself,” said Pythagoras, and the recollection of that
injunction points to our greatest stumbling-block. To me it is a little
Canute-like of L. T. More to forbid to science the subjective world.
And I say this with full awareness of the inadequacy and faultiness
of introspection as a method. But if introspection is of little use
to psychology as a science, it is equally of little use to Humanism.
Is it impossible, however, that a better method cannot be devised, is
it impossible to put self-study on a scientific basis? The rise of
Behaviorism is too impressive for any fiats to be delivered now against
the further endeavors to found psychology on a really substantial
basis. The Behaviorist has cleared the ground, he has come to the
threshold, but it is a question of the key to the subjective world. I
do not think the New Humanist need dread the search for the key.

 IX

 EDUCATION AND LEADERSHIP

  _“Tradition is the experience of society. It is stored up for use
  by what may be called the objective memory. The chief function of
  education is to transfer the wealth of a selective tradition from
  society to the individual.”_--Paul Elmer More in _The Drift of
  Romanticism: Shelburne Essays, Eighth Series_.


Insufficient attention has been paid by intelligent unacademic people
to the educational ideas and constructive suggestions for academic
reform offered by the New Humanists. Only one commentator in the
flurry of debate that ensued on the issuance of _Humanism and America_
wrote with gratitude of the salutary effect on literary scholarship
produced by the unremitting efforts of American Humanists, and he
was Henry Seidel Canby, who has known at first hand the _literae
inhumaniores_ that flourish rankly in our universities. Nowhere better,
it seems to me, than in their dissertations on the teaching of the
humanities--nowhere better than in such books as Babbitt’s _Literature
and the American College_ and Foerster’s _The American Scholar_--can we
witness the law of measure being applied to current problems. Moreover,
if anything calls for the most tactful mediatory treatment, it is the
tendencies and objectives of our colleges and universities.

It is right, I think, to regard American Humanism, when one refers
to the _movement_ rather than the doctrine and discipline, as being
primarily an educational force. Many of its adherents are actively
engaged in teaching and therefore the problems of education possess a
personal urgency for them. They do not dream at present of marching on
the literary citadels of the country and subduing their garrisons to
standards they have foresworn, but the teacher-classicists definitely
do propose to wrest education from the control of humanitarians. There,
in the field of education, the New Humanist affirms “the battle that
is to determine the fate of American civilization will be fought out
first of all.” For this reason I am giving a chapter to the members of
the movement as educators rather than as literary critics. As literary
critics, they consider the prevalent impressionism to be a low form
of criticism and seek to restore criticism to its former acknowledged
business, judgment by humane standards. But I feel no regret in
neglecting in this book the numerous important ideas for contemporary
literary critics which stud the works of Babbitt and More. With such
writers as T. S. Eliot and F. McEachran in England, Robert Shafer and
Seward Collins in America lifting them up and weighing them, they
are certain of a distinguished attention. So my conscience does not
trouble me because in the present interpretation only incidentally do
I visit the purely literary front of the New Humanist campaign. The
most serious fighting is on the educational front, and the enemy is
educational impressionism.

We can divide the educational front into three sectors,--the lower
schools, the college, and the university with its graduate schools. On
the first the New Humanists are not very active, on the second they
take a very determined stand. If they can hold the college to its
function, as they conceive it, they are willing to make concessions
in the graduate schools. The leading ideas they put at stake in each
sector are very easy to state. In the lower schools, where it is
granted that the humanitarian tinge is not undesirable, the pupil
should be acquisitive and receptive. In college the student should
be assimilative and reflective. In the university the scholar may
be productive, investigatory, original on the basis of his humane
discipline in the college. Reception when young, assimilation in
college years, productive research in graduate school--these are the
functions that should underlie our educational system. But the college
is being ground out between the encroachments of preparatory schools
and the invasion of university ideals of research. And there is where
the situation is most tense and critical. We shall begin, however, with
the lower schools and the theory of John Dewey, arch-foe of American
Humanism in education as well as in philosophy.

 * * * * *

No one can be insensible to the value of Dewey’s correction of certain
evils in our public school system. He has perceived the dangers of too
much abstractness in its curriculum, and the need for more education by
doing. It is largely through his influence that the Project Method, as
it is now called, the method of teaching by correlating school subjects
around some social activity of the children, around weaving, for
example, has been given its present vogue. But it is the leading ideas
of Dewey that are at issue, and not any desirable modification of the
elementary school curriculum.

We live in a democratic society: therefore, say the disciples of
Dewey, education should adapt itself to the needs and the condition
of the many children, not of the few exceptional ones. We live in an
industrial society: therefore education should be closely connected
with the major actualities of an industrial society. Going to school
is not “a preparation for life.” It is experience as much as any other
phase of life. The gap between life in school and life in the family
and on the playground should be decreased as much as possible. In a
sentence, education should be socialized. It should be thoroughly
democratic. It should develop the virtue of coöperation to the highest
degree.

To these guiding ideas every one must in some measure give assent,
but many people feel that their limited assent must be combined with
a great deal of criticism. Robert Shafer, for one, looking over the
records of educational experiments along the lines mapped out by Dewey,
has repeated the old proverb: one swallow does not make a summer. The
experiments have been made with picked children and picked teachers.
But, Shafer asks, can conclusions be drawn that will be valid for the
educational system as a whole, since in the whole system the children
are unpicked, the coöperation of the majority of parents cannot be
secured, and the quality of the teachers must inevitably sink far below
the standard of the experimental school enthusiasts. Waiving every
other consideration, are the new lower-school ideas feasible for a
wide application? But presupposing practicality, those other waived
considerations become the very important ones, and the New Humanist
cannot satisfy his strong doubts of the ideals of the Dewey reform.
Others, besides New Humanists, have come to experience like doubts,
and some of these are persons who at one time were wholly convinced
or almost convinced of the soundness of Dewey’s proposals. One of
them is a woman who, to my belief, is the most brilliant of all the
contemporary pioneering spirits in the “new education.” I mean Margaret
Naumburg, the founder and for eight years (1914-1922) the director
of the Walden School in New York city. In 1928 a book from her pen
appeared, _The Child and the World_, a series of dialogues on modern
education, in which she summed up the results of her thinking and
experience in her special field. In spite of the author’s reputation,
the book was not, as a rule, thoroughly reviewed. One surmises that
this was in part due to Miss Naumburg’s having abandoned and criticized
the positions so many other new educators were still clinging to.
However it happened, the central point of the book was overlooked:
very little attention was paid to the clash she staged between Dewey’s
program for socialized education and Whitehead’s views on education
which stress the encouragement of individual development.

Our immediate interest lies, of course, not in Miss Naumburg’s partial
advocacy of Whitehead, but in her critique of Dewey. Naturally, as
who would not, she speaks of Rousseau as the common ancestor of the
new school movement in America, approving of his praise of learning
by doing; to Rousseau she links Dewey as the immediate parent of
present-day experiments. But Rousseau’s general influence on the new
school movement, she emphasizes, has had very unfortunate consequences.
This is due to establishing so many experiments on “Rousseau’s
romantic hope that maladjusted human functions could right themselves
by a return to the simple life of nature ... the belief that we can
right ourselves by simply removing certain limiting trammels of
civilization.” Miss Naumburg, her book makes clear, has shed all her
illusions about so naïve a belief. Her counts against Dewey are four:
(1) he underestimates emotional culture in his educational plans, (2)
he has framed these plans too closely in accord with his own bias as
a psychological type, being particularly unfair to the possibilities
of the training of intuition, (3) he is too chary of the inner, the
subjective life, and (4) his socialized education completely neglects
individual psychology.

She holds up for close scrutiny a quotation from Dewey’s _Democracy and
Education_ which the New Humanist would certainly agree is an amazing
piece of sophistry. It is the concentration of all the arguments for
socialized education. “From a social standpoint, dependence denotes a
power rather than a weakness; it involves interdependence. There is
always a danger that increased personal independence will decrease
the social capacity of an individual. In making him more self-reliant
it may make him more self-sufficient; it may lead to aloofness and
indifference. It often makes an individual so insensitive in his
relations to others as to develop an illusion of being really able to
stand and act alone--an unnamed form of insanity which is responsible
for a large part of the remediable suffering of the world.”

On this strange argument Miss Naumburg’s comment is: “Much of the
present social philosophy that wishes to sacrifice the individual to
the good of the group is nothing but instinctive herd psychology,
translated into modern terms.... I’m sure the world now needs more
complete individuals so that group life can become integrated on a
higher plane. Dewey places emphasis on the individual’s living for the
group rather than for himself and suggests that his failure to do so
is the cause of much of the world’s suffering. But to me it seems that
the failure of the individual to know himself remains the cause of his
ineffectual adaptation to a positive and adequate social existence.”

This is pointed criticism from the inside of a movement whose advanced
minds are now beginning to realize that they have envisaged their
problems far too simply,--that self-expression is not the magic
formula, that psycho-analysis produces as much trouble as it dissolves,
that socialized education too much disregards the individual gifted
pupil, that liberty is a hard concept to understand aright. The New
Humanist on the outside phrases his criticism differently. It is that
Dewey offers no contribution to the urgent problem of producing leaders
of quality in a democratic society. For the New Humanist makes no
fetich of democracy, as does Dewey, nor does he stand for an unmixed
aristocracy either. To him the social problem is the blending of the
two, the securing of an aristocratic and selective democracy, and he
would like to see education from the kindergarten upwards organized for
the furtherance of that aim.

 * * * * *

Dewey is a Baconian philosopher with an admixture of Rousseauism. I am
here making use of the Babbittean classification of modern naturalists
into two types, those prefigured by Francis Bacon who are known as
scientific naturalists, and those prefigured by Rousseau and known as
sentimental naturalists. The classification causes so much resentment
among present-day intellectuals, it is so hotly repudiated, that I
am led to wonder if Babbitt has not again probed to the bottom of
our era’s mind and located basic trends we have not known we were
exemplifying. Certainly, his two types, however petty many of the
exemplars today may be, are of epical stature. The drama of the last
three centuries is no mean one, and requires as its leading characters
personages to fit the scale of the action.

Of course, we must discriminate between Babbitt’s estimates of Bacon
and Rousseau as personages and his description of the types of men he
names after them. In the character of Bacon, for instance, he notes
the presence of traditional Christianity and Renascence humanistic
elements. Babbitt does not, to my mind, allow sufficiently for the
enigmatic in Bacon--hardly any one today is cautious enough for
that--and he accounts for the Lord Chancellor’s fall, which is still
inexplicable when all is said, on too general grounds. But, to get his
types, Babbitt does not simplify either Bacon or Rousseau: he merely
extracts from them the large formative elements which released into the
world have created the Baconian and the Rousseauist. It is undeniable
that Bacon was, in Whitehead’s words, “one of the great builders who
constructed the mind of the modern world.” He was the prophet of the
Kingdom of Man, Babbitt declares. He foresaw man’s dominion over the
earth, he made efficient the impulse to unlock Nature with the keys of
Science, he stimulated men to the astounding utilitarian triumphs which
the centuries since Bacon have achieved. It was his genius that made
the idea of progress so potent.

The Baconians have gone forth and conquered the earth. If most of them
lack the slightest touch of Bacon’s dignity as a human being, they
nevertheless partake of the majesty of numbers and are not unworthy of
a mass-part in a planetary drama. But it has been a mass with notable
leaders down to John Dewey in our day. The mass firmly believes in
technological advance, material progress, the pragmatic life. For it
the “law for thing” is supreme. The leaders subtilize and rationalize
these beliefs.

The Baconians and Rousseauists are often superficially hostile to each
other. We all know the contempt the scientific analyst frequently
expresses for the dreamer, and in turn the dreamer’s horror of the
analyst. But Babbitt demonstrates that the two types are in close
accord underneath. Both deny the “law for man.” Both subscribe
fervently to the doctrine of progress. And oftentimes in the same
person you will find prominent strains of both types, happy in each
other’s company.

It scarcely needs saying that the Rousseauist is in a much greater
emotional ferment. The Baconian exalts practical intelligence,
strenuous deeds accompanied by acute if narrow thought: the Rousseauist
exalts sympathy, revery, spontaneity and liberty. His idea of liberty
is summed up in the popular expression, “doing as one likes,” which
means, Matthew Arnold labored to show, doing as one’s ordinary self
likes. “Do what thou wilt,” the injunction over the Abbey of Theleme,
is a far sterner permission and closer to the true idea of liberty.

It is, I think it necessary to repeat, not claimed by the New Humanist
that contemporary Baconians and Rousseauists have been directly
influenced by the remarkable men they are named after. The actual
matter is very complex, as you will see by recourse to Babbitt’s
books, but what is maintained is that unconsciously we breathe in the
widespread gaseous elements of the teachings of Rousseau and Bacon and
are profoundly modified in our entire outlook on civilization by the
fact that they once lived.

In recent years the American college has been overrun by the Baconian
scientific hustlers and by the Rousseauistic relaxed dilettantes--both
menaces to humanistic education.

 * * * * *

In college circles the Baconian is usually a specialist eager to
advance learning in his branch of research but applying quantitative
tests to his department and to the college in general. He is strenuous
and imbued with the spirit of change. When he invades the arts,
he is scientific rather than artistic. To give a concrete example,
in the literature department the Baconian is the philologist,
engaged in original and minute research, but usually lacking in
æsthetic appreciation or in general ideas. He is supposed to teach
the humanities: he has prepared for his vocation by composing a
dissertation on the use of the present participle in some minor
Elizabethan.

His colleague in the literature department is often a Rousseauist,
a gelatinous æsthete, incapable of hard mental labor, who utilizes
