the world going about him getting good.... There are a thousand hacking
at the branches of evil to one who is striking at the root and it may
be that he who bestows the largest amount of time and money on the
needy is doing the most by his mode of life to produce that misery
which he strives in vain to relieve.... Do not stay to be an overseer
of the poor, but endeavor to become one of the worthies of the world.”

This is the twofold defence of the individualist: he confesses his
doubt that men’s knowledge and powers _as they are_ can do more than
merely meddle and muddle in social relations and he insists upon the
regeneration of oneself as being in the long run beneficial to those
around one. It is the very essence of the social criticism of the
American Humanists and conceivably to some temperaments among them it
might lead, as it did in Thoreau’s case, to personal secession from
the United States! The social anarchist like Thoreau is not heartless:
he is convinced of being helpless--like the man who has not learned to
swim and sees some one offshore drowning. The humanitarian plunges to
the rescue, but as he cannot swim, the only result is he drowns too.
The Humanist suffers from the spectacle of drowning souls, but he may
sensibly conclude with Thoreau that nothing can be effected toward
saving them until one has oneself learned to swim.

If American Humanism spreads, my prediction is that we shall eventually
see its adherents manifest as rich a variety stemming from certain
conventions of thought, feeling and discipline as we perceive in
Renascence Humanism. We shall see humanistic disciples of Thoreau
doggedly resisting the pleas of the Doing-Good professors, liberal
Humanists annoying by their socratic tendencies the orthodox liberals,
detached Humanists accused of defeatism and retreating to ivory towers
of thought, Socratic Conservatives like More and Babbitt, and perhaps
even a few Humanists tinged with the religious view of politics and
government. But we shall hardly see any measure of popularity attach
itself to any of the positions I have adumbrated.

 XI

 THE DILEMMA OF THE LIBERATED

  _“Intellectual freedom is earlier and easier than complete
  spiritual freedom.”_--T. S. Eliot in the _Hound and Horn_ magazine.


In the last sixty years isolated individuals of distinction have
told the Western world that it was living in a state of decline.
Nietzsche was probably the most tormented of these prophets and used
the strongest language. Modern man to him was an instrument out
of tune: mankind itself was degenerating. We in America had Henry
Adams charting the change from mediæval unity to twentieth century
multiplicity and theorizing about the degradation of the democratic
dogma. Today Bertrand Russell is quoted as saying that the clock of
Europe has been running down since the Renascence, Chesterton advises
us that we are simply existing on our Catholic capital and using it
up, Whitehead notes that science has lived on the accumulated capital
of the ideas of the seventeenth century and that religion has been
weakening for several centuries. But all these contemporaries manage
nevertheless to keep a cheerful outlook, Whitehead elaborating thoughts
for a new scientific and philosophic synthesis, Chesterton rejoicing
in his personal rediscovery of Catholic truths, and Russell being, as
a wit has expressed it, terribly at ease in hell. The man with the
most complete brief is Oswald Spengler, who studies the morphology
of history. In his philosophy of history synthesized from a survey of
eight cultures (Indian, Mayan, Greek, Egyptian, etc.), it is held that
we in the West have our spring and summer, are now in the autumn and
inevitably must pass to the winter stage of our cultural cycle. Gone is
any possibility of a Renascence: we have had ours, and are now in the
megalopolitan “civilization” phase, committed to technology, destined
for more huge wars, the uprising of untutored hordes, and the coming of
dictators. The arts will perish as important influences.

Spengler has popularized the notion of the decline of the western
world, but after all it remains just a notion to most people. Very
few are really convinced to the marrow that European and American
civilization is on a downward slope. Some say it is, but they merely
say it: some think so, but merely think it. To find a man who is
saturated with pessimism about our present and future state is almost
as rare as to find a Rosicrucian in contemporary intellectual society.
Our general tone is optimistic and conceited with only an occasional
breakdown when we focus on some particular spot and admit that it is
unhealthy now and likely to get worse. But we cannot grant that certain
epochs in the past were greater than our own: we cannot get it out of
our heads that we are an improvement on all previous generations: we
cannot shed our faith in progress and see our whole civilization as a
retrogression.

The chief exceptions are Paul E. More and Irving Babbitt. They differ
from Spengler in being much more cautious generalizers: they talk about
one culture only, and chiefly the last four hundred years of that.
They do not think metaphorically about history or try to construct a
science or philosophy of history. A chief point of difference is that
they repudiate fatalism, admitting that we are biassed to roll down one
groove of the future but not denying that other possibilities still
remain open and asserting that by a great effort we may select one
of these as our future. The nearest they come to writing history by
analogy is to point out the similarity between Alexandrian culture and
many of the phenomena of contemporary learning and to make a tentative
comparison between the decadent imperialism of Rome under Tiberius or
Nero and the present tendencies in the United States toward discarding
standards, increasing luxury, and fomenting factional strife just as
we approach the acme of power. But, generally speaking, Babbitt and
More confine themselves strictly to the portrayal of psychological
decay in one locality, Europe and America taken together as Western
culture, during one definite span of time, the last four centuries
and particularly the last two. I have remarked that they do not think
metaphorically about history, yet their teaching can be concentrated
in a metaphor, and perhaps without loss of subtlety gain in power of
appeal.

We can go about picturing the Fall of Western Man if we adopt the
Platonic truism that society is simply a man writ large, for we are
then free to figure Western society as an Atlas the Endurer who rebels
against being otherworldly, ceases to uphold the heavens, and turns
this-worldly. At first his old habits of thinking and feeling persist:
he has merely ceased to make effort to miraculously sustain his center
of gravity outside himself in the “other world.” But now his center
of gravity shifts to within: he no longer stands perfectly upright,
strained toward the Absolute, but leans a little toward this earth. As
he now thinks, the heavens did not all these centuries really need his
support; he was deceived by a myth, he thinks, and it does not occur
to him that he was the one who benefited by his posture and effort. As
he leans toward the earth, he rejoices in his freedom to lean. This is
the end of the Renascence. Well in the past now is the era of Gothic
cathedral building which showed what incomparable architecture Atlas
was capable of when he held up the heavens. Well past, too, are many
other strangely beautiful things, among them “La Gaya Scienza” of the
Provençal culture. From now on a new order of behavior, and it is at
this turning-point that More and Babbitt begin closely to observe
Atlas. What they see is a gradually shifting and settling of the center
of gravity of his psychology.

For a time he holds himself almost upright. He cultivates
_sprezzatura_. He is the seventeenth century gentleman and scholar
who does not pride himself on anything. He _fears_ specialization and
believes that “perfected good-breeding consists in having no particular
mark of any profession, but a general elegance of manners.” But he
sways a little: a slight turn and he is neo-classical and becomes
stiff, conventional, puristic, the center of his gravity in his mind.
He has begun to totter in the direction he leans--toward the earth.

Then More and Babbitt observe how the giant makes a violent effort
to right himself. It is the romantic recoil from the neo-classical
aridity. It is Rousseau and his followers raving against trying to
put one’s gravity-center outside oneself, or against trying to keep
it established inside oneself in the mind. Slowly the center changes,
then drops suddenly into the emotions. Atlas becomes fiery. He is a
Jacobin. He sets up democracies wherein popular feeling may sweep more
freely. He colors philosophy with subjectivism: henceforward it is not
the drama of the universe but the inward drama of the psyche that he
ruminates upon. He institutes rebellion in the arts, confusing them,
and he chases like a madman after sensation. The phenomena of his new
state--his predilection for idyllic dreams, his praise of spontaneity
and idiosyncrasy, his love of delicious revery, his dislike of
discipline, etc.--grow countless. Atlas is ruled by his feelings, and
they are not counterweighted by his mind. Whole parts and functions of
his psychology are going to rust, most disastrously of all his will. No
wonder he sprawls forward on all fours. With his center of gravity in
his unbridled feelings, he must fall.

But now is his center sinking again? Will Atlas, no longer the Endurer,
be changed into a cunning savage? Will he become simply a creature who
exploits the earth’s resources and alternates his labors in production
with a complete spineless subsidence into physical comforts? Is the
day of Apeneck Sweeney and Molly Bloom dawning on a culture that has
known the sardonic Swift and the charm of Madame de Sévigné? Atlas who
used magnificently to bear up the sky--is he to be stretched prone in
comfort in the twenty-first century, no vulture tearing at his flesh as
at his brother Prometheus, but a radio to lull his mind?

The signs are plentiful of another transition: we have gone from
the dominance of religious values to the dominance of intellectual
ones and then to the primacy of emotional values. The last
stage--barbarism--will occur with the complete triumph of practical and
instinctive values. Then Atlas will indeed be but an economic creature,
cleverly producing what he needs by the least effort, and spending his
increased leisure in the pursuit of cheap distractions from thought and
serious emotion. He will truly have diminished to the ninth part of a
human being.

Such, in a picture, is my reading of the critique of Western
civilization so elaborately and weightily presented by Babbitt and
More. They have traced the movements of our psychological center of
gravity and view with foreboding the future of a society whose members
will not make the almost impossible struggle to elevate its center, to
resume the former standing posture of Atlas. But this simplification
should be followed at once by a warning to the reader who has not
consulted the whole body of works by Babbitt and More. Do not surmise
they have trimmed to a thesis. They have been the patient collectors
of historical evidence, and the thesis emerges from a study of all
the gatherable evidence. “By their fruits ye shall know them,” is
Babbitt’s working rule. Secondly, do not think that the evidence, while
susceptible of simplification in the mass, is in detail simple. Man is
as prolific in ideas as Nature is prolific in insects (unfortunately,
most ideas are of the insect order): he is a being as full of desires
as an egg is full of meat (and his shell has been broken): he is
as active and restless as a monkey (and about as purposeless).
Consequently, his mental, emotional and instinctive manifestations are
variegated in the extreme. Variegated, but not pure chaos, not without
a pattern. Babbitt and More are pattern-perceivers, not imposers of
arbitrary patterns. In the third place, do not think that all value
is denied to romanticism and naturalism. They have in fact many good
elements, which More and Babbitt fully recognize. What is bad about
these schools is their rejection of the more human elements in our
psychology, what is bad is their glorification of a lowered center of
gravity.

The New Humanist, as any one knows who is acquainted with his mind,
does not wish to turn the clock back but to incorporate the gains of
romanticism and naturalism into a broader synthesis with a more stable
center.

 * * * * *

A few people can take seriously the question of the degeneration of our
civilization. If you are one of these, prepared to admit seriously the
possibility that the species of mankind may over long periods fall and
rise in quality, then you are likely to reflect seriously on all the
questions that American Humanists find of the first rank and interest.
If you approach these questions with the same intent sobriety with
which they are propounded, then you will find a dilemma confronting
you. At one horn there is Naturalism: at the other--Humanism. The
statement signalizes a change in this book: up to now it has been
an interpretation of American Humanism, from now on it will be a
criticism. It is plain that I find the naturalistic horn no horn to
grasp. Its disadvantages have been too forcibly presented by the
writers grouped around Babbitt and More. On the other hand, we would
have to thank their opponents had they revealed with equal skill and
force the drawbacks and dangers attached to the alternative horn,
but I cannot feel that the anti-Humanists have done their business
satisfactorily to date. They have been rather living examples of the
fallacies and partialities exposed by the New Humanists than the
exposers of the latter’s weaknesses. The reason is that almost to a
man the anti-Humanists were not seriously and fundamentally interested
in the questions put in the foreground by the Humanists. They saw no
dilemma, because in the absence of serious and passionate concern over
the main issues, there is no dilemma.

What is there new that the young chips from the old block of
rationalism can tell us? The limitations of nineteenth century
rationalism have been established for some time: years ago we cashed
all its cheques and its account has not been replenished. Likewise
the humanitarian’s credit since the Great War has been low. He may
tell us now that the problem of the will is unreal and the business
of improving the environment of man will gradually solve the problem
of human happiness, but we are not living in the hopeful spring of
1914. We do not see man any longer in a rosy light. It is the same
with the æsthete: he too is a pre-war creature. Representatives of
all these types were huddled together in the symposium, _The Critique
of Humanism_, and there carried on the old warfare of the allied
Romanticists and Naturalists against the Humanists. But that war has
been fought to a finish, and it is tiresome not to have some new
developments. Also it appears we have come down a long way from the
days of Shelley and Coleridge, of Darwin and Spencer, of Flaubert
and Zola who did indeed make Naturalism and Romanticism _something_
to argue about. We are down now to the intellectual nursery--to the
childish quarrelsomeness of a Burton Rascoe and the peevish complaints
of an Edmund Wilson (whose excursion, by the way, into the criticism of
Sophocles deserves a mirthful place in the memory of the _cognoscenti_).

We wish however to take the anti-Humanist attack at its high point,
not at its feeblest, and the acclamation of reviewers nominates the
piece by Lewis Mumford, _Toward an Organic Humanism_, as the best to be
had. The essay produces a glow in the reader, as Mumford almost always
succeeds in doing, but when the glow fades and the substance is coolly
inspected, the keen reader is bound to feel that he has been cheated
by the author’s cheerfulness. There is no originality of thought. The
usual contention that the American Humanists cut themselves off from
contemporary life. The usual contention that they are too negative.
The usual misinterpretation of “the inner check.” The air of an
enthusiastic master unveiling the secrets of “organic humanism.” And
the secrets? They appear rather banal, vague, inconclusive. Life is a
“multitude of functions”: we must live in a state of tension and grow
and renew ourselves: we must reconcile almost everything in sight.
“The world is so full of a number of things, I’m sure we should all be
as happy as kings.” At the end the essayist generously offers a big
bed wherein he assures us romanticist and classicist, Humanist and
mechanist, naturalist and idealist, divested of their philosophical
clothes, can lie down happily together. I am not convinced that we
can progress very far by the method of obliterating intellectual
distinctions which discriminating men have labored to erect. If this
piece by Mumford is the best anti-Humanist criticism, it looks as
though there was nothing to do but to set out from here to plough a
fresh furrow. Let me hope it will not be a lonely furrow as well.

It is, from my viewpoint, necessary to make an almost clean discard
of all the current adverse criticism of the New Humanists recently
committed by American writers. I would myself give away all of it to
the needy poor in intellectual circles for the few lines written years
ago by A. R. Orage on Paul Elmer More’s _The Drift of Romanticism_.
“Mr. More,” he said, “has also, it appears to me, the defect of
orthodoxy--he fails to realize the inevitability of heterodoxy and the
consequent necessity of paradoxy. The classic is for him, as it is for
me, the perfect literary form: all form, in fact, is classic. But the
romantic should be recognized, when it arises, as a challenge to form,
as an impulse demanding severer forms to contain it.” This, praise be
given, does take a step towards a new criticism of orthodox classicism.
It does not rest on mere dissatisfaction with the emotional side of
American Humanism, a dissatisfaction that is so widespread there must
be something to it, but it actually points out the defect. What is
more, it suggests a way to overcome the defect, and that not by denying
the distinctions involved but by incorporating them into a paradoxy.
It challenges the student to make an entirely new approach to an old
controversy.

Orage, it should be noted, has always been profoundly interested in
such questions as the American Humanists now raise anew: the quality
of modern society and the quality of the modern individual, the art
of life and a technique for practicing it, the nature of man and his
need for regeneration, moral responsibility and how to attain it, the
contraction of men into specialists and the way unto Renascence fulness
of experience. Without profound interest in questions of this nature,
there is no dilemma to be produced, and no need for a paradoxical
attitude to face it.

With that recognized, let us now press forward to grips with the New
Humanism.

 * * * * *

Twentieth century Humanism undeniably has an appeal for the liberated
man or woman. Of course, I do not mean liberated in any absolute
sense, but use the word in its present conversational meaning to
signify those who have freed themselves of popular delusions. I mean
just that civilized minority that do not need the offices of H. L.
Mencken to open their eyes to current cant. They are, to be brief,
total disbelievers in the so-called “American Credo.” It is not easy
to shock them about sex. They have learned to view many of the facts
of life realistically. They are of a questioning turn of mind. They
prefer sophistication to godliness. Generalizing, we can say they have
shed two classes of superstitions, the superstitions that have usurped
the place of ancient religion and the superstitions that flourish in
the neighborhood of modern science. They do not believe, that is,
that either the churches or the scientists “can fix everything up.”
Emancipated from the official Christianity of our day, disillusioned
about the day’s science, they experiment and drift in a middle region,
an amorphous group, curious about the possibilities of life but
skeptical of finding a pansophy.

In the last decade and a half in America they veered toward
naturalism and now know what awaits them in that quarter. Romantic
disillusionment--disheartened optimism--the modern distemper so
eloquently confessed for our generation by Joseph Wood Krutch. Now
apparently a vanguard is veering toward Humanism. And so far one can
distinguish two main types among this advance guard--those who have the
humanistic temper by nature and those who are naturally of a religious
inclination.

What the humanistic temper is this book has sought to make clear.
I will add now one further example and one more description of the
temper. The example is the poet Robert Frost who in all probability
holds no formal views on American Humanism, and perhaps is largely
unacquainted with the ideas of Babbitt and More, but who nevertheless
writes verse highly satisfactory by humanistic criteria. An intimate
understanding of this poet’s psychology _through_ his works will
give you better than reams of exposition what the actual flavor and
humor and clear-cut quality of humanistic experience is like. The
description of the humanistic temper is by Robert Shafer. Writing
in the _Hound and Horn_, Summer Issue, 1930, Shafer said: “The true
humanist has ever been a practical man of the world; avoiding those
tempting chimeras metaphysical speculation and pseudo-science;
suspicious of rigid abstract formulations; preferring to define his
position tentatively, by the methods of critical scrutiny, in close and
fruitful relation to actual but ever-changing circumstances; avoiding
the self-defeating violence of revolution; preferring to work with and
through existing institutions; sacrificing sudden impressiveness to
steady, quiet persistence; not expectant of victory; and content even
with apparent defeat in untoward times if apparent defeat is the reward
of moderation, sane judgment, disciplined taste, and a comely serenity.”

As for the liberated who are religiously inclined, who have, that is,
the religious temperament, they are quite easy to describe. I refer
simply to intelligent civilized men and women who are accessible to
ideas of a religious order, who are prepared to entertain but only
to entertain religious assumptions, who experience a little thrill
from the (to us) occult language of the Sacred Books. I mean no more
than that: it amounts to a kind of sympathetic curiosity in religious
matters.

Now, forsaking the liberated for a while, I have to consider something
very unusual in contemporary experience--_the religious spirit_.

The difficulties in the way of conveying a fresh sense of this spirit
are almost insuperable. I cannot begin as Norman Foerster and other
Humanists do by professing respect and admiration for the great
organized religious bodies in their present condition, for the thing I
attach value to is the religious spirit and that has departed. I agree
with Hulme and other scattered observers that the present age totally
misunderstands religion. Warm ashes of the Christian Faith remain, most
notably in Catholicism, but we moderns are almost hopelessly clogged
with false associations, sentimentalities, stock responses to religion,
and nothing very potent in us can be lit up by the feeble glow cast by
the best surviving elements in the Churches. So thick is the forest
of our associations about religion, so lost and wandering are we in
it, that I shall scrupulously avoid citing any historic examples of
the religious spirit: that would in all likelihood lead simply to
tangential arguments, to treading in the mazes of our associations
about figures. Whereas it is the essence itself, this spirit so
foreign to our ways, that I would suggest. Without a prior common
understanding, examples may in fact be pernicious.

Try then, my reader, as I have tried, to throw out of the mind all
our stale notions about the spirit of the religious man. Realize as
strongly as possible the nonreligious motives that have entered into
modern Christian thought, making the “religious life” what Whitehead
protests it should not be, a research after comfort. Put to a violent
death the democratic feelings that have been allowed to corrupt the
uncompromising character of religions at their source. To go on in the
hortatory way,--shock your preconceptions one by one. Be prepared to
admit that a genuinely religious man would seem monstrous to you.

As I conceive of the religious spirit, neither the religious
inclination nor the humanistic temper approximate to it in the least
degree. There is an absolute difference. They do not grow into the
religious spirit, but must suffer a transformation. My emphatic
judgment is that Humanism can never merge into religion by a mere
extension of itself, but that there exists a chasm between the two
which the New Humanist _qua_ Humanist can never leap. Religious and
humanistic inclinations can make terms with each other, but between the
religious spirit and the humanistic temper there is opposition forever.
I point to the _early_ Christians who insisted, as Babbitt notes, that
there is only a stark supernaturalism and a stark naturalism with no
intermediate territory for the Humanist.

Briefly now I will essay a probably futile task, the adumbration of the
man of religious spirit. Probably futile, because he is incredible to
us. We cannot credit such a passion for perfection as he incarnates.
To us religion is anthropomorphic: to him religion is theomorphic.
In that you have the astounding reversal of our suppositions which
he makes. Consequently when we read, “So God created man in his own
image, in the image of God created he him,” we at once think of God
as being like man: the religious spirit, however, thinks of man as
being like God, an utterly revolutionary difference, for, since man
in his ordinary condition is obviously anything but Godlike, this
theomorphism stimulates in the religious spirit the tremendous ambition
to become like God, a perfect being. Could there be anything more
rash and sublime and impossible? Provided one has shed sentimental
conceptions of deity. “Be ye perfect, even as your Father in Heaven is
perfect.” Every resource must be used, every power must be strained,
every second of life must be taken for the impossible task. The mind
is an instrument of perfection: it must accordingly be developed
to the utmost. The body must be strong to undertake the enormous
labors imposed on it. The emotions must be unbelievably enriched.
Consciousness itself must expand! Religion is indefeasibly the study
and pursuit of perfection: it requires not only that no stone but no
grain and no mountain be left unturned which might furnish a clue or
a stimulus to its quest. This, in its desperateness, its consuming
thirst, its impossibilism, is almost as remote, I need not say, from
Humanism as it is from Naturalism. To the religious spirit it is the
absolute or nothing.

Absolute Truth, absolute Beauty, absolute Goodness--these are what he
yearns for, and he is impatient of moderation, decency, good sense _as
ends_. He cannot, as the Humanist can, seek primarily for stability of
character: he seeks for perfection of character with stability coming
as a by-product. Nor is there any known limit to his skepticism: the
very nature of his pursuit of the unattainable enforces skepticism on
him to the last degree. The religious life is a continual destruction
of those elements of imperfection, illusions. “Prove all things; hold
fast that which is good.”

Obviously, such men as I speak of are extremely uncommon. Their
mark, I have tried to suggest, is that they make their life-task the
wholehearted, single-minded pursuit of a perfection whose fulness we
cannot even dream of, and to which we are quite indifferent as an idea.
They have in our era another mark that differentiates them from us as
completely as if they belonged to another race: an agony of bafflement.
In Biblical language, they have “a zeal of God, but not according to
knowledge.” Their zeal leads them everywhere, they stop at nothing, but
the form of their life in modern times is a searching, not a finding.
Zealous searchers, they are blocked by that “middle wall of partition”
spoken of in the Bible which they know not how to break down. They see
in their quest the need for an enlargement of their powers, but how to
increase them, that they do not know. Theirs is the agony of baffled
search for the means to the perfection they crave with all their being.
They would seem to know in terms of the most exasperating personal
experience that there does exist a radical discontinuity, as Hulme
thought, between man and God or the Absolute. The Humanist we have seen
dwelling on the discontinuity between man and nature. The religious
spirit discovers in his efforts toward self-perfecting, toward
expansion and intensification of consciousness, the discontinuity at
the other border, and it is this frontier between man and the absolute
that he emphasizes. Here, he says, is the all-important barrier, and we
cannot rest until we have leaped it.

Thirst for divinizing oneself, agony of frustration before the “middle
partition”--these are at all times the stigmata of a few men on our
planet. We can _recognize_ these stigmata even though what they signify
lies outside our experience, but because we have not experienced, we
cannot _comprehend_. Yet if one is interested in human limits, one has
to make a rude stab at description of this race of men. Have you never
heard that in the original doctrine of no single great religion is it
stated that man by nature has a soul? Then blame religious democracy.
Man in the great religions at their source is exhorted to labor for a
soul, is urged to acquire one, but religion says he begins without one.
Religion begins with stark naturalism, but ends with constructing a
bridge to a new life. Many are called to the work of creating the new
life--in that respect religion is democratic; but few are chosen, few
succeed in “crossing the stream”--an unpalatable saying to religious
democrats. This brings forward an idea that pales the humanistic
notion of a turning away from the natural self, just as the idea of
the divinization of man pales the idea of the humanization of man. The
religious spirit darkens naturalism far beyond the Humanist portrayal,
and at the same time it gives the possibilities of man an effulgence
that drowns with glory the modest conceptions of the Humanists. The
connecting idea brought forward is rebirth by a process as definite but
far more terrific than physical birth. This is an idea still present,
as we noted, in Pico della Mirandola’s _Oration_, but since vanished
from humanistic teaching.

The suffering of the religious spirit today, however, comes not, I
venture to say, from the pangs of rebirth but from non-satisfaction.
He cannot conceive a soul and therefore cannot gestate one. No wonder
a modern religious spirit occasionally shatters itself in madness!
The zeal of God, but not according to knowledge, is fraught with the
utmost danger, for the truth of our human situation we are told is
terror-inspiring, and a sudden glimpse--

We have done with the armchair mysticism prevalent in the West today.
It is devoid of the desperation, the readiness to leave the library
and the round of civilized comforts and take all the risks, the sheer
heroism that mark the genuine religious man. It clearly has naught of
the spirit of these Buddhistic verses.

  Scorched, frozen, and alone,
  In fearsome forest dwelling,
  Naked, no fire to warm,
  Bent on the quest is the Sage.

The distinction is not, please mark however, between comfortable
literary mysticism and self-torturing asceticism. Great religious
teachers have pointed out the fallacy of the latter as the Way. Our
distinction is between the unwillingness to experiment dangerously with
life and the resolute disposition of the genuinely religious to go to
any lengths, if necessary.

And now we may come back to ourselves, the liberated, who have been
illuminated by the steady light thrown on Naturalism by the New
Humanists and have afterwards turned to examine critically Humanism
itself. By the blazing light shafted on it from the religious spirit,
what do we now see there? Holding Humanism up against this searching
sun-like radiance, shall we find ourselves in an unexpected dilemma?

 * * * * *

It is patent that a man aflame with the religious spirit would,
when striking on the New Humanism in his investigations, formulate
his requirements of it and the aims he wished it to satisfy in a
language the contemporary Humanist does not speak. But we who call
ourselves the liberated employ, so it happens, a vocabulary keyed a
note above characteristic Humanist speech. The New Humanist’s favorite
words are: decency, moderation, measure, commonsense, the normal and
representative, the centralized, discipline, standards, humility,
self-control, vital restraint, proportionateness, decorum, stability.
His thoughts circulate around these words: they constitute the magnetic
portion of his vocabulary. The magnetic portion of the liberated
man’s vocabulary runs somewhat as follows, and you will note the
higher keying at once: honesty, daring, curiosity, experiment, zeal
for truth, self-knowledge, disinterestedness, harmony, the free mind,
spiritual freedom, integration of experience, art of life, increased
life, greater consciousness. The respective vocabularies are not
mutually exclusive; there is much overlapping in actual usage. But the
point is sound that the New Humanist is apt to employ the second set
of terms in the second place, whereas the liberated man approaching
the study of Humanism uses the Humanist’s favorite words as secondary
to the magnetic words of freedom, increased consciousness, etc. This
difference in emphasis gives us a clue that may conduct to an important
criticism of the New Humanist’s supreme method, the rule of mediation.

In approaching any subject, it is well to formulate in advance where
one stands at present, what motives impel one toward the new subject,
what one expects to find, and what one wants to discover. The liberated
man (common gender) has, in theory at least, been freed from dogma,
either naturalistic or theological. Dogma to him is any statement
offered which may be true but which is unaccompanied by a means of
verifying it. He has grown clever in distinguishing between what is
verifiable by himself and what is not: the latter he calls “dogma.” In
theory also he is freed from hypocrisy: he strives to be completely
honest with himself. His objection to most conventional modes of
thought is that they have hampered him in getting at the truth about
himself. Today he is discovering that truth, and it is, that he is
completely self-ignorant. Hear him confess his ignorance, as he is
doing in increasing numbers.

“I perceive my own ignorance on all essential matters. Why I exist I
do not know. What I am I do not know. I am ignorant of any purpose
in life. My friends and especially my enemies are more aware of my
weaknesses than I am. My economy of life is bad--I do not make the most
of it, or know how to do so. I am subject to unknown laws. Blind to
the future, forgetful of my past, in the present I exist, because of my
ignorance, as the plaything of chance.”

The discovery of the liberated man is that he is not a fallen Angel but
a feeble son of Man. On the basis of his ignorance, however, he can and
does formulate what he wishes to know and what abilities he wishes to
acquire. One word sums up the first:--Self-Knowledge. “I would like to
know what sort of creature I really am. What are my real wishes? Why do
I live and for what end? What are the conditions of happiness? How may
I live with real advantage to myself? What is the true nature of the
world that impinges on me? In short, I would like to know everything
about myself from my puzzling trivial habits to my relation with the
universe.”

As for Ability, he would, in my view, like to be a Reynard the Fox,
the “animal capable.” He wishes to be active, to become valuable and
therefore valued by men, to be devoid of humbug in the use of his skill
and single-minded in what he undertakes, above all to succeed wherever
he is. The power to do what he wishes to do!

But not one word about character. We have a confession of ignorance
followed by the recognition of a need for self-knowledge and the need
for general ability. Perhaps the intuition of the liberated person
tells him that character is a resultant, the product of his knowledge
and his ability interacting. With his needs uppermost in his mind, he
encounters the New Humanism and this system, as we have learned, puts
character foremost. The juxtaposition gives us another critical clue.
For the Humanist replies first to the very last questions--_the still
unformulated questions_--of the liberated, and the first questions of
the liberated are relegated in humanistic thought to a lesser position.
Which order is correct?

Are we close to the nub at last? Several years ago in an essay on
Irving Babbitt I closed by saying that the crux of the whole matter of
Humanism was in the questions: “To what ends in human character does
the technique of Humanism surely lead? Can it by its route attain to
the victory over circumstance announced by religion or can it only
ameliorate a determined scheme of things by the production of such
relatively minor virtues as moderation, decency and good sense?” These
questions seem to me now as then to touch the central issue. The
technique of Humanism is mediation: the law of measure, Nothing Too
Much, is supreme. The validity of this law I do not question. Whether
it is always and everywhere to be observed, that I question. What I
specifically challenge is the supremacy of the technique based on the
law of measure. Is mediation the prime law of life?

My answer must be, No.[9] I cannot set aside the fact that mediation
presupposes two things, the acquisition of self-knowledge and the
acquisition of will. _It is a technique of control._ By trying to
practice it some self-knowledge is picked up incidentally, and a call
is made upon the will to awake. But mediation is primarily concerned
with character, not with knowledge, not with development of will. A
prior method is absolutely required if mediation is to yield any great
results and that prior method is necessarily supreme. To be blunt and
homely, the New Humanist has overlooked the horse which is to pull his
cart: he has overlooked the working out of a trustworthy technique for
learning the exact truth about the individual creature who is to be
disciplined by mediation to a norm of humanity. For me then, and very
possibly for many other liberated men and women, the New Humanism is
too “advanced.” We stand at the beginning: we are pupils who have first
to learn how to attain a better working knowledge of what some day
we may hope to control. We are willing to admit the mediatory method
as one of a group of allied methods, but we cannot isolate it as the
leading and one essential means to self-development.

I have some hopes of making this point clear. It is not a destructive
criticism. I would not myself take the trouble to write a whole
book simply to overthrow something. It is, however, a criticism
of incompleteness and misplaced emphasis, and by no means implies
the discarding of the law of measure. The aim is to advance the
contribution of American Humanism nearer to a _total_ solution of the
modern problem. And I consider that an advance is made by pointing
out the absence in Humanism of a technique for self-knowledge and
will-development that at all compares in precision and plausibility
with its technique for control.

Suppose a man is today converted to the philosophy of Rousseau. He
finds his conversion need not be simply a literary one, so to speak,
for associated with the philosophy there is now a psychological
technique alleged to corroborate the rightness of Rousseauistic
ideas. The new convert can study psycho-analysis, undergo treatment
in an analyst’s office, emerge with a rather elaborate method for
interpreting his own psychology. He has, along with his philosophy, an
apparent means for verifying it, for living up to it. He is possessed
of a technique based on Rousseauistic principles which, it is claimed,
provides a continuous key to self-knowledge. It is hardly necessary for
me to add, however, that psycho-analysis is a discredited technique
for self-knowledge, and that there exists today no advertised, easily
accessible, tested, thoroughly accredited way to carry out the
Pythagorean command, Know Thyself. The illustration serves all the
same to point the fact that the New Humanism is unequipped with a
psychological technique for taking the first step. It has to rely on
introspection which notoriously is unreliable. But it relies too on
traditional knowledge, and this is to a certain degree a corrective
of introspective vagaries. For ancient wisdom--in India, in Egypt, in
Pythagorean Greece, in Palestine--may have been founded on realities
and methods to which we have lost the clue. There are good grounds for
surmising that Pythagoras, for example, was not a fanciful guesser at
all. The New Humanists being in contact with the best knowledge of
the past may therefore be right in their ideas, but we and they need
to remember that the complete technique for validating these ideas in
terms of concrete individual experience is still to find or perhaps
rediscover.

Another difficulty I have always had with the New Humanism arises over
the Will. In theory I concede the Humanists are right, but it is a
question whether in their practice the Will becomes an accomplished
fact. What is the sure test? We make effort--that we know; and
furthermore effort occurs in the prescribed field of working on
oneself. Will is psychological effort distinguished from physical
effort. But is all psychological effort to be called Willing? Remember
that psychological effort appears always to be powerfully motived: by
self-interest, fear, rivalry, desire for fame, intellectual curiosity,
a hundred varying motives. When the motive dies, the effort invariably
dies with it. Now just as I cannot grant that a man with an empty belly
who labors like a Trojan for his next meal is an example of Will, so
I cannot accept the man laboring great hours to satisfy intellectual
hunger as an instance of Willing. Will must occur in the absence of
incentive. Will would persist in working in a given direction _after_
the motive had ceased. Will is that which overcomes indifference.

I hold that we cannot be too skeptical on this topic. Nowhere else does
illusion insinuate itself so much as in regard to responsible choice
and Will. How can we make sure that we are not subtly deluded?

“Freedom is the will to be responsible for oneself”--this is one of
Nietzsche’s profoundest sayings. Certainly we all have a desire to
be responsible for ourselves. But it is equally certain that the
desire is comparatively weak, buried most of the time, intermittent
in appearance, quickly exhausted when it does appear. Clearly, we do
not get very far on our occasional desires for self-responsibility.
Our tendencies flow the other way: we delight in irresponsibility. All
the odds experience demonstrates are against any continuous effort at
being responsible for oneself. My conclusion is that Will is just that
continuous effort carried on against the whole weight of the organism,
an effort that is finally unaided by wishes and unprovoked by external
inducements--nothing less than an _independent_ activity of the psyche.

The practical conclusion I must draw from this is that men are
Will-less. The conclusion that I am not warranted in drawing in the
face of extraordinary past testimony is that men are devoid of the
potentiality of Will. “Thou art not free yet, thou seekest freedom
still”--this is Nietzsche again, and freedom must be sought in human
potentialities and won by the actualization of potentials, since as
we actually are, we are not free. The New Humanist has confused a
potentiality, the Will, with an actuality that can be described as
_conditioned_ psychological effort directed toward self-control. I
attach much weight to that criticism.

It seems to me of great consequence to realize this:--it is “failure
of will” we know, not acts of Will. The testimony of a therapeutist
strikingly confirms what I have just said. Therapeutists often find
that their patients want to get well, know what the trouble is and
what they must do, and yet fail to exert the required will. In an
unusually candid paper read some years ago before the Medical Section
of the British Psychological Society Dr. James Carruthers Young,
formerly a surgeon, now a practitioner of the Jung school of analytic
psychology, made the following remarks which succeed in putting the
problem of the Will on a practical basis. “Every form of occultism
aims at self-development through deepening, or expanding the limits
of, self-consciousness, or however you like to express it. Now it
is obvious that this laudable object cannot be attained merely by
embracing the ideas of a system, however rapturously. The all-important
factor of the exercise and proper application of will must enter, if
anything is to be achieved. If it does not, then the ideas, however
beautiful and intriguing, in the end become merely ‘dope’ ... it seemed
to me that ‘failure of will’ was _the bête noire_ in neuroses. Roughly,
the neurotic symptom from the Freudian standpoint is the disguised
expression of an affect which is too painful to be _faced_. From the
Adlerian standpoint, it is an ‘incomparable arrangement,’ by which
the patient avoids _facing_ a certain aspect of what we call reality.
In the broadest sense in both systems, it is a question of failure to
_face up_ to reality. Now it by no means follows that, when it is made
clear to the patient by analysis what aspects of reality he has been
unconsciously avoiding, that he will at once be able to cope with it.
This is particularly evident in the case of obsessionists, as I have
proved to my own satisfaction again and again. A washing obsessionist,
for example, cheerfully subscribes to the theory of origin of her
washing, but when called upon to make the slightest new adaptation,
always falls back on her washing. In effect she says: ‘I cannot marry,
or do this, that or the other, because, you see, I wash.’ To put
the matter in a nutshell, analytical knowledge is not necessarily
effective knowledge.

“Now I think it will be generally agreed that what I have called
‘failure of will’ is often bound up with an endocrine deficiency or
dyscrasia, sometimes acquired and sometimes apparently so fundamental
as to justify the term ‘primary plasmic insufficiency,’ which
corresponds to Adler’s organic inferiority. Unfortunately, the science
of endocrinology was not, and is not yet, so far advanced as to enable
us to remedy such dyscrasias with anything approaching to certainty
or precision, even granted that our powers of diagnosis may be able
to divine the true nature of such complicated conditions. My problem,
then, was how to overcome the difficulty of ‘failure of will’ by means
of a definite psychological method.”

It seems to me no less important for the Humanists to realize that
_there_ is the problem for the ordinary person as well as for the
pathological, namely, how to reinforce a sound theory of the Will
with a sound method for overcoming ‘failure of will.’ To vanquish the
obstinate difficulties, the theory must become practical.

 * * * * *

The dilemma turns out to be like this:--pursue your natural desires
and you find they do not lead to that signal of well-being we call
happiness but reveal instead the need for a re-education of desire,
so that you may train yourself to desire the kind of happiness that
is possible for men; choose Humanism as a system of re-education of
desire and you must wonder if it has the strength to perform the task.
One alternative deceives you by its bright appeal, the other seems
inferior in its call to the imagination, to the hidden latent powers of
psychology. But inferior to what? Inferior to religion at its purest.
Religion says it is a superhuman task to overcome one’s naturalism.
If it is a superhuman undertaking, then one may ask, what chance is
there of effecting a real mastery by anything less than a science for
divinizing man?

For here is man, the prey of innumerable illusions, stunted in
psychological growth, spiritually inert, bewildered by himself,
discordant in behavior, perhaps incurably paralyzed in will--will
anything but the most tremendous means metamorphose him into a falconer
of reality, an experiencer of bliss and ecstasy, a doer of deeds on
the fighting edge of the cosmos? The New Humanism is a morality not
untouched by dignified emotion. But I say that on the whole it is
morals not sufficiently touched with poetry, not endowed with the
tremendous shaking appeal to our whole being that religion has been
known to exercise. Religion is more than Arnold defined it, it includes
morality touched by emotion, but it includes art, science, philosophy
as well. It is insatiable for perfection and so must quicken and
recharge every side of our consciousness. It is, I think, properly
impatient of all half-measures everywhere, impatient of such a reason
as Babbitt gives, the fear of self-delusion, for choosing a more
moderate attack on life’s riddles, impatient of short aims--_because
it realizes the almost hopeless condition of man_. For psychological
freedom everything must be risked. That is what we learn when we study
the religious spirit. By its fiery light, we liberated ones perceive
that we are in truth only pseudo-liberated. We have put the weight
on being free from something, but the real question is, free for
what? I have been writing, quite knowingly, about the dilemma of the
_pseudo-liberated_. If we realize our pseudo-state, then as we approach
the New Humanism, our question becomes a searching one, will it truly
liberate? My suggestion has been to hold this system up against the
blazing background of the religious spirit, and against that background
it seems certain to me two major defects appear in Humanism: the lack
of a science of self-study and a not sufficiently stringent conception
of the Will.

Might we not say that twentieth century Humanism is a system of right
conjectures? “Do you not perceive,” the prophetess Diotima says to
Socrates in _The Banquet_, “that there is something between ignorance
and wisdom?” “What is that?” asked Socrates. “To have a right opinion
or conjecture. Observe, that this kind of opinion, for which no reason
can be rendered, cannot be called knowledge; for how can that be called
knowledge, which is without evidence or reason? Nor ignorance, on the
other hand; for how can that be called ignorance which arrives at the
persuasion of that which it really is? A right opinion is something
between understanding and ignorance.” Humanism to me has the value, and
it is a high value, of wise conjecture about the way to lead a human
life. For undoubtedly it is better, in default of wisdom, to be guided
by true opinions than by demonstrably false ones. But we must not lose
sight of the conjectural basis of Humanism. We must throw ourselves
into the task of validating to the point of indisputability the right
opinions involved. The New Humanist draws upon the Sages: his business
is to become a Sage, to achieve in his own person the sensation of
absolute knowledge, to go beyond the intermediate stage to wisdom.

Can he do so without hitching his wagon to a more remote star? I think
not. There is the effort to be superman, not to be solely identified of
course with the peculiar effort of one man, Nietzsche, and this effort
while failing to reach its end does leave as a by-product a growth in
human-ness. But the less ambitious effort to be human--does it hit the
mark? Superhumanism gives birth to the human, or humanism with the
small h. It is claimed that Humanism gives birth to the human, too, but
must we not qualify that somewhat in our final chapter?

 XII

 THE END OF THE TETHER

  _“It is time for man to mark out his goal, to plant the germ
  of his highest hopes. His soil is still rich enough for that
  purpose.”_--Friedrich Nietzsche.


It is not only in politics that the advice to use large scale maps is
excellent. If one refers daily “crises” to a big map, one is far less
likely to fly into political hysteria over trifling remote events: one
thinks politically in terms commensurate with the planet, not of one
corner of it distant only a few inches on the small map from the other
side of the world. But one requires also, and even more, a large scale
map of life as a preventive of petty thoughts. We need to refer such
questions as closed the preceding chapter to a map of life that will
enlarge our mental activities in trying to reply to them. The main
trouble with the cult of contemporaneity is that it has lost the sense
of greatness and spaciousness. Its map is a crude child’s drawing--a
large out-of-proportion area across which is scrawled, “Here Be the
Moderns,” and outlying this are indeterminate shadowy regions peopled
by lesser beings, dwarfs, and cavemen--our forerunners. History to the
one-sided enthusiasts for the contemporaneous is really Aunt Polly’s
Story of Mankind which I sometimes think Donald Ogden Stewart has
immortalized: it is a series of steps forward from ancient Egypt to the
great and glorious present, a progress culminating in Darwin, Freud,
Joyce and Proust, Ford and Lenin, Stravinsky and Bertrand Russell, and
a host of other recently elected candidates for the New Olympus. It
will not do to use so disproportioned and confined a map in measuring
the work of Babbitt and More who have plotted out their intellectual
campaign on a larger scale than we are accustomed to. We must relate
their work to very extensive perspectives: we must find some scale for
the greatness of the ages. How else can we measure the truly human
and the superhuman? For the superhuman covers all that man may be the
bridge to: the truly human covers the bridge, the whole transition
itself. The American Humanist makes the bridge the end. But can he
build it unless the struggle is to reach the other shore, to cross the
discontinuity, the gulf between Man and the Absolute? The superhumanist
treats the bridge as means for prospective travel. An unsuccessful
traveller, he yet may go further in completing the bridge than the
Humanist who thinks only of bridge-building.

The map of life I shall avail myself of was drawn by a genius at the
age of twenty-eight, a genius of whom the leading New Humanists do not
share my high opinion. But that discrepancy I choose to think arises
from the defect of orthodoxy. To Paul Elmer More, for example, _The
Birth of Tragedy_ by Friedrich Nietzsche merely makes a distinction in
the classical world that “takes the form of a harsh and unreal contrast
between the Dionysian principle of unrest and growth and creation for
which he expresses the highest regard and the Apollonian principle of
rest and renunciation and contraction for which, as Platonism, he has
the deepest aversion.” But for me _The Birth of Tragedy_ is a profound
and terrific document by which we may get the most vivid impression
of the deterioration of modern life, classify the New Humanists as
socratic or theoretical men, and learn what we must try to do with the
new situation in American culture created by the advent of Humanism.
The book had extravagances of judgment and a fatuous hopefulness
for German music which Nietzsche corrected in _Ecce Homo_ but the
central thesis he left unimpaired--and the modern world has left it
uncomprehended.

The interpretation of Greek tragedy has been a field of bitter
contention between romanticists and classicists. The romanticists
see chiefly in Greek tragedy the violence, the melodrama, great
horrors, headlong actions, titanic personages. The classicists see
ethical imports, a background of order, superb control, religious
values. From the standpoint of a student of Nietzsche both parties
are right--and wrong; for Nietzsche divined a third interpretation,
a paradox of understanding, whereby the romantic and the classicist
views are held in solution, but the solution itself is a new conception
of tragedy. Nietzsche saw in Greek tragedy the ever-renewed conflict
and reconciliation of two principles, the Dionysian to which alone
the romanticist is susceptible and the Apollonian which alone wins
the classicist. The tragic view perceives the necessity for the
mutual agonistic struggle: it is based on the realization that Apollo
and Dionysus must war with each other, must continue forever in
that strange paradox, a synthesis founded on struggle. There must
ever exist, if life is to ascend, a turbulent frenzied uprush of
Formability, and there must coexist a constant reduction to Form.

On the one side of the world-stage you have Dionysus with his wildest
music, his ecstasy and poetry, his drunkenness, his sheer states of
Being. On the other side there is Apollo, the soothsayer, the god of
plastic energies, the cool and comely, olympian in his dream-states.
The Formable and the Formed!

There is a suffering attached to actualization, because to actualize a
potentiality is to exclude other potentialities. It is to concentrate
and limit. Dionysus suffers because he must pass into limitations:
his great surges of impulse must eventuate in the actual phenomenal
world. He is the foe of limitation, and his enemy, Apollo, must impose
limits on him. As the Dionysian spirit is strong, it breaks limits and
requires new and wider ones; as the Apollonian spirit is strong, it
accepts the challenge and seeks ever better forms and containers.

The tragic view of Nietzsche and according to him of the Greeks too is
that this struggle must be consciously intensified. The will to surpass
must grapple with the will to maintenance. Liberty battles law; love,
duty; change, custom; intuition, science; and inspiration battles
art. But the antithesis goes deeper than these human aspects reveal,
ultimately reaching energy and matter, Dionysian pure energy and
Apollonian pure form. In the æsthetics of Attic tragedy Nietzsche held
that the chorus was Dionysian and the play an Apollonian projection of
the dithyrambic being-states of the chorus.

But an observer of contemporary civilization in America and Europe who
has had awaked in him a glimmering sense of the ancient Apollonians
and Dionysians would be forced to think that the two types of men are
almost extinct. How many _Moby Dicks_ have there been in the last fifty
years of American literature? Where are the post-Nietzscheans? On
account of the pseudo-Nietzscheans whose grossness and insincerity were
so glaring, no one now dares to call himself a Nietzschean, for we have
learned from the pseudo-Nietzscheans how transparent the pose is, how
fraudulent one is to play Nietzsche without the Dionysian flame in very
truth burning within. So there are no successors to Nietzsche, no line
of initiates of Dionysus. We can appreciate Nietzsche, but we cannot be
Nietzscheans. Our heritage comes from Greece, but not from the Greece
of the Mysteries: we are the heirs of Greek rationalism, of Greek
decadence. Euripides and Socrates have turned out to be the fathers of
the future.

For speaking by and large, our art is devoid of the Æschylean mystical
conception and models itself on Euripides who reasoned with the Gods,
not for them. His principle, says Nietzsche, was this: “To be beautiful
everything must be intelligible.” Accordingly he used the familiar for
his substance, he brought the spectator on the stage, he introduced
realism. “The mirror in which formerly only grand and bold traits
were represented now showed the painful fidelity that conscientiously
reproduces even the abortive outlines of nature.... The spectator
now actually saw and heard his double on the Euripidean stage, and
rejoiced that he could talk so well.” The Euripidean drama, like our
plays and novels today, concerned common matters of which every one
feels he is a competent judge. The audience therefore never comes to
be initiated but to apply without the slightest scruples its criteria
of ordinary experience. This was the betrayal of the Dionysian cause
which Euripides recanted at the end of his life, but the European world
has not recanted, today less than ever when a minuscule Euripides like
Bernard Shaw is loaded with the highest honors.

As for Socrates, although I am utterly unable to decide on the justice
of Nietzsche’s portrait of the historical figure--it is the most
devastating drawing of Socrates ever penned, but is it true?--that
makes no matter here. Nietzsche knew to the core the long line of men
descending from Socrates, and if he imagined a mythical father to fit
the men he knew, we can accept the myth for its truth of portraiture
of a type, if not of the individual it is named after. The Nietzschean
contention is that to Socrates was denied the pleasure of gazing into
the Dionysian abysses. He was therefore no more than Euripides _en
rapport_ with the Apollonian dream-world projected from Dionysian
ecstasy. He was a disintegrating force, withdrawing the Apollonian
tendency into the hard shell of logical schematism as Euripides shrunk
the Dionysian state into naturalistic emotion. He liked art to cling to
the trunk of dialectics, to be overgrown with philosophic thought. He
had the optimism of the dialectician which Nietzsche felt is fatal to
the tragic spirit. And he was most of the time anti-musical. From him
stream the theoretical men, the men of science in the broad sense, the
men who get their enjoyment and satisfaction out of the cast-off veil
rather than out of gazing with rapture at whatever still remains veiled
after the unveiling. They care more for the search after truth than
for truth itself. This Lessing, called by Nietzsche the most honest
of theoretical men, boldly admitted. Finally, they are insensitive
to mystery and attribute to knowledge and perception the power of a
universal panacea, seeing evil itself in error.

_The Birth of Tragedy_ gives us a sense of grand scale by which to
try contemporary literature and culture. It makes it ridiculous to
advance claims for Eugene O’Neill as a Dionysian--to mention an
example of the sort of correction that can immediately be made. It
gives us in Euripides the norm of our literary art, the decadent Greek
justification of the ways of man to God. It replaces Benda’s modest
analysis of the plastic and musical sensibilities, remarked upon in
an earlier chapter, with a majestic view of the highest contrasted
principles in man. Among other things, it throws a piercing light on
the twentieth century Humanists, revealing them to be theoretical or
socratic men as remote from the inspired by Apollo as their romantic
enemies are remote from the inspiration of Dionysus. We modern men do
but whisper inaudibly of the old gods.

In support of my classifying the New Humanists as socratic men, I
will bring forward two or three points. In the Dionysian-Apollonian
opposition, things were reconciled, brought to a dynamic equilibrium,
through struggle. The principle of individuation was in strife with
primordial unity. The most fertile sense of possibilities clashed with
the strictest sense of limitations. But the Humanist does not speak of
struggle as the means of achieving stability: he endorses mediation,
the tempering of liberty by law, the selection of a middle course.
This I hold is the procedure of the logical man with an undeveloped
sense of tragedy. Again, the New Humanist tries to rest on what may
be essentially a transitional phase of man. He concedes that the
highest level of life is religious, but he does not aim to reach it.
The humanistic level attracts him most, but may this attraction not
be translated into a love of the search for truth rather than for
truth itself--the mark of the socratic type? In the last place, it is
significant that several American Humanists confess to a distrust of
the art of music, _the_ Dionysian art. And unlike Socrates no inner
voice reminds them to their consternation, “Humanist, practice music!”
There does not even linger in them as in their monumental predecessor
an uneasy sense that in their philosophizing on the literary tradition
they may through lack of understanding the Dionysian be sinning against
a deity.

 * * * * *

But we have to work with the elements of the contemporary situation.
Just as it is idle to sit dreaming of great wealth when one has to work
for bread and butter, so it is idle to sit thinking about becoming
Dionysian or Apollonian when the immediate situation requires buckling
down to the production of humble intellectual and æsthetic fare. I
have totally failed if by shrinking our valuations against a grand
scale of values, I seemed to suggest taking the road to despair of the
modern situation. On the contrary, we want to know where we are at and
how best we may spend our energies on the sound assumption that there
is a zest and a satisfaction in making the most of what we have. Since
I have used Nietzsche to expand our ideas about the state of modern
culture, I shall also give his word of encouragement to us: “A constant
struggle against uniformly unfavorable conditions is what makes a type
stable and hard.” The joy of overcoming is ours to be had for work in
present-day terms on the present-day condition of things: it will be
our rustic fare which notoriously tastes better to those who sweat for
it than to those who live in cushioned ease.

We may use conceptions of Dionysians and Apollonians for measurement,
but the current actualities thus measured are socratic men (Humanists)
and rebellious men (Baconians, Rousseauists--of many sub-varieties, the
Baconians having a touch of socratism, the Rousseauists in their art
converging toward the Euripidean standard). The demigods and satyrs do
not frequent the modern stage, but there is still played across it a
pseudo-Apollonian--pseudo-Dionysian warfare. _Our disaster is that the
participants have lost the sense of being necessary to each other_;
this vitiates all our spiritual economy.

The New Humanists are seeking to vitalize in American life the
principle of concentration and stability: the New Rebels have as their
task the vitalizing of the principle of expansion and flux. The
changed situation brought about by the advent of American Humanism is
that we now have a strong well armed party of conservation to balance
and offset the reigning party of change. A true spiritual economy would
require that each party should intensify its efforts, should surpass
its previous productions, should grow toward greater goals by vigorous
contention with the other. The rôle of the Humanist is to _contain_
the age: the rôle of the rebel is to break out of the containing forms
and to demand new forms for the new enlarged more powerful spirit. But
actually what do we find? The total absence of spiritual economists of
the sort just mentioned. No unconscious sense of a need for passing
from inchoate restlessness into major forms and no need for breaking
out from these forms in turn. Or perhaps I should say scarcely any, for
on a few occasions in the last decade there have been laments that in
American life there was no _resistance_ offered to a school of ideas,
nothing on which a new school could sharpen its mind. Criticism with us
is mostly an affair of acclamation and denunciation, a false state of
excitement in which ideas stagnate.

In the light of the economy sketched above, our recent literary history
reads somewhat as follows. The revolt of the generation now in power,
successfully carried out between 1915 and 1920, was against a _decadent
conservatism_ well named “genteelness.” It savagely broke the forms
of genteel prudish culture, but the forms were weak: it may be said
that genteelness was no enemy at all for strong writers. Well, the now
ruling Middle Generation rested on its easy laurels: there followed
ten years of waxing fat and flabby for the insurgents, to be ended by
the surprising challenge of a virile conservatism that had quietly
grown up in the meantime. _How_ they met the challenge is a matter
of recent history--the jejune thought and rhinestone wit of Carl Van
Doren in the debate with Irving Babbitt at Carnegie Hall, the ignorant
identification of Humanism with the decadent stupid conservatism of
fifteen years ago by Theodore Dreiser, the putting to work again by H.
L. Mencken of his overworked phrases like “vague and witless blather”
left no doubt whatever of the middle-aged rebels’ lack of stuff to meet
the new issues. Most assuredly the containing forms of the New Humanism
can cope with this decadent revolt movement, and have plenty of vacant
space too. It is to the younger generation of literary thinkers that
one must turn in hope of a real resistance, and its performance
likewise was keenly disappointing--the repetition of stale attitudes
for the most part. There was a lack of curiosity, _a pronounced
desire to reject and not the hunger of youth to assimilate and pass
beyond if possible_, that was distinctly discouraging to observe. The
New Humanists may well call out for good enemies: if I judge them
correctly, they have no taste for hollow victories.

To illustrate in the particular field of literature what I have been
contending: the younger generation thought that one of the important
issues involved a defence of contemporary American writing. But the
real issue is how may things work together for the greater glory of
American literature. On that all should unite, and toward it each
should make contribution. The rest is a question of judgment of
contemporary production. There is a Hollywood anecdote that seems to
make clear the differences in judgment involved on this point. A new
motion picture had been given its first showing, and the magnate had
asked his three advisers for opinions. “Stupendous,” said the first.
“Colossal! Unexcelled! Unsurpassable!” the second replied. The third
said in a low tone: “Excuse me, sir, but to me that picture was merely
great.” The New Humanist appears to be that third man judging current
American letters: to him our writing is “merely great.”

The signs of the day should tell the younger literary generation to
beware of forming a new league of mediocrity to succeed the old.

The better path now for the spirit of revolt would seem to be _through_
Humanism. Revolt to be effective must be more than a mere spilling over
weak barriers. You must have worthy forms and trammels to give force to
your new endeavor. You must be contained before you can overflow with
any meaning of importance to the future. To state it another way, the
thorough assimilation of Humanism is the next step.

That there are gaps in Humanism I have pointed out. They are ways of
egress to those who cannot be content within the Humanist doctrine and
discipline. But as they imply in turn the assuming of a more severe
discipline and form, I do not fancy an enthusiastic greeting for them
from an opposition with the naïve faith in man of the present rejecters
of the New Humanism. There is no way out for the addicts of milk
for babes. “The feeble ask for liberty; the strong ask for rule.”
Nietzsche, thou shouldst be living at this hour, when “independence,
free development, and _laisser-aller_ are claimed the most vehemently
precisely by those for whom no restraint _would be too severe_.”

 * * * * *

In somewhat mythological language I have been saying that there are
certain possibilities of great development not inherent in the present
state of American culture. To come within reach of other possibilities
we must change to a state that holds them. Matthew Arnold noted that
“in the Greece of Pindar and Sophocles, in the England of Shakespeare,
the poet lived in a current of ideas in the highest degree animating
and nourishing to the creative power; society was, in the fullest
measure, permeated by fresh thought, intelligent and alive. And this
state of things,” he added, “is the true basis for the creative power’s
exercise, in this it finds its data, its materials, truly ready for
its hand; all the books and reading in the world are only valuable as
they are helps to this.” This happy state Arnold had to judge was among
the possibilities excluded by the condition of English culture in the
nineteenth century, and there seems no ground, alas, to believe that it
exists among the possibilities now open to American culture. But Arnold
did consider it possible to initiate an intermediate phase in English
life.

“Even,” he said, “when this (state of things described above) does
not actually exist, books and reading may enable a man to construct
a kind of semblance of it in his own mind, a world of knowledge and
intelligence in which he may live and work. This is by no means an
equivalent to the artist for the nationally diffused life and thought
of the epochs of Sophocles or Shakespeare, but besides that it may be
a means of preparation for such epochs, it does really constitute,
if many share in it, a quickening and sustaining atmosphere of great
value.” Arnold proceeded then to cite an example of this second and
possible state. “Such an atmosphere the many-sided learning and the
long and widely-combined critical effort of Germany formed for Goethe,
when he lived and worked. There was no national glow of life and
thought there as in the Athens of Pericles or the England of Elizabeth.
That was the poet’s weakness. But there was a sort of equivalent for
it in the complete culture and unfettered thinking of a large body of
Germans. That was his strength.”

It is plain that America has yet to reach that state, and that
furthermore a great deal of work by all minds will be necessary to
bring her there. No one would argue otherwise. But the sense of urgency
one would expect is not noticeably present. We do not seem to realize
that we must accelerate our pace of work. There is, so to speak,
plenty of slack on the tether of our culture, and we are not running
it out with any admirable rapidity. Else, so I think, we would be
welcoming the contribution of the Humanists with eagerness and not with
resentment.

As it is, we lag and flounder. We are, to take a common instance from
literature, prone to make late importations of European tendencies
which have already been fully worked out abroad. Consider the vogue
of the Dreiserian novel: it is written in America several decades
after the Zolaesque novel has been thoroughly studied in France. We
import the method of naturalistic fiction--late; we do not import
simultaneously the criticism of the method, but go blundering on as
if nothing had been previously learned from the experiment. The New
Humanists ask us not to be so tardy in a detail like this but to learn
more quickly of the past, that is, to _consciously_ create the field
of our present out of the best available past. In my opinion, the
Humanists can more quickly release us from a multitude of opinions now
holding us back than any other American school of thought current. They
should enable us to get to the end of our tether that much sooner.

It was clear some time before this point that I do not regard American
Humanism as a final resting place. So far as I champion the New
Humanists, it is because they can enormously further reaching the end
of our present phase. Let us put that end very high. Let us say it is a
state of culture as high as that ascribed by Arnold to the Germany of
Goethe, or perhaps better, say it is the equal of eighteenth century
culture in England, the century Arnold underestimated. Observe I do not
say identical with, but equal to, in quality. This achievement for me
represents the end of the rope. Because then we shall have thoroughly
digested all the ideas of the Western mind from the Greeks to Bertrand
Russell: we will be familiar with them all, be able brilliantly to
reshuffle them and deal out clever new hands for ourselves. But the
game will be always the same--always a preliminary game. For the
reason that there is not now current in the Western world _a single new
seminiferous principle for human culture_. There may be one accessible,
but if so, it is certainly not current. We know or shall know all the
existing ideas and there is not a comprehensive regenerating idea in
the ensemble. In our present phase we are limited to old ideas and
our understanding or misunderstanding of old ideas. We cannot expect
a revolutionary change--how can we?--when we have nothing to change
ourselves with.

Furthermore, we are not at all ripe for that inward transformation,
the only right way to an outward social transformation, the business
for us and for our age--according to Arnold. The sense of need for it
has not even been born. We have to reach by intense activity the end
of the situation in which we are cast before a new situation can begin
with its fresh group of possibilities. We have to need something before
we can acquire it. The birth of a new need--that alone will profoundly
alter the present drift and provide an unexpected future tense for
things.

I return to the metaphor of the end of the tether. Most people know
now and then the state that it expresses--a compound of well-being,
distress and the greatest alertness. Well-being, because one has
utilized every resource, put forth every ounce of effort, thought of
every possible scheme, done the very best one could to cope with the
circumstances. Distress, because the best one has is insufficient for
the emergency, because one is at one’s wits’ end, because one needs
more power within or help from without to forestall disaster. The
greatest alertness, because in one’s desperation one overlooks nothing,
one is willing to try anything, one racks oneself for a new plan. In
that state one is most realistic, most clear about means and ends,
most determined, most dissatisfied, straining at the leash of one’s
limitations.

The condition of being at the end of the tether, torn between despair
and the highest hopes, preternaturally awake to any means promising
deliverance, marks for me the highest point a Western culture can
now attain. It is both the one truly fertile state and a pure cry
for deliverance from iron restrictions. It goes without saying that
American culture is distant from it. Who will say we experience now
the need born of such a state for whole orders of ideas that appear
in a time of Renascence, when we have not exhausted our present
orders of thought? We are from all appearances so comfortable and
optimistic--shall I add slothful?--that even American Humanism is too
strong a potion at the present time. We have the illusion of being
free while we are in fact tethered at a comparatively short range. Our
progress must consist in finding out the length of our tether, and for
that Humanism is one of the most available means. The Great Revolt will
be the revolt against the limits of Humanism as new vistas of action,
thought and being become discernible beyond the end of the tether. But
just now our energies appear to be all engaged in the lesser warfare
between Naturalism and Humanism.

It is nevertheless certain that what has here been named the dilemma of
